C H A P T E R

The Medicare Advantage
program: Status report

Chapter summary

In this chapter

Each year, the Commission provides a status report on the Medicare
Advantage (MA) program, which gives Medicare beneficiaries the option
of receiving benefits from private plans rather than from traditional
fee-for-service (FFS) Medicare. The MA program is quite robust, with

growth in enrollment, considerable plan offerings, and a record-high
level of supplemental benefits. In 2025, 55 percent of eligible Medicare
beneficiaries enrolled in MA, up from 54 percent in 2024 and 37 percent
in 2018. In 2025, the MA program included 5,492 plan options offered by
164 organizations, enrolled about 34.9 million beneficiaries, and paid MA
plans an estimated $537 billion (not including payments for drug coverage
offered by MA plans). In 2026, on average, a Medicare beneficiary can
choose from 39 plans offered by an average of eight organizations.
The Commission is required by law to report in March of each year on
the status of the MA program, including a review of payment policies,
risk-adjustment methods, the impact of risk selection, mechanisms for
promoting quality, access to care, and other issues. We examine MA
enrollment trends, plan availability for the coming year, plan generosity
(including enhanced financial protections and supplemental benefits), and

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

343

Medicare spending. We also provide information about Medicare payments and
MA plan costs for enrollees with end-stage renal disease (ESRD).
The Commission strongly supports the inclusion of private plans in the
Medicare program. We hold the goal of meaningful and transparent
competition in MA to create incentives for plans to improve quality and
reduce costs for beneficiaries and taxpayers. Beneficiaries should be able to
choose among Medicare coverage options since some may prefer to avoid the
constraints of provider networks and utilization management by enrolling in
FFS Medicare, while others may prefer features of MA, like reduced premiums
and cost-sharing liability. MA plans are required by statute to offer an outof-pocket spending limit—which is not included in FFS Medicare—and plans
can reduce cost-sharing liability, offer integrated Part D benefits, and provide
supplemental benefits that generally are not available to beneficiaries in FFS
unless they purchase additional health insurance coverage or pay for the
services out of pocket. Medicare’s capitated payments to MA plans (which are
expected to average $16,242 per beneficiary per year in 2026, including rebate
payments) provide plans with incentives to reduce unnecessary services and
increase the use of preventive services.
In 2026, the Medicare program will pay MA plans, on average, $2,660
per beneficiary per year in rebate payments, which plans use to provide
supplemental benefits for their enrollees. These rebates have more than
doubled since 2018 and now account for a projected 15 percent of Medicare’s
total payments to MA plans. The benefits that rebates finance reduce outof-pocket costs for MA enrollees and have the potential to address health
challenges that many seniors face as they age (such as vision and hearing
issues) and for which there is limited coverage under FFS Medicare. On
average, plans project using about 26 percent of the rebate funds to reduce
cost sharing for MA enrollees, 38 percent for non-Medicare services, 19 percent
to enhance plans’ Part D benefit through lower premiums or reduced cost
sharing, 7 percent to reduce MA enrollees’ Part B premiums, and 10 percent
for administrative expenses and profit. New data from CMS show that in
2023, MA organizations spent roughly $24 billion on non-Medicare services;
however, available data do not provide information about the extent to which
beneficiaries use these benefits, making it difficult for policymakers to weigh
the cost of rebate payments against the unknown value of the added benefits.

344 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

When risk-based payment for private plans was first added to Medicare in 1985,
payments to private plans were set at 95 percent of FFS payments because it
was expected that plans would share savings from their efficiencies (relative to
FFS) with taxpayers. But in total, private plans have never been paid less than
FFS Medicare because of policies that increase payments to MA above FFS
payments and distort the nature of plan competition in MA. For example, MA
benchmarks are set above FFS spending in many markets in part to encourage
more uniform plan participation across the country. Payments under the
quality-bonus program further increase MA payments above FFS without
producing meaningful information on plan quality for Medicare beneficiaries or
the Medicare program.
The two largest factors responsible for higher payments to plans in recent
years are favorable selection and coding intensity. “Favorable selection”
into MA occurs when beneficiaries with risk scores (which are used to set
MA payments) that overpredict their spending tend to enroll in MA more
often than beneficiaries whose risk scores underpredict their spending.
Favorable selection is the extent to which, without any management or other
intervention from MA plans, the risk-standardized spending of MA enrollees
is lower than what Medicare would spend if they were in FFS. This selection
may stem from a variety of factors, including beneficiaries’ propensities to
use care for reasons unrelated to their health, differences in health status
that are not accounted for by risk scores, or plan benefit designs that are
less attractive to beneficiaries whose spending is underpredicted by their
risk scores, among other reasons. “Coding intensity” refers to the tendency
for more diagnosis codes to be recorded for MA enrollees, which causes risk
scores—and payments—for the same beneficiaries to be higher when they are
enrolled in MA than they would be in FFS Medicare. MA coding intensity is
driven by several factors, including MA plans’ documenting diagnoses more
comprehensively than providers in FFS Medicare (who may not report all
possible diagnosis codes), submitting fraudulent diagnostic data, and other
reasons. Separately identifying all of the factors causing favorable selection and
coding intensity is challenging and in many cases not possible, given available
data. However, regardless of the causes, favorable selection of enrollees into
MA and higher MA coding intensity lead to pricing errors that cause CMS’s
risk-adjustment system to set payment rates too high for MA enrollees, thereby
increasing Medicare’s payments to plans.

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

345

We estimate that Medicare will spend 14 percent—a projected $76 billion—
more for MA enrollees in 2026 than it would spend if those beneficiaries were
enrolled in FFS Medicare, an amount equal to 12 percent of total MA payments.
The higher payments that we estimate relative to FFS vary significantly
across MA parent organizations and are not an estimate of plan profits and
administrative expenses. These increased payments to MA plans contribute
to funding for the supplemental benefits discussed above, which beneficiaries
in our annual focus groups report are the primary reason that enrollees
choose MA. But the relatively higher payments to MA plans are financed by
the taxpayers and beneficiaries who fund the Medicare program. Higher MA
spending increases Part B premiums for all beneficiaries, including those in FFS
Medicare who do not enjoy subsidized supplemental benefits. The Commission
estimates that Part B premium payments will be about $11 billion higher in 2026
because of higher Medicare payments to MA plans (equivalent to roughly $175
per beneficiary per year, or $14.61 per beneficiary per month).
The Commission contends that important reforms are needed to improve
Medicare’s policies of paying and overseeing MA plans. First, reforms are
needed to reduce the overall level of Medicare payments to MA plans. As
noted above, relatively higher levels of payment stem largely from favorable
selection and coding intensity. Those effects vary across plans and distort
the nature of competition in the program. Past experience with reductions in
MA payments has demonstrated that plans can adjust their bidding behavior,
which lessens the effects on plan participation, supplemental benefits offered
to enrollees, and beneficiary enrollment. Second, reforms are needed to
the quality-bonus program because it is administratively burdensome, adds
significantly to program costs, and does not provide meaningful information
about quality of care to beneficiaries choosing among MA plans. Third,
reforms to the MA benchmark system would be valuable because current
benchmarks generate a number of inequities, including “cliff” effects due
to grouping counties into quartiles, caps on benchmarks, and benchmarks
that are skewed by the inclusion of FFS-spending data for beneficiaries
with only Part A coverage. Fourth, it is important for Medicare to carefully
monitor and respond to the challenges, burdens, and care disruptions that
beneficiaries experience when choosing among plans and navigating changes
to provider networks. Finally, the Commission finds that plan-submitted
data about beneficiaries’ health care encounters are incomplete, and we lack
information about the use of many MA supplemental benefits. Without these
data, policymakers cannot fully understand enrollees’ use of services, which

346 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

limits policymakers’ ability to oversee the program and assess the value that
enrollees get from supplemental benefits.
Over the past few years, the Commission has made several recommendations
to improve the program. These recommendations call for the Congress and
CMS to address coding intensity, replace the quality-bonus program, establish
more equitable benchmarks, and improve the completeness of encounter data.
In addition, because of Medicare’s fiscal situation, the growth in subsidization
of supplemental benefits should be considered with attention to their value.
Medicare payments to plans—In 2026, Medicare’s payments to MA plans are
projected to be $615 billion. We estimate that Medicare will spend 14 percent
more for MA enrollees than it would spend if those beneficiaries were enrolled
in FFS Medicare, which translates to a projected $76 billion. In this year’s
report, we incorporate the ESRD population in our estimates of payments
to MA plans relative to FFS, favorable selection, and coding intensity. Our
prior reports had not included the ESRD population because we had not yet
developed the analytic framework for constructing an estimate for that group.
The higher payments we estimate relative to FFS vary significantly across MA
parent organizations and are not an estimate of plan profits and administrative
expenses.
Last year, we projected that in 2025, Medicare would spend 20 percent more
on MA enrollees than on similar enrollees in FFS Medicare. The lower projected
payment difference for 2026 is primarily due to the completed phase-in of a
new risk model (called Version 28, or V28) designed to reduce differences in
coding and the availability of new risk-score data, which show slower growth in
MA risk scores in recent years (described further below). The Commission has
supported the change to the V28 risk model, and our lower estimated payment
difference indicates that V28 is having the intended effect of reducing the
impact of differences in MA and FFS diagnosis coding on payments. Altogether,
our estimates suggest that plans were able to maintain their aggregate level of
rebates ($222 per enrollee per month) in 2026 by adjusting their bids—and, in
fact, rebates reached a record high—despite the complete phase-in of the V28
risk model.
Favorable selection—We project that in 2026, favorable selection will increase
MA payments by roughly 11 percent above what the program would have paid
under FFS Medicare, or $57 billion of the $76 billion in higher total payments

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

347

to MA plans. The Commission’s estimates of favorable selection are reasonably
robust and in line with a growing body of research.
Risk adjustment and coding intensity—We estimate that MA risk scores in
2024 were about 14 percent higher than scores for similar FFS beneficiaries
due to higher coding intensity. For 2026, we project that MA risk scores will
be about 10 percent higher than scores for similar FFS beneficiaries. Declining
differences in MA and FFS coding intensity are driven by the completed
phase-in of the V28 risk model, which we estimate to reduce coding intensity
by 2.9 percentage points in each year of the model’s phase-in (2024 through
2026). By law, CMS reduces MA risk scores by a minimum amount to make
them more consistent with FFS coding; CMS has the authority to impose a
larger reduction than the minimum required by law but has never done so.
In 2026, the adjustment will reduce MA risk scores by the minimum amount,
5.9 percent. We project that, after the adjustment, MA risk scores will remain
about 4 percent higher than they would have been if MA enrollees were in
FFS Medicare. We estimate that those higher scores will result in $22 billion
of the $76 billion in higher total payments to MA plans. (We estimate that MA
spending would have been about $3 billion lower than FFS spending for similar
beneficiaries before accounting for the effects of favorable selection and
coding intensity.)
Although the V28 risk model reduces the variation in coding intensity across
MA plans, we continue to estimate meaningful heterogeneity in coding
intensity: 16 percent of MA enrollees were in plans that had coding intensity in
2024 that fell below CMS’s coding adjustment, and other plans coded far above
that amount, including eight MA organizations with average coding intensity
that was more than 20 percent higher than FFS levels in 2024. Eight of the 10
largest MA organizations had coding intensity that was at least 5 percentage
points higher than CMS’s coding adjustment. Higher coding intensity allows
some plans to offer more supplemental benefits—and attract more enrollees—
than other plans, thereby distorting both the nature of plan competition in MA
and plan incentives to improve quality and reduce costs.
Quality in MA—The MA quality-bonus program rewards contracts with a
high star rating by increasing their benchmark. In 2026, about $16 billion in
payments to MA plans will result from the program. In 2026, 64 percent of MA
enrollees are projected to be in a plan that receives a quality-bonus increase to
its benchmark. Enrollees generally report satisfaction with their coverage—a
trend that has held over time. For example, scores for all measures on the MA
Consumer Assessment of Healthcare Providers and Systems surveys, except

348 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

for the annual flu vaccine, were above 82 percent from 2018 to 2024. The
Commission continues to be concerned that the MA quality-bonus program
and star-rating system are not adequate to assess the quality of care that
MA enrollees can expect to receive nor to evaluate alternatives for assessing
beneficiary access to care, quality, and experience in MA.
Medicare payments and MA plan costs for enrollees with end-stage renal
disease—In the March 2021 report to the Congress, the Commission compared
MA plans’ medical costs with their revenues for individuals with ESRD,
described MA enrollment trends by coinsurance amounts, and estimated the
prices that plans paid for dialysis services in 2018. In this report, we update
those analyses using the most recent available data.
Medicare payments to MA plans grew faster, on average, than plans’ reported
medical costs for enrollees with ESRD, and were substantially higher relative
to costs in 2023 than in 2018. In 2018, MA plans roughly broke even on medical
costs for enrollees with ESRD, on average, implying that plans may have lost
money on ESRD patients once administrative costs were accounted for. By
2023, Medicare payments to plans were much higher relative to plans’ medical
costs for enrollees with ESRD, by 12 percent. Most MA enrollees with ESRD in
2023 (93 percent) were enrolled in contracts with higher ESRD revenues than
medical costs.
Most plans apply the maximum allowable amount of cost sharing—20
percent coinsurance—to dialysis services. In 2022, about 90 percent of
enrollees with ESRD in conventional plans and special-needs plans for
dually eligible beneficiaries were responsible for a 20 percent coinsurance
for dialysis. MA enrollees with ESRD are likely to receive some financial
protection from the maximum out-of-pocket limit ($9,250 in 2026) that MA
plans are required to offer.
MA plans pay, on average, more than FFS rates for dialysis services. In 2022, the
average MA plan price for dialysis was 22 percent higher than the FFS Medicare
rate, down from 28 percent higher in 2020. The high level of consolidation
in the dialysis industry—and the associated provider market power—could
help explain, at least in part, the difference between average MA and FFS
prices for dialysis. Since 2021, increasing MA enrollment by beneficiaries with
ESRD, combined with the removal of network-adequacy rules for outpatient
dialysis facilities, may have increased negotiating leverage for many MA plans,
accounting for the drop in MA prices for dialysis in 2021 and 2022. ■

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

E

ach year, the Commission provides a status
report on the Medicare Advantage (MA)
program, as required by law. The MA program
allows Medicare beneficiaries enrolled in both Part
A and Part B to receive benefits from private plans
rather than from the traditional fee-for-service (FFS)
program. MA plans typically have flexibility to use
alternative payment models, negotiate with individual
providers, use care-management techniques that fill
potential gaps in care delivery, and provide incentives
for beneficiaries to seek care from more efficient
providers. By contrast, traditional FFS Medicare has
lower administrative costs but has fewer incentives to
coordinate care and is limited in its ability to make care
delivery more efficient.1
The Commission strongly supports including private
plans in the Medicare program. Beneficiaries should
be able to choose among Medicare coverage options
since some may prefer to avoid the constraints of
provider networks and utilization management by
enrolling in FFS Medicare, while others may prefer
features of MA, such as reduced premiums and costsharing liability. MA plans are required by statute to
offer an out-of-pocket (OOP) spending limit that is
not included in FFS Medicare. MA plans can also offer
integrated Part D benefits, provide supplemental
benefits not covered by Part A or Part B of Medicare,
and reduce cost-sharing liability and premiums.
These benefits are particularly appealing for those
who are unable to afford a Medicare supplemental
insurance (Medigap) policy that would help cover
cost sharing in FFS Medicare.2 In exchange for these
benefits, MA plan enrollees accept provider networks
and utilization-management tools such as prior
authorization to ensure that a health care service is
medically necessary before it will be covered by the
plan. Because private plans and FFS Medicare have
structural aspects that appeal to different segments
of the Medicare population, the Commission supports
payment policies that do not unduly favor MA or FFS.
We hold the goal of meaningful and transparent
competition in MA to create incentives for plans to
improve quality and reduce costs for beneficiaries
and taxpayers. But the Commission is concerned that
the MA program, as currently structured, does not
serve beneficiaries or taxpayers as it should. Indeed,
current MA payment policies distort the goal of plans

competing to reduce health care costs and improve
quality; instead, these policies increase program
spending and Part B beneficiaries’ premiums. The
rapid growth of MA enrollment and spending elevates
the urgent need for reform. Over the past decade, the
Commission has developed four recommendations
(some that incorporate and update prior
recommendations) that would substantially improve
the MA program for both beneficiaries and taxpayers
(see text box, “Commission recommendations to
improve MA payment policies,” pp. 352–354).
In this chapter, we review types of MA plans and
Medicare’s mechanisms for paying them to provide
Medicare benefits to enrollees. We then provide
updated information on MA enrollment and plan
availability. We also discuss MA rebates, used by plans
to provide supplemental benefits to enrollees, which
are projected to reach record levels in 2026. Next, we
examine Medicare’s payments to MA plans, including
the impact of higher MA coding intensity and favorable
selection into MA on spending. Overall, we estimate
that Medicare’s payments to plans substantially exceed
what would have been spent on MA beneficiaries had
they instead been enrolled in FFS Medicare. We then
assess the structure of the MA market, including trends
in the concentration of enrollment and the integration
of MA organizations with other entities in the health
care sector. We also review MA’s flawed system for
quality reporting and measurement. Finally, we update
our 2021 analysis of Medicare payments and MA plan
costs for individuals with end-stage renal disease
(ESRD), who are increasingly enrolling in MA plans.

Background
Medicare Advantage (MA) plans include health
maintenance organization (HMO), preferred provider
organization (PPO), private fee-for-service (PFFS), and
medical savings account (MSA) plans.3 For payment
purposes, there are two categories of MA plans: local
plans and regional plans. Local plans may be any of
the available plan types and may serve one or more
counties. Medicare pays them based on their enrollees’
counties of residence. Regional plans, however, must be
PPOs and must serve an entire region as established by
CMS; there are 26 regions and each region comprises

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

351

Commission recommendations to improve Medicare Advantage
payment policies

F

or some enrollees, the supplemental benefits
that Medicare Advantage (MA) plans offer
fill gaps in the Medicare benefit by adding
coverage for services that are not included in
traditional Medicare.4 These benefits are particularly
appealing for those who are unable to afford a
Medigap policy that would cover cost sharing in
fee-for-service (FFS) Medicare. But the Commission
is concerned that many of Medicare’s payment
policies and policies to incentivize high-quality care
do not serve beneficiaries or taxpayers. Indeed, MA
payment policies distort the goal of plans competing
to reduce health care costs and improve quality;
instead, these policies increase program spending
and Part B beneficiaries’ premiums.
• CMS’s annual coding adjustment has for many
years been inadequate to address the level of MA
diagnostic coding that we have estimated. For
2026, we find that the V28 risk model has made
important improvements in addressing the overall
level of coding and in improving the fairness
of risk adjustment across MA organizations.
However, we estimate that after the coding
adjustment, higher MA coding intensity continues
to result in higher payments to MA plans and
inequity across MA organizations.
• The quality-bonus program boosts payments to
plans for 64 percent of MA enrollees but does not
effectively promote high-quality care.
• Benchmarks are set at a high level, and we
estimate that MA plan bids in 2026 are, on
average, roughly equal to the costs of covering
the Medicare benefit under FFS, when we
account for the effects of higher coding intensity
and favorable selection in MA.
• Plan-submitted data about beneficiaries’ health
care encounters are incomplete. If these data
were complete and accurate, they could be used
to identify MA plan efficiencies, improve quality

measurement, and provide more robust oversight
of the MA program.

Overall, we estimate in 2026 that Medicare is paying
MA plans 14 percent, or $76 billion, more than it
would spend if MA enrollees were covered under
FFS Medicare, which increases the financial burden
on taxpayers and beneficiaries who fund the MA
program. Paying MA plans more than FFS spending
for beneficiary care also creates inequities among
beneficiaries since beneficiaries in FFS Medicare
help finance the higher payments that MA plans
use to provide supplemental benefits for their
enrollees (benefits that FFS beneficiaries generally
must pay for out of pocket or through supplemental
insurance). We estimate that aggregate Part B
premiums will be about $11 billion (8 percent)
higher in 2026 because of payments above FFS
spending (equivalent to about $175 per beneficiary
per year, or $14.61 per beneficiary per month).5
Further, incentives for efficient delivery of care
are undermined by paying MA plans more than the
program pays for FFS beneficiaries.
The Commission continues to support including
private plans in the Medicare program so that
beneficiaries can choose among Medicare coverage
options. But the rapid growth of MA enrollment and
spending elevates the urgent need for reform. As
MA enrollment continues to grow, higher payments
to plans relative to FFS will worsen Medicare’s fiscal
sustainability. To encourage efficiency and promote
value for taxpayers and beneficiaries, an overhaul
of MA payment policies should include reducing
the level of Medicare payments to MA plans. Past
experience with reductions in MA payments
under the Affordable Care Act of 2010 (ACA) has
demonstrated that plans can adjust their bidding
behavior and lessen the effects on plan participation
and beneficiary enrollment. If payments to MA
plans were lowered, plans might reduce the
supplemental benefits they offer, but research has
found that plans have responded to past payment

in order to continue offering benefits to attract
enrollees (Cabral et al. 2018, Chernew et al. 2023,
Congressional Budget Office 2022, Song et al. 2013).
Over the past decade, the Commission has
developed four recommendations (some that

incorporate and update prior recommendations)
that would improve the MA program for both

TABLE

12–1

Commission’s recommendations to improve MA payment policies

Recommendation
Fully account for MA coding intensity—March 2016
The Congress should direct the Secretary to develop a risk-adjustment model that uses two years of FFS and MA
diagnostic data and does not include diagnoses from health risk assessments from either FFS or MA, and then
apply a coding adjustment that fully accounts for the remaining differences in coding between FFS Medicare and
MA plans.
Improve encounter-data accuracy and completeness—June 2019
The Congress should direct the Secretary to establish thresholds for the completeness and accuracy of MA
encounter data and rigorously evaluate MA organizations’ submitted data and provide robust feedback;
concurrently apply a payment withhold and provide refunds to MA organizations that meet thresholds; and
institute a mechanism for direct submission of provider claims to Medicare administrative contractors as a
voluntary option for all MA organizations that prefer this method starting in 2024, for MA organizations that fail to
meet thresholds, or for all MA organizations if program-wide thresholds are not achieved.
Replace the quality-bonus program—June 2020*
The Congress should replace the current MA quality-bonus program with a new MA value-incentive program that
scores a small set of population-based measures, evaluates quality at the local-market level, uses a peer-grouping
mechanism to account for differences in enrollees’ social risk factors, establishes a system for distributing rewards
with no “cliff” effects, and distributes plan-financed rewards and penalties at a local market level.
Establish more equitable benchmarks—June 2021**
The Congress should replace the current MA benchmark policy with a new MA benchmark policy that applies
a relatively equal blend of per capita local-area FFS spending with price-standardized per capita national FFS
spending; a rebate of at least 75 percent; a discount rate of at least 2 percent; and the Commission’s prior MA
benchmark recommendations—using geographic markets as payment areas, using the FFS population with both
Part A and Part B in benchmarks, and eliminating the current pre–Affordable Care Act cap on benchmarks.
Note:

MA (Medicare Advantage), FFS (fee-for-service).
* The June 2020 quality recommendation incorporates the Commission’s prior recommendations to eliminate the doubling of the
quality increases in specified counties (recommended in March 2016) and to establish a geographic basis for MA quality reporting that
reflects health care market areas (June 2005, March 2010, and March 2018).
** The June 2021 benchmark recommendation incorporates the Commission’s prior recommendations to eliminate the cap on
benchmark amounts implemented by the Affordable Care Act of 2010 (ACA) (recommended in March 2016), base benchmarks on FFS
spending data only for beneficiaries with both Part A and Part B (recommended in March 2017), and establish a geographic basis for
MA payments that reflects health care market areas (recommended in June 2005, March 2010, and March 2018).

Types of MA plans
Our analysis of the MA program uses the most recent
data available, and we report our results by plan type
as defined by CMS. CMS uses these plan types to
distinguish between ways that Medicare pays MA plans,
but there are important differences even within these
types that we discuss below. The analysis (including
the analysis of enrollment and payments to plans) does
not include non-MA private-plan options (e.g., 1876
cost plans, Program of All-Inclusive Care for the Elderly
plans) that may be available to some beneficiaries. The
primary MA plan types (covering more than 99 percent
of enrollees) are:

354 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

create incentives for them to make better decisions
about their use of discretionary care. Protecting
beneficiaries against high OOP spending would
modernize the FFS benefit and mitigate the need for
beneficiaries to purchase supplemental insurance
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2012a).
That recommendation also would create clearer
incentives for beneficiaries to make better decisions
about their use of care through adjustments and
refinements in cost sharing based on evidence
of service value while holding the aggregate
beneficiary cost-sharing liability about the same
as under current law. Finally, our recommendation
would add a charge on supplemental insurance
(such as Medigap) to recoup at least some of the
additional costs resulting from the higher service
use that supplemental insurance imposes on the
Medicare program while still providing beneficiaries
the choice to buy supplemental coverage if they
wish to do so. ■

•

HMOs and local PPOs—These plans have provider
networks and, if they choose, can use tools such as
selective contracting and utilization management
to coordinate and manage care and control
service use. They can choose individual counties
to serve and can vary their premiums and benefits
across counties. There are meaningful differences
between HMOs and PPOs. For example, HMOs
generally do not pay for care furnished by out-ofnetwork (OON) providers and often require that
enrollees select a named primary care provider
(PCP), who manages referrals to specialists. PPOs
provide more flexibility for enrollees by not
requiring a named PCP and by allowing enrollees
to see both in- and out-of-network specialists
without a referral. However, these plans generally
have both higher premiums than HMOs and higher

cost sharing for OON providers compared with
in-network providers. HMO point-of-service is a
subset of the HMO plan type that allows members
to seek OON care for certain types of services or
in certain cases (such as travel). HMO point-ofservice plans offer less flexibility to seek care out of
network than PPOs but more than standard HMOs.
•

Regional PPOs—These plans must offer a
uniform benefit package and premium across
CMS-designated regions made up of one or
more states. Regional PPOs have more flexible
provider-network requirements than local
PPOs. For instance, regional PPOs may meet
Medicare access requirements by arranging for
enrollees to obtain plan-covered services through
noncontracted providers at in-network costsharing levels for enrollees.

Two additional plan classifications cut across plan
types: special-needs plans (SNPs) and employer
group plans. SNPs offer benefit packages tailored
to specific populations (that is, beneficiaries who
are dually eligible for Medicare and Medicaid,
are institutionalized, or have certain chronic
conditions). Each SNP must be an HMO or PPO plan.
Employer group plans are available only to Medicare
beneficiaries who are members of employer or
union groups that contract with those plans. SNPs
are included in our plan data, with the exception of
plan-availability figures because these plans are not
available to all beneficiaries. Employer group plans do
not submit bids and are not available to all Medicare
beneficiaries, so they are not included in our analysis
of bids or plan availability.

How Medicare pays MA plans
In contrast to FFS Medicare’s fixed rates per service
paid to providers, Medicare pays MA plans a fixed
rate for each enrolled beneficiary, which is the
product of a base rate and a risk score. Risk scores
adjust a plan’s base rate to account for differences in
expected beneficiary medical costs by increasing a
plan’s payment rate for beneficiaries who are likely
to have higher medical expenses and decreasing a
plan’s payment rate for beneficiaries who are likely to
have lower medical expenses. The general purpose
of risk adjustment is to accurately predict costs on
average for large groups of beneficiaries and to reduce

incentives for plans either to avoid beneficiaries with
certain unprofitable attributes or to attract those with
profitable attributes.
A plan’s base rate is determined by the MA plan’s
bid and the benchmark for the county in which the
beneficiary resides. The bid is intended to represent
the dollar amount that the plan estimates will cover its
costs of providing the Part A and Part B benefit package
for a beneficiary of average spending. The benchmark
is the maximum amount of Medicare payment set
by law for an MA plan to provide Part A and Part B
benefits.6 (Medicare also pays many plans for providing
the Part D drug benefit, but those payments are
determined through the Part D bidding process and are
not discussed here.) Plans with higher quality ratings
are rewarded with a higher benchmark (although
the increase to the benchmark can be limited by the
Affordable Care Act of 2010 (ACA) benchmark caps).
For 2026, almost 100 percent of plans bid below their
benchmarks. For these plans, the base rate is the plan’s
bid plus a “rebate” equal to a share of the difference
between the plan’s bid and the benchmark (as low as 50
percent but typically either 65 percent or 70 percent,
depending on a plan’s quality ratings).7 The beneficiary
pays no additional premium to the plan for Part A and
Part B benefits (but continues to be responsible for
paying the Medicare Part B premium).
The rebate that plans receive must be used to provide
supplemental benefits to enrollees in the form of lower
cost sharing, lower premiums, or coverage of nonMedicare services (benefits not covered by Part A or
Part B). Plans also devote some of the rebate to their
administrative costs and profit. Plans can choose to
include additional supplemental benefits that are not
financed by the rebate in their benefit packages and
charge premiums to cover those additional benefits.8
(A more detailed description of the MA program’s
payment system can be found in our Payment Basics
series at https://www.medpac.gov/document-type/
payment-basic/.)
How Medicare calculates MA benchmarks

Under the statute, each county’s benchmark, excluding
quality bonuses, equals a certain share (ranging from 95
percent to 115 percent, subject to caps) of the projected
average per capita FFS Medicare spending for the

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

355

county’s beneficiaries.9 Each county’s benchmark is
determined by organizing the counties into quartiles
based on their FFS spending.10 Low-FFS-spending
counties have benchmarks higher than their county’s
FFS spending level to help attract plans and enable
enrollees to receive supplemental benefits, and highFFS-spending counties have benchmarks lower than
FFS spending with the goal of generating Medicare
savings because MA plans have greater opportunity
to produce spending efficiencies in these areas.
Counties are assigned to quartiles based on average
FFS spending; the highest-spending quartile of
counties has benchmarks set at 95 percent of local
FFS spending. The next-highest-spending quartile of
counties has benchmarks set at 100 percent of FFS
spending, followed by the third-highest quartile set at
107.5 percent of FFS spending. The lowest-spending
quartile has benchmarks set at 115 percent of local
FFS spending. U.S. territories are treated like counties
in this lowest-spending quartile. Counties that move
among quartiles from year to year receive a blended
quartile factor. For example, a county that moved
from the 100 percent quartile in 2025 to the 107.5
percent quartile in 2026 would have a blended rate of
103.75 percent in 2026.
By statute, plans that are awarded quality bonuses
have benchmarks that are 5 percentage points higher
than the standard county benchmarks (subject to
benchmark growth caps); in certain counties, plans
can receive a double bonus, and the benchmarks for
plans awarded quality bonuses are 10 percentage
points higher than the standard benchmarks.11
Unlike nearly all of Medicare’s FFS quality-incentive
programs, these quality bonuses are not budget
neutral but are instead financed by added program
dollars and beneficiary premiums. The Commission
has repeatedly recommended that the Congress
revise the MA quality-incentive program to be
budget neutral (that is, financed with a share of plan
payments) and focused on measures tied to clinical
outcomes and patient experience (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2020a, Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2004).
How Medicare calculates risk scores

Risk scores are beneficiary-level index values that
indicate the expected Medicare costs for an enrollee
relative to the national average FFS beneficiary. How

356 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

well Medicare’s payments to MA plans match their
enrollees’ costliness depends in large part on how well
the risk scores predict the expected costs for the plans’
enrollees.
CMS calculates risk scores with the CMS hierarchical
condition category (CMS–HCC) risk-adjustment model,
which uses demographic information (e.g., age, sex,
Medicaid eligibility, institutional status, and disability
status) and certain diagnoses grouped into HCCs to
calculate a risk score for each enrollee. HCCs are
medical conditions or groups of related conditions
with similar treatment costs. Some conditions have
more than one HCC, which differ by severity of the
condition and are arrayed in a hierarchy. For example,
the CMS–HCC model has four HCCs for chronic
kidney disease, listed here in order of highest severity:
Stage 5, Stage 4, Stage 3B, and Stage 3 (except Stage
3B). The “hierarchical” aspect of HCCs means that if a
beneficiary’s diagnoses map to more than one HCC in a
condition hierarchy, CMS applies only the HCC that has
the largest effect on the beneficiary’s risk score—the
highest-severity HCC applicable to that beneficiary.
CMS tracks beneficiary demographic information,
but MA plans submit diagnostic information to CMS
through encounter records, which contain basic
information about each Medicare-covered encounter
an enrollee has with a health care provider and each
Medicare-covered item provided to the enrollee.12
Diagnostic data collected from encounters in one year
are used to predict Medicare costs for the following
year. For example, diagnostic data collected during
2025 was used to calculate risk scores used to adjust
payments to plans in 2026.
CMS designed this risk-adjustment model to maximize
its ability to predict annual medical expenditures
for FFS Medicare beneficiaries while also ensuring
that the model’s diagnostic categories were clinically
meaningful and “sufficiently clinically specific to
minimize opportunities for gaming or discretionary
coding” (Pope et al. 2004). CMS has two requirements
aimed at ensuring the validity and reliability of the
diagnostic data used in an enrollee’s risk score:
Diagnoses must (1) appear on a claim from a hospital
inpatient stay, a hospital outpatient visit, or a faceto-face visit with a physician or other health care
professional; and (2) be supported by evidence in the

patient’s medical record.13 Diagnoses resulting from
telehealth services meet the face-to-face requirement
when the services are provided using interactive audio
and video telecommunication that enables real-time
communication with the beneficiary. To verify that
diagnoses are supported by evidence in the patient’s
medical record, CMS conducts risk-adjustment datavalidation (RADV) audits. RADV audits have been
limited so far, but the available results show significant
issues with medical-record support for risk-adjustment
diagnoses. For example, the Department of Health and
Human Services Office of Inspector General (OIG) has
conducted RADV-like audits of high-risk diagnoses
for at least 30 MA contracts and found that 70 percent
of all diagnosis codes audited were not supported
by medical records and that some diagnoses were
not supported over 90 percent of the time (Office of
Inspector General 2023). For example, OIG audited
diagnoses of acute stroke that were documented
on one physician encounter record but were not
documented on an inpatient or outpatient encounter
record. Such acute stroke diagnoses were found to be
in error 96 percent of the time, and OIG concluded
generally that a “history of stroke” diagnosis (which
does not map to an HCC) should have been used for
such cases (Office of Inspector General 2023). (See
text box, “Update on risk-adjustment data-validation
audits,” pp. 402–403.)
The CMS–HCC model is calibrated using FFS claims
data so that each beneficiary’s risk score reflects the
expected spending that would occur for a beneficiary
relative to the national average spending in FFS
Medicare. Relying on FFS data for risk scores, and for
other aspects of Medicare’s payments to MA plans,
generates two payment-accuracy issues. First, risk
scores do not predict spending for MA enrollees as
accurately as they do for FFS beneficiaries, on average.
Risk scores systematically overpredict spending for
MA enrollees, leading to higher average payments to
MA plans relative to what FFS spending would have
been for similar beneficiaries. This favorable selection
of enrollees into MA results from relying on FFS data
for risk scores and benchmarks. (See “Before coding
differences, favorable selection causes risk-based
payments to overpredict spending for MA enrollees,” p.
383). Second, diagnoses are not coded with the same
frequency in MA and FFS. Higher MA coding intensity
leads to higher average payments to MA plans and

advantages plans that code more intensively than other
plans. (See “Coding differences increase payments
to MA plans in 2026 by an estimated $22 billion and
continue to generate inequity across plans,” p. 386.)

Robust MA enrollment, plan availability,
and rebates
Continued growth in MA plan enrollment, considerable
plan availability, and substantial rebates indicate a
robust MA program. In each year since 2023, more
than half of eligible Medicare beneficiaries have
been enrolled in MA plans. For 2026, the average
beneficiary has access to 39 plans sponsored by
eight organizations. Rebates, which are used to fund
supplemental benefits, are projected to reach recordhigh levels in 2026.

In 2025, enrollment in MA plans grew by
4 percent; 55 percent of eligible Medicare
beneficiaries were enrolled in MA plans
Between July 2024 and July 2025, enrollment in MA
plans grew by 4 percent—or 1.3 million enrollees—to
34.9 million enrollees, while the total MA-eligible
population (beneficiaries with both Part A and Part B
coverage) grew by only 2 percent. Between 2024 and
2025, MA enrollment rose from 54 percent to 55 percent
of eligible Medicare beneficiaries (Figure 12-1, p. 358).
The share of eligible Medicare beneficiaries enrolled in
MA has more than doubled since 2010. MA has become
increasingly attractive to beneficiaries because plans
provide cost-sharing reductions, subsidized Part D
coverage, non-Medicare benefits, and a cap on outof-pocket (OOP) expenses at little or no additional
premium. (The magnitude of these additional benefits
stems in part from Medicare’s higher payments to MA
plans.) Most beneficiaries with care needs that are met
within plan networks and coverage policies will likely
have lower total financial liability (premiums and cost
sharing) compared with beneficiaries who stay in FFS
and purchase the most comprehensive supplemental
coverage (Ippolito et al. 2024). Some MA enrollees with
high care needs do experience greater cost liabilities
compared with beneficiaries in FFS (e.g., greater cost
sharing for in-network and out-of-network services
compared with Medigap premiums) (Keohane et al.
2015).

Among plan types, HMOs continued to enroll the most
beneficiaries (20 million) in 2025. Enrollment in HMOs
grew by 5 percent between 2024 and 2025, outpacing
growth of local PPOs (3 percent) (Table 12-2). Between
2024 and 2025, enrollment in HMOs grew by nearly
1 million, accounting for roughly two-thirds of the
overall increase in MA enrollment; enrollment in local
PPOs grew by just under half a million and accounted
for roughly one third of overall MA growth. For the
first time in several years, HMO enrollment growth has
outpaced that of local PPOs; the shift could reflect a
change in the types of plans offered by MA insurers in
2025.

down of Medicare–Medicaid plans (MMPs), which
are demonstration plans (operated outside of the MA
program) that CMS and certain states have been testing
as part of an effort to develop new models of care for
dually eligible beneficiaries. In 2022, CMS announced
that it would be ending the MMP demonstrations at
the end of 2025.14 Beginning in 2026, most remaining
MMPs will convert to operating as MA dual-eligible
SNPs (D–SNPs). At their peak, MMPs had between
400,000 and 450,000 enrollees; in July of 2025, roughly
240,000 enrollees remained in MMPs.

however, has grown dramatically in recent years.
C–SNP enrollment grew by 22 percent, 54 percent,
and 69 percent in 2023, 2024, and 2025, respectively.
This growth has been driven, in part, by a rise in the
number of C–SNPs offered by MA insurers—nearly
three times as many C–SNPs in 2025 as they offered in
2019 (Stein et al. 2025a). Several aspects of Medicare
policy may make C–SNPs attractive to MA insurers
and to Medicare beneficiaries (Polakowski et al. 2025).
For example, eligible MA enrollees are permitted to
enroll in a C–SNP at any point in the year (rather than
just during the annual open enrollment period), and
Medicare uses higher risk scores for new Medicare
enrollees enrolling in C–SNPs than it would use if the
same beneficiaries were to enroll in a conventional
MA plan or other type of SNP. Additionally, since
C–SNP enrollees must have a chronic condition to
enroll, plans may have an easier time identifying riskadjustment-eligible diagnoses for C–SNP members

than for enrollees in other types of MA plans. The
chronic-condition enrollment requirement might
also enable C–SNPs to offer easier access to non–
primarily health-related supplemental benefits (such
as food and produce, pest-control services, or housing
support). This feature could be particularly important
in 2026, given the end of the MA value-based
insurance design (VBID) demonstration at the end of
2025. The MA–VBID demonstration enabled plans to
make non–primarily health-related benefits widely
available to SNP enrollees because it allowed plans
to target the benefits on the basis of socioeconomic
status. Without the demonstration, plans will be able
to offer these benefits only as special supplemental
benefits for the chronically ill (SSBCI), meaning
that they must be targeted to enrollees who meet
CMS’s definition of being chronically ill. Since C–SNP
enrollees must have a chronic condition to enroll,
giving members access to these benefits in C–SNPs

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

359

could be easier than in other plans (see the section
on supplemental benefits for more information about
non–primarily health-related benefits, p. 368).
Researchers, as well as CMS, have noted an uptick in
the number of full-benefit dual-eligible beneficiaries
enrolled in C–SNPs (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid
Services 2025f, Stein et al. 2025a). For most C–SNPs,
the share of enrollees who are fully dually eligible is
generally low—averaging roughly 15 percent between
2011 and 2024. However, for a small but growing
number of C–SNPs, the share is significantly higher
(at or above 60 percent of plan enrollees) (Centers
for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2025f, Stein et al.
2025a). Between 2021 and 2025, the number of C–SNPs
for which dually eligible individuals comprised more
than 60 percent of enrollment rose from 16 (of 207) to
66 (of 385) (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services
2025f). Because C–SNPs are not required to meet the
same standards as D–SNPs with respect to how they
coordinate Medicare and Medicaid benefits, both CMS
and researchers have expressed concern that the rising
number of C–SNPs with high shares of dually eligible
enrollees may hamper efforts to integrate benefits
for these enrollees (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid
Services 2025f, Stein et al. 2025a, Stein et al. 2025b). Of
all full-benefit dual-eligible individuals newly enrolled
in C–SNPs in 2024, roughly 10 percent were previously
enrolled (in 2023) in a highly integrated D–SNP (HIDE–
SNP), fully integrated D–SNP (FIDE–SNP), or other
integrated plan (such as a Program for All-Inclusive
Care for the Elderly or MMP) (Stein et al. 2025b).
Looking across plan types, HMOs accounted for nearly
all (90 percent) SNP enrollment growth. Enrollment in
SNP HMOs grew by 12 percent, accounting for twothirds of total HMO growth. Although local PPO SNPs
have proliferated since 2018, rising from 4 percent
of SNP enrollment to 19 percent in 2025, most SNP
enrollees (81 percent) are enrolled in HMOs. Altogether,
in 2025, Medicare beneficiaries who were eligible to
enroll in SNPs were predominantly enrolled in HMOs,
while enrollment of beneficiaries who did not qualify
for a SNP was more evenly distributed between HMOs
and PPOs.
Enrollment patterns differ in urban and rural areas. In
urban areas, 57 percent of eligible beneficiaries were
enrolled in MA in 2025; in rural areas, 49 percent of

360 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

eligible beneficiaries were enrolled in MA.15 The growth
of MA enrollment in urban and rural areas was similar
(4 percent) between 2024 and 2025. The predominant
plan type often differs between urban and rural areas:
In 2025, 41 percent of rural MA enrollees were in HMO
plans, compared with 59 percent of urban enrollees.
By contrast, 57 percent of rural enrollees were in local
PPOs compared with 40 percent of urban enrollees.
Geographic variation in the growth of MA has
resulted in some areas having a very high share
of eligible Medicare beneficiaries enrolled in MA
and other areas having a relatively low share. In
some states (including Iowa, Kansas, Maryland,
Massachusetts, and Nebraska), less than 40 percent
of eligible Medicare beneficiaries are enrolled in MA;
however, a relatively small share of the MA-eligible
population (less than 10 percent) lives in such states.
In contrast, in 30 states (including California, Florida,
New York, Pennsylvania, and Texas) and Puerto
Rico, home to more than three-quarters of eligible
Medicare beneficiaries, more than half of the eligible
population was enrolled in an MA plan in 2025. In
10 of these 30 states (including Alabama, Florida,
Georgia, Louisiana, and Michigan), encompassing
roughly 20 percent of eligible beneficiaries, the share
enrolled in MA exceeded 60 percent. MA enrollment
is particularly high in some metropolitan areas (e.g.,
El Paso, TX; Grand Rapids, MI; Miami, FL; Pittsburgh,
PA; Rochester, NY) and in Puerto Rico, where more
than 75 percent of eligible Medicare beneficiaries are
enrolled in MA plans. MA benchmarks are computed
at the county level and are based on the area’s FFS
spending; in an increasing number of counties,
more than half of eligible Medicare beneficiaries are
enrolled in MA plans.

Availability of MA plans remains high in
2026
Every year, we assess plan availability and projected
enrollment for the coming year based on the bid data
that plans submit to CMS. We find that access to
MA plans remains high in 2026, with most Medicare
beneficiaries having access to many plans and multiple
insurers. Measures of availability slightly declined
relative to 2025, though these measures remained
high and stable. While almost all beneficiaries have
had access to some type of MA plan since 2006, local
HMOs and PPOs are widely available (Table 12-3). In

The availability of SNPs continues to be high across the
types of special-needs populations served (Table 123). In 2026, 98 percent of beneficiaries reside in areas
where SNPs serve beneficiaries who are dually eligible
for Medicare and Medicaid (up from 95 percent in
2025), 90 percent live where SNPs serve beneficiaries
with chronic conditions (up from 85 percent in 2025),

and 79 percent live where SNPs serve institutionalized
beneficiaries (down from 80 percent in 2025). Overall,
99 percent of beneficiaries reside in counties served
by at least one type of SNP (data not shown). Our
measure of SNP availability reflects only the share of
MA-eligible beneficiaries residing in a county served
by a SNP. However, individuals must meet additional
coverage criteria to be eligible to enroll in a SNP; for
example, to enroll in an institutional SNP, a beneficiary
would typically reside in a skilled nursing facility that
has a relationship with the plan.

361

In 2026, 98 percent of eligible Medicare beneficiaries
(down from 99 percent in 2025) have access to at least
one “zero-premium” plan with drug coverage—that is,
a conventional (open to all MA-eligible beneficiaries)
MA plan that includes Part D drug coverage and
charges no Part C or Part D premium (enrollees may
pay the Medicare Part B premium) (Table 12-3, p. 361).17
About 76 percent of conventional MA enrollment is
projected to be in these zero-premium plans (data
not shown). Also in 2026, 99 percent of beneficiaries
(unchanged from 2025) have access to at least one plan
that offers some reduction in the Part B premium (data
not shown); about 29 percent of 2026 conventional
MA enrollment is projected to be in these premiumreduction plans (a decline from 32 percent in 2025);
and the median monthly premium reduction is $61
for enrollees in one of these plans (data not shown).
As plans have increasingly offered Part B–premium
reductions, MA enrollees may become increasingly
attracted to the direct assistance these benefits
provide.
In most counties, beneficiaries have access to many MA
plans. While nearly all Medicare beneficiaries have had
an MA plan available to them since 2006, the number
of plans offered per insurer has increased substantially
since 2019, coinciding with CMS’s regulatory repeal of
its “meaningful differences” requirement.18 In 2018, the
average number of plans available per county was 10
(equivalent to an average of 20 plans per beneficiary).
In 2026, the average number of plans available in a
county is 26 (down from 28 plans in 2025) (Table 12-3,
p. 361). About 4 percent of counties in 2026 do not have
an available conventional MA plan (up from 3 percent
in 2025; data not shown).19 Plan availability can also be
evaluated by the number of plan choices available to
the average beneficiary. According to that calculation,
the average beneficiary in 2026 has 39 available plans,
down from 42 plans in 2025. An additional measure
of plan access is the number of insurers offering
products to the average beneficiary. In 2026, the
average beneficiary can choose from plans sponsored
by eight organizations (unchanged from 2025); 96
percent of beneficiaries have access to MA plans
sponsored by at least three organizations, 92 percent
of beneficiaries can choose from plans sponsored by at
least four organizations, and 86 percent of beneficiaries
can choose from plans sponsored by at least five
organizations (data not shown).20

362 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

Beneficiaries in our focus groups consistently report
being confused about their coverage options and
overwhelmed regarding the number of choices they
face (NORC at the University of Chicago 2025). Across
sectors, studies of marketing and consumer behavior
have consistently indicated that “choice overload” can
both be distressing and lead to suboptimal decisions,
and the potential negative consequences of “too
much choice” are amplified in sectors with a high
degree of complexity, such as health care (Dar and Gul
2025, Frank and Lamiraud 2009, Misuraca et al. 2024,
Reutskaja et al. 2020, Schlesinger et al. 2014, Taylor
et al. 2016). One published literature review found
that as the number of health plan choices increases,
individuals are less likely to correctly identify the
lowest-cost plan, less likely to review all of their
coverage options, and more likely to select a plan that
is clearly inferior to another available plan (Taylor et al.
2016). Included studies also found that individuals had
difficulties even when the increase in the number of
choices was relatively small, for example, from around
5 choices to around 10, and that many people have
difficulty understanding concepts such as coinsurance
and deductibles, tend to put too much emphasis on
premiums over cost sharing when picking plans, and
are susceptible to how plan choices are presented.
Another study compared growth in MA enrollment
with growth in the number of MA plans and found that
enrollment grew faster in areas where the increase in
the number of plans was relatively small (fewer than 15
plans), suggesting that beneficiaries were more likely
to enroll when they had a more manageable number
of choices (McWilliams et al. 2011). The Commission
has expressed concern that, given the large number
of plan choices, beneficiaries may find it difficult to
discern differences in plan benefit packages in order to
make an optimal choice (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2023b).

MA rebates, which plans use to provide
supplemental benefits to enrollees, are
projected to reach record levels in 2026
MA plans that bid below their benchmark are paid a
“rebate” equal to a share of the difference between the
plan’s bid and the benchmark (as low as 50 percent but
typically either 65 percent or 70 percent, depending
on a plan’s quality ratings). Plans are required to use
rebates to provide supplemental benefits. These
benefits have the potential to address health challenges

that many seniors face as they age (such as vision and

under FFS Medicare. Surveys of MA enrollees, as
well as MedPAC’s beneficiary focus groups, generally
show that enrollees appreciate having access to the
supplemental benefits that plans use rebates to offer
(Commonwealth Fund 2025, NORC at the University of
Chicago 2025). For 2026, almost 100 percent of plans
bid below their benchmarks and therefore receive
rebates.21 Average MA rebates are expected to reach an
all-time high in 2026. Across all non–employer group
plans, the average rebate is expected to be $222 per

member per month (PMPM), up from $209 in 2025
(dollar values not adjusted for inflation).22 The average
rebate for conventional plans (i.e., non-employer group,
non-special-needs plans) in 2026 will be about $199
PMPM, up from $188 in 2025; for special-needs plans,
the average rebate will be about $275, up from $271 in
2025.23
MA rebates have grown dramatically over the last
decade (Figure 12-2). The average rebate paid to
conventional MA plans in 2026 will be nearly two and
a half times the amount paid to MA plans in 2016 (not

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

363

accounting for inflation). (Accounting for inflation,
rebates in 2025 for conventional plans were about 1.7
times their 2016 level, and those for SNPs were about
2.5 times their 2016 level.) The increase is due primarily
to rapid growth in rebates between 2018 and 2023;
average rebates for conventional plans rose from $95
PMPM in 2018 to $197 in 2023; they then declined
slightly to $188 PMPM in 2025 before rising again to
$199 in 2026 (Figure 12-2, p. 363). For SNPs, the average
rebate is significantly higher—$275 PMPM in 2026—and
has increased in every year since 2016.
MA plans are required to use the rebate to provide
supplemental benefits for their enrollees. Supplemental
benefits can be organized into four broad categories:
reduced cost sharing for Part A and Part B services,
reduced Part B premiums, enhanced Part D benefits
(including reduced Part D premiums), and coverage
of non-Medicare services (services not covered under
FFS Medicare, such as dental, vision, and hearing
services).24 Plans have flexibility regarding which
supplemental benefits they offer and the generosity
of the coverage. Plans also use some of the rebate to
cover their administrative costs and profit.25 In the
bids they submit to CMS, MA plans must report how
much of their rebate they plan to allocate to each of
the four broad categories of supplemental benefits.
Plans’ rebate allocations are a projection of how the
plan anticipates rebate dollars will be used but might
not reflect actual use. In 2026, the Medicare program
will pay conventional plans approximately $2,391 per
beneficiary per year for supplemental benefits; plans
report that they expect to use $199 of those funds
(about 8 percent) for plan administrative expenses
and profit. Medicare will pay SNPs about $3,306 per
beneficiary per year for supplemental benefits, and
plans expect to use about $417 (about 13 percent) for
plan administrative expenses and profit.
In 2026, as in most previous years, conventional MA
plans project allocating the largest share of their rebate
dollars ($81 PMPM) to reducing cost sharing for Part
A and Part B services (Table 12-4). That allocation
represents a similar share of the total rebate as in 2025
(40 percent in 2026 vs. 41 percent in 2025) (Table 124).26 The lower cost sharing that plans use rebates to
offer can provide MA enrollees with additional financial
protection. For conventional plans, the projected
rebates allocated to cost-sharing reductions include

364 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

coverage for the beneficiary maximum out-of-pocket
(MOOP) cap on plan-approved Part A and Part B
expenses. In 2026, plans project that, on average, their
liability for the MOOP will be $19 PMPM—equivalent
to 8 percent of rebates (unchanged from 2025; data
not shown) and 1 percent of projected plan payments
(unchanged from 2025; data not shown).27,28 Because
cost-sharing reductions lower a beneficiary’s OOP
expenses, rebate dollars that are used to lower
beneficiary cost sharing also lower a plan’s MOOP
liability. All cost-sharing reductions, including the
MOOP, are subject to plan coverage policies. In certain
circumstances, MA enrollees can be granted special
enrollment periods that permit them to change plans
midyear.29 For beneficiaries who switch MA insurers
midyear, their OOP spending contribution toward their
MOOP limit is reset when they switch plans such that
OOP expenses accumulated under their initial plan are
not counted toward reaching the MOOP limit under
the second plan. For beneficiaries who switch midyear
between plan types offered by the same insurer, the
insurer has discretion regarding whether beneficiary’s
OOP spending contribution toward the MOOP limit is
reset. MOOP limits are not reset for beneficiaries who
switch plans midyear but remain enrolled with the
same insurer and in the same plan type.
Table 12-4 shows how plans expect to use rebate
dollars, including any funds the plan expects to use for
administrative costs and profit. In addition to the 40
percent they anticipate using to reduce cost sharing,
conventional plans expect to use 25 percent for nonMedicare benefits like dental, vision, and hearing; 26
percent for Part D enhancements like lower premiums
or cost sharing; and 9 percent to reduce the Part B
premium for their enrollees. Accounting separately
for plans’ administrative costs and profits (data not
shown), that amount equates to using about 35 percent
to reduce cost sharing, 22 percent for non-Medicare
services, 26 percent for Part D benefits, about 9
percent for Part B premiums, and about 8 percent for
administrative expenses and profit.
Because most SNP enrollees are dually eligible for
Medicare and Medicaid, many of their OOP costs are
often covered by other programs: Medicaid generally
covers Part A and Part B cost sharing and pays the
Part B premium in most cases, and Medicare’s Part
D low-income subsidy typically covers the premium

administrative expenses and profit). Accounting

The increase in the average rebate per member,
combined with rapid MA enrollment growth, has
resulted in a substantial increase in the amount
Medicare spends on rebates. We estimate that in 2025,
Medicare paid MA plans approximately $84 billion in

rebates (accounting for 17 percent of non-ESRD plan
payments).30 Conceptually, the rebates paid to MA
plans were originally intended to be a form of shared
savings in which plans would be rewarded for providing
Medicare benefits at a lower cost than would have been
the case under FFS.31 However, the Commission has
shown that a large portion of Medicare’s spending on
MA rebates is financed by additional program spending
rather than by savings derived from plan efficiencies
(see “Higher payments to MA plans stem from favorable
selection and coding intensity”). Because MA rebates
are paid from the Medicare trust funds, they are
partially financed by Part B premiums collected from
all beneficiaries (including those in FFS Medicare).
The Commission estimates that Part B premiums will
finance about $13 billion of MA rebates in 2025, with
nearly $6 billion coming from FFS beneficiaries who do
not have access to the supplemental benefits financed
by the rebates.32
MA supplemental benefits

As shown in Figure 12-3, MA plans—particularly SNPs—
allocate a large fraction of rebate dollars to providing
non-Medicare services as supplemental benefits.
Coverage for vision, hearing, and dental services are
some of the most common of these benefits: In 2025,
nearly all MA enrollees (in both non–SNPs and SNPs)
were in plans that offered some coverage of dental,
vision, or hearing services (Ochieng et al. 2025). MA
plans may also offer nonmedical supplemental benefits
such as nonemergency transportation services,
assistance paying for over-the-counter items, meals,
and gym memberships.33 The share of plans offering
these types of benefits—and the share of enrollees in
such plans—has risen as MA rebates have increased.
These benefits have the potential to address health
challenges that many seniors face as they age and for
which there is limited coverage under FFS Medicare.
Relatively little is known about the extent to which MA
enrollees use the many supplemental benefits available
to them. The Commission has previously examined the
data that Medicare collected before 2024 about MA
enrollees’ use of services and, for most supplemental
benefits, found that the data were insufficient for
examining utilization (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2025a). Most available evidence about MA
enrollees’ use of supplemental benefits comes from
broad surveys or from analyses conducted by MA plans.

A 2024 survey by the Commonwealth Fund found that 69
percent of MA enrollees used at least one supplemental
benefit, but the survey did not shed light on which
enrollees used which benefits (Commonwealth Fund
2024). The Commission’s review of surveys and other
studies found that the available evidence provides little
insight into MA enrollees’ use of supplemental benefits
and leaves basic questions unanswered (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2025a).
Starting in 2024, CMS began implementing a series
of actions to improve and increase the amount
of data that plans report regarding supplemental
benefits. Among these is a requirement that plans
report their spending for a number of commonly
offered supplemental benefits as part of the data used
to monitor and enforce medical-loss-ratio (MLR)
requirements. The data provide new insights about
how much plans spend on supplemental benefits and
the distribution of spending across benefits (see text
box on MA MLR data, pp. 368–372, for more detail).
In addition to the new MLR requirements, CMS also
began, in 2024, collecting additional information about
MA enrollees’ use of supplemental benefits, including
encounter records for dental services and other
supplemental benefits and plan-level use and spending
data as part of the Part C reporting requirements
(Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2024b,
Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2024c).
Those data were not available at the time of our
analysis, but we anticipate the data for 2024 will be
available for analysis in 2026.

Higher payments to MA plans stem
from favorable selection and coding
intensity
The Commission estimates that MA payments (which
combine base payments to cover Medicare benefits
and the rebates that finance supplemental benefits)
substantially exceed what would have been spent on
MA beneficiaries had they instead been enrolled in
FFS Medicare. Before applying any adjustments for
favorable selection or coding intensity, we estimate
that Medicare payments to MA plans have generally
been similar to historical spending for FFS beneficiaries
with both Part A and Part B coverage. But in aggregate,
the effects of favorable selection (before any coding

MA medical-loss-ratio data show MA organizations spent roughly $24 billion on
non-Medicare services offered as supplemental benefits in 2023

M

edicare Advantage (MA) plans that bid
below their spending benchmark receive a
“rebate” from Medicare that they must use
to provide supplemental benefits for their enrollees.
These benefits can include reduced cost sharing for
Part A and Part B services, reduced Part B and Part D
premiums, enhanced Part D benefits, and coverage
of non-Medicare services (services not covered
under fee-for-service (FFS) Medicare, such as dental,
vision, and hearing). Historically, there has been
little information about how plans use the rebates
they receive from Medicare and how much they
spend on various types of supplemental benefits.
The new data show that, across the non-Medicare
services offered as supplemental benefits, MA plans
spent the most on dental benefits. Non–primarily
health-related benefits accounted for the secondmost spending; these benefits are more commonly
offered by special-needs plans (SNPs) than by
conventional plans, and the data suggest that a large
share of the spending was associated with benefits
offered under the MA value-based insurance design
(MA–VBID) demonstration, which concluded at the
end of 2025. Despite the new insights enabled by
these data, there are limitations to the questions
they can be used to answer. Data on MA enrollees’
use of supplemental benefits is not yet available and
will be important for assessing whether Medicare’s
spending for supplemental benefits produces good
value for Medicare beneficiaries and the taxpayers
who fund the program.

are commonly offered as supplemental benefits, as
well as their expenditures for special supplemental
benefits for the chronically ill (SSBCI) and “all other
primarily health-related supplemental benefits” (a
total of 18 reporting categories). The MLR data are
collected at the end of the calendar year following
the year in which benefits are provided (e.g., data
pertaining to plans’ spending in 2023 were collected
at the end of 2024). In contrast to bid data, which
include plans’ projections of expected spending,
MLR data reflect MAOs’ actual spending during
a year. The new data represent the first time
that information about plans’ actual spending for
supplemental benefits has been available.

MA medical-loss-ratio data provide new,
but limited, information about plans’
spending on supplemental benefits

One potential use of the MLR data could be to
assess how spending by MAOs compares with the
rebates they received from Medicare. However,
several factors make it difficult to use the data for
this purpose. One barrier is that the data do not
disaggregate information about how much plans
spent on reducing cost sharing for their members
(plan bids show that conventional plans expected
that a majority of rebate dollars would be used to
reduce cost sharing). In its rulemaking, CMS chose
not to collect this information because it assumed it

Starting in 2024, CMS began requiring MA plans to
report their expenditures on supplemental benefits
as part of the data used to monitor and enforce
medical-loss-ratio (MLR) requirements.34 Under
the new requirements, MA organizations (MAOs)
are required to report, for each contract, their
expenditures for 16 non-Medicare services that

However, the data have several limitations. One
limitation is that the data are reported at the
contract level. MA contracts can consist of multiple
plans and can span wide geographic areas. Contractlevel reporting means that we cannot assess how
spending on supplemental benefits varies at the
plan or enrollee level. Because MA contracts can
comingle conventional plans, SNPs, and employer
plans, it is difficult to answer certain questions
such as how spending by SNPs compares with
spending by conventional plans. Because employer
plans do not submit bids, we do not have a basis for
accurately estimating the share of the rebate that
those plans expected to allocate to non-Medicare
services, making it difficult to compare plans’ rebate
revenue with their expectations and expenditures.

368 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

MA medical-loss-ratio data show MA organizations spent roughly $24 billion on
non-Medicare services offered as supplemental benefits in 2023 (cont.)
would be “exceedingly difficult for MA organizations
to separately identify and track” such spending
(Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2022c).
As a result, we cannot know the total amounts that
insurers spent on all supplemental benefits; we can
assess spending only for the subset of benefits that
CMS requires insurers to report separately.
An additional barrier is that the spending reported
in the MLR data includes benefits that are financed
by rebates and benefits for which enrollees paid an
additional premium.35 Further, the data include only
the amount the plan spent on the benefit, excluding
any portion of the rebate the plan kept to cover
its administrative costs or profit. In 2026, of the
rebate dollars that plans project using to provide
non-Medicare services as supplemental benefits,
conventional plans project that 13 percent will be
used for plan administrative costs and profit; SNPs
project using 14 percent.
A further limitation of the data is that some of the
reporting categories—including some with very high
levels of spending—are somewhat broad, making
it difficult to understand what types of services
taxpayer dollars are being used for. (For example,
non–primarily health-related benefits, which
can encompass a range of items and services, are
grouped into just two categories.) Last, because the
data lack measures of utilization, the MLR data alone
cannot tell us anything about how much MAOs are
spending per user or per unit (MAOs report per
member per month amounts, but these provide
limited insight because benefit eligibility, access, and
use can vary widely across MA plans). Altogether,
although the new data provide several valuable
new insights into insurers’ overall spending on
supplemental benefits, data limitations significantly
restrict the types of analyses for which the data can
be used; the limitations underscore the ongoing
importance of the other data-collection efforts that
CMS has begun implementing (e.g., in the encounter
data and Part C and Part D reporting requirements).

A large share of supplemental-benefit
spending is on non–primarily healthrelated benefits
We analyzed the 2023 MLR data for a subset of MA
contracts to characterize MAOs’ spending on nonMedicare services offered as supplemental benefits.
We limited our analysis to contracts for HMOs,
local and regional preferred provider organizations
(PPOs), and private fee-for-service (PFFS) plans. We
excluded contracts that included either Medicare
Medical Savings Account (MSA) plans or end-stage
renal disease special-needs plans (ESRD SNPs)
because the rules for how these plans are paid
and how they may use rebates differ from other
types of MA plans (42 CFR 422.102(a)(1), 42 CFR
422.304(c)(1)). After these exclusions, our sample
included 98 percent of total 2023 MA enrollment
and 98 percent of reported supplemental-benefit
spending. We estimate that, in 2023, Medicare
paid approximately $74 billion in rebates to the
contracts included in our analysis.
We then summed the total supplemental-benefit
spending that MAOs reported for non-Medicare
services (Table 12-5, p. 370). For 2023, MAOs
reported spending roughly $24 billion on the nonMedicare services they offered as supplemental
benefits (i.e., supplemental benefits other than
cost-sharing and premium reductions). Dental
benefits accounted for the most spending,
encompassing more than a quarter (28 percent) of
the total reported spending. This finding aligns with
MedPAC’s previous analyses of MA bids, which have
shown that dental benefits account for a significant
portion of conventional MA plans’ projected
spending on non-Medicare services (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2025a).
The next-largest category of spending reported
by plans was for “non–primarily health-related”
benefits. In 2023, plans spent nearly $6 billion on
these types of benefits—accounting for roughly a
quarter (24 percent) of all reported supplemental(continued next page)
