purpose had to be “to prevent, cure, or diminish an
illness or injury” (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid
Services 2016). This definition included dental,
vision, and hearing services but also included
enhanced or expanded coverage of Medicare-

covered services (e.g., additional days of inpatient
care), health-related transportation services,
fitness benefits, acupuncture, enhanced diseasemanagement services, bathroom safety devices,
posthospitalization meals, over-the-counter items,
and other benefits. Beginning in plan year 2019, CMS
broadened its definition of “primarily health related”
to permit additional services that address physical
impairments, lessen the functional or psychological
(continued next page)

370 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

MA medical-loss-ratio data show MA organizations spent roughly $24 billion on
non-Medicare services offered as supplemental benefits in 2023 (cont.)
impact of injuries, or reduce avoidable health
care utilization (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid
Services 2018b). Under this new definition, plans can
provide services such as adult day-care services,
home-based palliative care, in-home support
services, and support for caregivers of enrollees.
Beginning in 2020, as required by the Bipartisan
Budget Act of 2018, MA plans were given the
ability to offer non–primarily health-related items
or services to chronically ill enrollees, so long as
there is a “reasonable expectation of improving
or maintaining the health or overall function of
the chronically ill enrollee” (Centers for Medicare
& Medicaid Services 2019). These benefits are
referred to as special supplemental benefits for the
chronically ill (SSBCI) and must be targeted to MA
enrollees who (1) have one or more comorbid and
medically complex chronic conditions that are life
threatening or that significantly limit the overall
health or function of the enrollee, (2) have a high risk
of hospitalization or other adverse health outcome,
and (3) require intensive care coordination.
Examples of SSBCI include things like meals, food
and produce, pest-control services, structural
home modifications, or subsidies for rent or utilities
(Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2019).
For 2023, MA plans reported spending just under
$700 million on SSBCI—roughly 12 percent of total
spending for non–primarily health-related benefits
and just 3 percent of spending reported across all
benefit categories.
The remainder of the roughly $6 billion spent on
non–primarily health-related supplemental benefits
was reported under a catch-all “other” (non-SSBCI)
non–primarily health-related supplemental benefits
category. In the MLR data for 2023, plans reported
more than $5 billion in this category (88 percent
of spending on non–primarily health-related
benefits and 21 percent of all reported spending
on supplemental benefits). This category likely
consists primarily of spending for supplemental

benefits offered under the MA–VBID demonstration,
which could include many of the same benefits
offered as SSBCI (such as food and produce, meals,
pest-control services) but offered with additional
regulatory flexibilities.36 MA plans participating
in the MA–VBID demonstration, which ran from
2017 through the end of 2025, were permitted
to offer non–primarily health-related benefits
and were given additional flexibility to target the
benefits to enrollees on the basis of socioeconomic
status (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services
2020b). A substantial share of MA plans that
offered non–primarily health-related benefits
in 2023 (especially D–SNPs) did so through the
MA–VBID demonstration (ATI Advisory 2023). In
2023, 86 percent of D–SNP enrollees were in plans
participating in the MA–VBID demonstration, and
the model was the predominant pathway by which
D–SNPs offered non–primarily health-related
supplemental benefits (ATI Advisory 2023, Friedman
et al. 2024, Laktas et al. 2024). Because the MA–VBID
demonstration ended at the end of 2025, the share
of spending reported in this category could change
in future years. Consulting groups, researchers,
and some MAOs have suggested that many benefits
offered under the VBID will transition to being
offered as SSBCI in 2026 (Friedman et al. 2024,
Health Management Associates 2025, Schmidt and
Graham 2025, UnitedHealth Group 2025).
The third-highest category of reported
supplemental benefit spending in 2023 was for
over-the-counter (OTC) items ($4.4 billion), which
accounted for 18 percent of total reported spending.
In recent years, it has been very common for OTC
benefits to be offered in the form of a prepaid
debit card, or “flex card,” provided to enrollees
by MA plans. Often, enrollees can use the cards
for expenses related to a set of plan-defined
supplemental benefits; it is particularly common for
plans to combine OTC benefits with other benefits
through these types of arrangements (ATI Advisory
2025, Yeh and Yen 2024). (Plans define which
(continued next page)

benefits the cards can be used for, and the benefits
can be primarily or non–primarily health related).
Altogether, spending on non–primarily healthrelated benefits and OTC items accounted for more
than 40 percent of reported supplemental benefit
spending for 2023. MAOs’ reported spending for
most other categories of benefits was considerably
lower: spending for vision, transportation, fitness,
and hearing benefits—some of the most widely
available benefits across MA plans—together
accounted for less than $5 billion of total reported
supplemental-benefit spending (about 17 percent).

For a sample of MA contracts, insurerreported spending on supplemental
benefits is in line with projections in
plans’ bids
One important question about MAOs’ spending
for supplemental benefits is how it compares with
the rebates MA insurers receive from Medicare. As
with medical spending, MAOs accept financial risk
for their enrollees’ use of supplemental benefits.
If enrollees use their supplemental benefits more
intensively than the plan expected, such that
the cost of that use exceeds the rebate the plan
received from Medicare, the plan bears the cost of
the difference. If enrollees use their supplemental
benefits less than expected and the cost to the plan
is less than what the plan received in rebates, the
insurer benefits. (Insurers may mitigate risk by, for
example, entering into risk-sharing agreements with
third-party vendors or providers of supplemental

differences) cause the risk scores of MA enrollees to
overpredict what their spending would have been
in FFS—increasing MA payments by an estimated 11
percent ($57 billion) above FFS spending in 2026. In
addition, even after CMS applies the coding-intensity
adjustment, diagnostic coding by MA plans overstates
the differences in health conditions between MA and
FFS enrollees that are used for risk scores—further

372 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

benefits). There could be cause for concern if plans’
spending is consistently below the amount the plans
received in rebates.
To assess this possibility, we identified a subsample
of MA contracts and compared their reported
spending with the rebates they received—which
were estimated using information from their
bids. Because employer plans do not submit bids,
we limited our subsample to bids of plans in MA
contracts that did not have any members enrolled
in employer plans. Roughly one-third of MA
enrollees from our full sample were included in our
subsample.
Medicare paid the contracts in our subsample
approximately $25 billion in rebates. On average,
those contracts projected spending roughly 45
percent of their rebates—roughly $11 billion of
the $25 billion—on non-Medicare services (plans
projected allocating 53 percent of their rebates to
non-Medicare services when administrative costs
and profit were included). Looking at the MLR data
for these contracts, we found that these contracts
reported spending about $11 billion on non-Medicare
services. In other words, we found that—in the
aggregate—for a limited subset of contracts, plans’
reported spending was roughly in line with the
amount we would expect based on their bids.
However, the results varied by contract and by
insurer, with some insurers and contracts reporting
spending that was higher or lower than the expected
level (based on their bids). ■

increasing MA payments by an estimated 4 percent
($22 billion) above FFS spending in 2026.

for all enrollees, including those with ESRD. We
estimate that, because of differences between MA and
FFS stemming from favorable selection and coding
intensity, Medicare spends 14 percent more for MA
enrollees than it would spend if those beneficiaries
were enrolled in FFS Medicare, a difference that
translates to a projected $76 billion—or 12 percent of
total payments to MA plans—in 2026.38 The payments
above FFS spending comprise a large share of the
funding for plan rebates in 2026, which we estimate
to be 17 percent of FFS spending and 15 percent of
total payments to plans. These higher payments
relative to FFS Medicare vary significantly across MA
organizations (as shown by the variation in the effects
of coding intensity across MA organizations, among
other factors).
Importantly, these payments above what would have
occurred in FFS Medicare are not an estimate of plan
profits and administrative expenses. Instead, these
additional payments are the primary reason that plans
are able to provide generous supplemental benefits and
better financial protection for many MA enrollees, which
help attract enrollees and increase plans’ total revenues.

We reach this estimate by first projecting total
payments ($615 billion) to MA plans in 2026. We obtain
this number by using the Trustees’ projections of MA
enrollment and MA payments per capita (Boards of
Trustees 2025).39 We project that those payments,
on average, will be equal to about 99 percent of FFS
spending in 2026 (Table 12-6), after standardizing for
differences in risk scores and the counties of residence
between MA and FFS beneficiaries. Benchmarks and
bids will be 107 percent and 83 percent of FFS spending,
respectively. Those estimates are calculated before
accounting for any inaccuracies in the risk-adjustment
model used for payment.
However, to accurately compare MA and FFS spending,
we must ensure that the two populations are
comparable by accounting for differences in coding
intensity and favorable selection. “Coding intensity”
refers to the tendency for more diagnosis codes to be
recorded for MA enrollees, which causes risk scores
for the same beneficiaries to be higher when they are
enrolled in MA than they would be in FFS. “Favorable
selection” into MA occurs when beneficiaries whose
risk scores overpredict their spending tend to enroll

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

373

in MA; it is the extent to which, if MA enrollees were in
FFS, their risk-standardized spending would be lower
than the FFS average; that is, it captures MA enrollees’
preexisting tendency for lower spending before any
intervention from MA plans (including coding intensity
or plan management). As in prior years, we have made
technical refinements to our methods for estimating
coding intensity and favorable selection, and these
updates are reflected in both projected and historical
comparisons of MA payments and FFS spending.40
Accounting for greater coding intensity and
favorable selection in MA increases our projections
of benchmarks, bids, and plan payments as a share of
what FFS spending would have been for MA enrollees.
Indeed, we project that benchmarks in 2026 are
124 percent of what FFS spending would have been,
indicating that MA plans bid against an amount that
is 24 percent more, in aggregate, than what their
enrollees would have cost if they were in FFS Medicare
(Table 12-6, p. 373). Actual MA payments in 2026 are
an estimated 114 percent of FFS spending because
nearly all plans bid below those benchmarks. Plan bids
are typically far below their benchmarks but can vary
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2022).41
Our projection of the payment difference between
MA and FFS for 2026 (14 percent) is lower than the
projection we reported last year for 2025 (20 percent).
That difference stems largely from a lower estimate
of the effect of coding intensity on payments in
2026. That change is primarily due to the completed
phase-in of the V28 risk model and the availability
of new risk-score data that show slower growth in
MA risk scores under the prior risk model in recent
years. The Commission has supported the change to
the V28 risk model, and our lower estimated payment
difference indicates that V28 is having the intended
effect of reducing the impact of differences in MA
and FFS diagnosis coding on payments. (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2025b). This effect is
discussed in greater detail in our estimate of coding
intensity (p. 390).
MA bids and rebate levels are consistent with
both plan efficiencies and higher payments from
coding and selection

We project that—after accounting for coding intensity
and favorable selection—plan bids ($1,132 per enrollee
per month) in 2026 are 95 percent of what FFS spending

374 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

would have been for MA enrollees (Table 12-6, p. 373). In
other words, plans are offering to provide the standard
Medicare benefit for an estimated 5 percent less than
it would cost the Medicare program to cover their
enrollees in the FFS program. Bids in 2026 were the
lowest relative to FFS that we have estimated in recent
years. For example, we estimate that bids were about
98 percent of FFS spending in 2024 and 97 percent of
FFS spending in 2025 (data not shown). This decrease
in relative bids coincides with the phase-in of the
V28 risk model, which decreased plan risk scores. On
a risk-adjusted basis, lower risk scores caused plan
benchmarks to be lower than they otherwise would
have been, and it appears that plans responded to their
lower risk-adjusted benchmarks with lower bids.42 Plan
bids in 2026 are projected to be 23 percent lower than
risk-adjusted benchmarks, on average, unchanged from
2025 despite the downward pressure on risk-adjusted
benchmarks in 2026 (data not shown).
Altogether, our estimates suggest that plans were
able to maintain their aggregate level of rebates ($222
per enrollee per month) in 2026 by adjusting their
bids (and, in fact, rebates reached a new record high)
despite the complete phase-in of the V28 risk model.
At the same time, the program’s overall payment
difference between MA and FFS fell during the years
of V28 implementation. We estimate that the Medicare
program paid MA plans 21 percent more than what FFS
spending would have been in 2023 (the year before the
transition to V28 began), which is projected to fall to
14 percent more than FFS spending in 2026 (when the
model was fully implemented). Our estimates suggest
that, overall, plans have largely absorbed or adapted
to those lower payments in order to achieve similar
levels of rebates that fund supplemental benefits to
their enrollees. Those estimates reflect the average
experience in 2026. Experiences may vary by market,
and the Commission will continue to monitor the
effects of changes in MA payments.

their projected cost of providing the standard Medicare
benefit to their enrolleest, including the amounts
they expect to spend on their enrollees’ medical
expenses, administrative costs, and profit. Figure 12-4
reports plans’ overall projected medical expenses and

three components of the Commission’s methodology for comparing MA payments with FFS spending: a base comparison of MA payments with
FFS spending that standardizes for differences in risk scores and geography; an adjustment to that base comparison for favorable selection;
and an adjustment for coding intensity. MA payments have two components: the bid and the rebate. Estimates of medical expenses and
administrative expenses and profit relative to FFS spending break out the bid portion of plans’ payments into those two components using

(Frank and Milhaupt 2023, Frank and Milhaupt 2022).
Therefore, the administrative expenses and profits
reported in plan bids may be a lower bound on the
portion of bids that are accounted for by those items.
One way to assess the validity of the Commission’s
estimate of MA payments relative to FFS spending is
to verify that the overall estimate is consistent with
aggregate data from several sources, including plan
bids, overall rebate levels, and studies of MA plans’
efficiencies in reducing the medical spending of their
members relative to FFS. As just described, comparing
plans’ bids with our estimates of what FFS spending
would have been for MA enrollees implies that medical
efficiencies were between 15 percent and 18 percent
on average between 2024 and 2026. In other words,
our estimates are consistent with (1) plans achieving
material efficiencies in their medical expenses relative
to FFS and (2) higher payments from selection and
coding. Without accounting for the effects of favorable
selection and coding, plans’ bids would have indicated
that plan medical spending projections would be 28
percent below FFS spending in 2026—far more than
the 18 percent medical spending reductions that our
analysis finds for 2026. We reviewed recent studies
to assess whether the Commission’s estimates of MA
plans’ medical spending reductions relative to FFS were
supported by the literature. The studies we reviewed
generally did not indicate that MA plans reduce their
enrollees’ medical expenses by nearly 30 percent on
average, indicating the implausibility of estimates that
do not account for selection and coding.
We found that the weight of the recent evidence on
MA plans’ medical spending reductions is in line with
the Commission’s estimates. Three recent studies
we identified found estimates of MA plans reducing
their enrollees’ medical expenses, estimates that
ranged from 12 percent to 16 percent, similar to the
15 percent to 18 percent we estimated by comparing
the components of plan bids to the Commission’s
estimate of what FFS spending would have been for
MA enrollees.43 Overall, the studies indicate that
substantial effects of coding and selection are needed
to explain the bid and rebate payments observed in the
MA market.
Our review included two recent white papers
(Inovalon 2025, Teigland et al. 2025a) and one recent
peer-reviewed study that estimated the effect of MA

376 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

enrollment on medical spending (Jung et al. 2025).
Both white papers examined the combined effect
of MA enrollment on medical and prescription drug
spending, and both papers used a study design that
controlled for preexisting differences between a
sample of MA enrollees and a FFS comparison group.
One white paper focused on the non-dual-eligible
population and found that MA plans reduced spending
by 12 percent relative to FFS (Teigland et al. 2025a).
The second white paper included non–dually eligible
and the dual-eligible population in their sample and
found that MA plans reduced spending by 10 percent
relative to FFS, including a 16 percent reduction in
spending for nonprescription medical costs (Inovalon
2025).44,45 Neither white paper found spending
reductions for prescription drugs. The peer-reviewed
study by Jung and colleagues used MA encounter
data to examine differences in spending between
MA and FFS beneficiaries using a sample of MA and
FFS beneficiaries from every state. That study found
13 percent lower spending in MA relative to FFS
when controlling for some differences in beneficiary
characteristics.46 While these studies were the best
available recent evidence at the time of publication,
they are subject to limitations. The two white papers
used small, nonrepresentative samples for their study
populations, and the study by Jung and colleagues
did not include all types of medical spending and
only partially accounted for differences between
the MA population and FFS comparison group. The
Commission will continue to monitor this literature.

percent above FFS spending, respectively. This change
is largely explained by an increase in the effect of
coding intensity. However, the phase-in of the V28 risk
model reduced the effect of coding intensity starting
in 2024. For 2025, the lower effect of coding intensity
resulted in MA payments that are a projected 17 percent

Our current methodology for estimating MA payments
relative to FFS spending has three components. These
three components are estimated separately and are
then combined in a single calculation of MA spending
relative to FFS. First, we estimate a base comparison of
MA payments relative to an estimate of FFS spending
that is standardized for differences in average risk
scores and geography but does not account for the

378 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

effects of coding intensity and favorable selection.
Second, we estimate the effect of favorable selection
and use that estimate to adjust the base comparison of
MA payments with FFS spending. Third, independently
of the effects of favorable selection, we estimate the
effect of coding intensity and use that estimate to make
an additional adjustment to the base comparison. We
use historical data to estimate the effect of each of the
three components on MA payments for the most recent
year for which data are available (2023 for the basecomparison and favorable-selection components, 2024
for coding intensity) and earlier years. We then project

Incorporating MA enrollees with ESRD into MedPAC’s comparison of payments
to MA plans relative to FFS spending

O

ur comparison of payments to Medicare
Advantage (MA) plans relative to feefor-service (FFS) spending has three
components: a base comparison, an estimate of the
effect of favorable selection, and an estimate of the
effect of coding intensity. In prior years, all of these
analyses excluded Medicare beneficiaries with endstage renal disease (ESRD) because Medicare uses a
separate payment system based on state rates rather
than bids and benchmarks and uses a separate set
of risk models to pay MA plans for enrollees with
ESRD. We had not yet analyzed the effects of coding
intensity and favorable selection under that risk
model (see section “Medicare payment and MA plan
costs for enrollees with ESRD” (p. 412) for more
details about those payments). In this year’s report,
we are now incorporating estimates for MA enrollees
with ESRD into each of the three components of our
comparison of Medicare spending on MA and FFS.
While only 1 percent of the MA-eligible population
are beneficiaries with ESRD, their average
Medicare spending is high relative to the non-ESRD
population. The share of MA enrollment among
beneficiaries with ESRD has grown rapidly in recent
years since the implementation of the 21st Century

Cures Act, which allowed beneficiaries in dialysis
status to enroll in an MA plan after starting dialysis
treatment. (See section on “Medicare payment
and MA plan costs for enrollees with ESRD” for
more information about MA enrollees with ESRD,
p. 412). CMS categorizes beneficiaries with ESRD
into three groups based on their treatment status:
dialysis, transplant (i.e., within the first 3 months
of a kidney transplant), and functioning graft (i.e.,
4 to 36 months after a kidney transplant). Unlike
enrollees without ESRD, payments to MA plans
for their ESRD population are not based on a plan
bid. Instead, CMS pays MA plans for enrollees with
ESRD based on a local risk-standardized average
(e.g., rates for beneficiaries in dialysis and transplant
status reflect the state-based spending average for
a dialysis or transplant beneficiary with a risk score
of 1.0). For enrollees in dialysis or transplant status,
CMS pays plans using their projections of statewide
average FFS Medicare spending for beneficiaries
with ESRD who are in dialysis status. For enrollees
in functioning-graft status, plans receive their nonESRD MA county benchmark rate. CMS adjusts the
base payment rate for each enrollee with ESRD
using the beneficiary’s risk score. CMS calibrates
three separate ESRD risk models, one for each

what we expect each component to be through 2026
in years for which data are not yet available, to provide
the Congress with our best estimate for the current
year. Those projections are subject to uncertainty.
In future reports, we will provide updated payment
comparisons for those years using historical data. More
details on the methods used for estimating each of the
components can be found later in this chapter and in
the technical appendixes to this chapter.

Before accounting for the effects of coding
intensity and favorable selection, MA
payments are generally similar to what FFS
spending would have been
The first component of the Commission’s payment
comparison involves constructing a base comparison
of MA payments with FFS spending for beneficiaries
with similar risk scores and counties of residence. We
begin with that base comparison because it aligns with

379

Incorporating MA enrollees with ESRD into MedPAC’s comparison of payments
to MA plans relative to FFS spending (cont.)
of the dialysis, transplant, and functioning-graft
populations (these models are different from the V24
and V28 risk models used for the population without
ESRD). Beneficiary risk scores are calculated using
the risk model that corresponds with their ESRD
treatment designation during the month.
For the base comparison, we generally followed the
same method of comparison we previously used
for non-ESRD beneficiaries (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2023c), but we made some
necessary modifications to account for differences
in the payment system for MA enrollees with ESRD
(for which plans do not bid against benchmarks).
We aggregate the amount of spending for the FFS
comparison group for each applicable MA enrollee
with ESRD (based on having the same area of
residence and risk score) and compare this amount
with the aggregated MA payment amounts for
those enrollees.
• For the dialysis and transplant populations, we
estimated MA payments using CMS’s state-based
payment rates (which are risk standardized
for a beneficiary with average risk, exclude
indirect medical education payments, and apply
CMS’s user-fee deduction), the number of MA
enrollee months in dialysis and transplant status
within those states, and their risk scores.48
For the FFS comparison group, we calculated
the monthly average of risk-standardized
spending per dialysis/transplant beneficiary
at the state level.49 State-level spending for
the comparison group included nonclaims
spending for provider incentive payments (e.g.,
shared-savings payments),50 dialysis bad-debt
payments, provider-settlement payments,51 and an
administrative add-on factor that reflects CMS’s
estimate of administrative costs when setting
rates for non-ESRD MA enrollees.52 We excluded
medical education payments, organ-acquisition
costs, and spending during hospice enrollment.53
We used our calculation of the state-based

risk-standardized spending average for the FFS
comparison group and multiplied this average by
the risk-adjusted (using MA enrollee risk scores)
MA (dialysis and transplant status) enrollment
months in each state. Thus, our total estimate
compares the aggregate MA payments for dialysis
and transplant enrollees with their corresponding
aggregate FFS spending amounts (using the same
risk score and state of residence).
• For the functioning-graft population, we
estimated MA payments using CMS’s countylevel payment rates (which use county-level
benchmarks for the non-ESRD population
that are risk standardized for a beneficiary
with average risk), the number of MA enrollee
months in functioning-graft status within those
counties, and their risk scores.54 For the FFS
comparison group, we calculated the monthly
average of risk-standardized spending per
functioning-graft beneficiary at the county and
state level.55 Because MA payment rates for
the functioning-graft population are not based
on a historical baseline of the population’s FFS
spending, CMS does not publish historical data
on nonclaims spending for this population.
Thus, our FFS comparison group included our
imputations of nonclaims spending for provider
incentive payments (e.g., shared-savings
payments),56 provider-settlement payments,57
and administrative costs. In addition, we applied
exclusions for estimates of spending in which MA
plans are not liable (medical education payments,
organ-acquisition costs, and spending during
hospice enrollment).58 Because the functioninggraft population divided into counties is
relatively small and could produce unreliable
results, we blended our county-based estimate
of risk-standardized spending with our statebased estimate (similar to CMS’s framework
for non-ESRD credibility adjustment).59 We
used our blended calculation of local-area riskstandardized spending averages for the FFS

comparison group and multiplied these averages

Using 2023 data, we found that MA ESRD payments
were 103 percent of what they would have been in
FFS—before the effects of selection and coding. For
the dialysis and transplant populations (representing
an estimated 91 percent of ESRD spending for MA
enrollees in 2023), we found that MA payments were
101 percent of what they would been in FFS. The 1
percent estimate above FFS spending likely reflects
a small degree of projection error in payment rates.
We observed a sharp decline (44 percent) in total
dialysis bad-debt payments in 2023, which may
have been unexpected in CMS’s projections and
contributed to expected spending that was higher
than actual spending. For the functioning-graft
population (representing an estimated 9 percent of
ESRD spending for MA enrollees in 2023), we found
that MA payments were 123 percent of what they
would have been in FFS. Developing an accurate
local-area payment rate for the relatively small
number of functioning-graft enrollees is challenging,
and the non-ESRD county benchmark served as a
poor proxy of risk-standardized spending for this
population in 2023. The combined dollar-weighted
estimates for all enrollees with ESRD resulted in an
overall estimate of MA ESRD payments that were 103
percent of what they would have been in FFS in 2023
(before the effects of favorable selection and coding
intensity). We use that 103 percent as the projected
value of the base component for enrollees with
ESRD for 2024 through 2026.
We conducted an analysis of favorable selection into
MA for beneficiaries with ESRD using our simple
method for switchers into MA from 2021 and 2023.

(See Technical Appendix 12-B, p. 425, for more
details about our simple method for estimating
selection.) In each year, we estimated a selection
percentage of either 99 percent or 100 percent,
which suggests that there is not a meaningful
selection effect (either favorable or adverse) among
the ESRD population into MA. Therefore, for our MA
payments relative to FFS spending comparison, we
estimate a selection effect of roughly 0 percent for
beneficiaries with ESRD in all years.
We also conducted analyses of coding intensity
for MA enrollees with ESRD, separately for the
three different risk-adjustment models for ESRD
beneficiaries. The transplant risk model does not
rely on diagnosis codes, so differences in MA and
FFS coding intensity are not possible. For enrollees
in dialysis and functioning-graft status, we applied
the demographic estimate of coding intensity
method as outlined in Technical Appendix 12-C, pp.
440, to generate a coding-intensity estimate for
beneficiaries on dialysis and for beneficiaries with
a functioning graft. Then we combined the three
estimates of coding intensity (including an estimate
of zero for the transplant population), weighting
each by its respective share of payments to MA
plans for ESRD enrollees in dialysis, transplant, or
functioning-graft status to generate a single ESRD
coding-intensity estimate. (See Technical Appendix
12-C, p. 440, for more details about our codingintensity estimates.) For example, we estimated
2026 coding intensity to be 9 percent for the dialysis
population, 9 percent for the functioning-graft
population (after coding-intensity adjustment, which
applies to functioning-graft risk scores but does not
apply to dialysis or transplant risk scores), and we
assumed coding intensity to be 0 percent for the
transplant population. To combine these estimates,
we weighted these estimates by 89 percent, 9
percent, and 2 percent, respectively, for a codingintensity estimate of 9 percent for the whole ESRD
population in 2026 after coding adjustment.

average (i.e., adjusted for a beneficiary with average
risk) in counties where MA enrollees reside and apply
the risk-adjusted (using MA risk scores) MA enrollment
months to the FFS spending average. The Commission
uses historical data on the actual FFS spending of
beneficiaries with both Part A and Part B coverage
(that is, people who would be eligible to enroll in MA)
to estimate the base payment comparison for the most
recent year for which data are available (2023).60 Our
estimates of the base payment comparison for years in
which we do not have historical data (2024 to 2026) rely
instead on CMS’s projections of FFS spending. Those
projections differ from the actual FFS spending of MAeligible beneficiaries for several reasons, including

that the projections include Part A–only enrollees and
that CMS’s projection of the FFS-spending trend is
subject to uncertainty. The method that CMS uses to
produce its FFS projections has been criticized because
it includes beneficiaries who have Part A but not Part B
coverage, although MA enrollees are required to have
both Part A and Part B coverage.61 The Commission
has recognized this shortcoming and previously
recommended that CMS calculate MA benchmarks
using FFS-spending data only for beneficiaries with
both Part A and Part B coverage (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2021a, Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2017).
The Commission’s estimate that Medicare payments
to MA plans in 2026 average 99 percent of projected
FFS spending—without any adjustments for coding
intensity or favorable selection—reflects several
aspects of MA payment policy (Table 12-6, p. 373). First,
benchmarks are set above projected FFS spending
in some counties and below FFS spending in other
counties. Second, benchmarks are increased based
on plan quality scores—resulting in quality-bonus
payments that are financed with additional program
dollars. We estimate that quality bonuses will account
for about 3 percent (an estimated $16 billion) of MA
payments in 2026. Combining both of those factors, we
project benchmarks to be 107 percent of FFS spending
in 2026 before accounting for coding and selection.
Third, payments to MA plans are below benchmarks
because bids are nearly always lower than benchmarks,
and plans receive a percentage (based on quality score)
of the difference between bids and benchmarks in
additional rebate payments.
We estimated the base comparison of MA payments
with FFS spending using both CMS’s projections and
the observed risk-standardized FFS spending of MAeligible beneficiaries enrolled in both Part A and Part B
in each year from 2016 to 2023 (Figure 12-7, p. 384). Our
analysis of historical data has shown that using CMS’s
spending projections has not systematically impacted
our base comparison of MA spending with FFS
payments.62 Both methods produced results that were
within 1 percentage point of each other for every year
except 2020 and 2021, years that were affected by the
coronavirus pandemic. The similarity of the estimates
for the two methods indicates that relying on CMS’s
projections of FFS spending (including adjustments
for Medicare as a secondary payer) for estimating

differences in MA and FFS spending has not introduced
meaningful error in our analysis, and increases our
confidence in relying on CMS’s projections for our
payment comparisons for 2024 to 2026.63 We will
continue to compare how our estimates differ between
using CMS’s projections and using historical data.
Should a systematic difference between the two
emerge, we will consider incorporating an adjustment
to our base payment comparison for the years that rely
on projected data.
We note that the comparison of our historical
estimates with CMS’s projections are not equivalent
to examining the unadjusted differences in national
raw FFS spending per capita with and without the Part
A-only population. In 2023, on an unadjusted basis,
national spending per capita was about 6 percent
higher when excluding the Part A-only population. This
finding suggests that other adjustments (e.g., Medicare
as a secondary payer, risk score normalization of Part
A-only enrollees, county-level standardization, countylevel distribution of MA enrollment) have caused our
historical estimates to be similar to CMS’s projections.

Before coding differences, favorable
selection causes risk-based payments to
overpredict spending for MA enrollees
The second component of the Commission’s payment
comparison adjusts the base comparison of MA
payments relative to FFS spending for favorable
selection. When setting MA benchmarks and paying
plans for each enrollee, CMS implicitly assumes that
if MA enrollees were in FFS Medicare, their average
Medicare spending would be equal to that of current
FFS enrollees in the same county after adjusting for
differences in risk scores. However, applying MA risk
scores to FFS-spending averages may overpredict the
actual spending that MA enrollees would have had in
FFS for two reasons: coding intensity and favorable
selection. (The Commission’s estimates of coding
intensity are discussed below.)
“Favorable selection” refers to the tendency for
Medicare’s risk-adjustment model to—on average—
overpredict the spending that the MA-enrolled
population would have had if they were enrolled in
the FFS program, even for beneficiaries with similar
coding intensity. Favorable selection can occur due to
unmeasured differences in health status but can also

result from factors such as differences in beneficiaries’
propensities to seek care for reasons that are
unrelated to their health (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2024c).
Risk models are imperfect, and beneficiaries with the
same risk score typically have a wide distribution of
actual spending relative to the spending predicted by
their risk score (Lieberman et al. 2023). Each year, a

384 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

mix of beneficiaries enroll in MA who either have lower
spending than predicted by their risk score or higher
spending than predicted by their risk score. The MA
program as a whole will experience favorable selection
if the average MA enrollee has less spending than
predicted by their risk score (before any effects from
plans’ utilization-management and coding efforts).
Because favorable selection occurs when beneficiaries
systematically spend less than is predicted by their

risk score, higher-risk beneficiaries (including dually
eligible beneficiaries) are not necessarily unfavorable to
MA plans. Indeed, our estimates have shown evidence
of favorable selection for beneficiaries with both low
risk scores and high risk scores (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2025b).
The effect of favorable selection may vary over time.
Those differences over time reflect a combination
of factors, including changes to Medicare’s riskadjustment model and changes in the composition
of beneficiaries who choose MA and FFS. If the share
of beneficiaries in MA continues to increase, it is not
clear how favorable selection will change. It is possible
that as MA grows, the favorability of the MA program
will converge with the population remaining in FFS,
and favorable selection will decrease. Alternatively, it
is possible that as fewer beneficiaries remain in FFS,
benchmarks will be set on an increasingly small group
that is not representative of the overall Medicare
population. For example, beneficiaries remaining in
FFS may have a much higher rate of comprehensive
supplemental coverage, which would tend to increase
their use of care and may increase favorable selection
in MA. We have found that favorable selection persists
as the share of MA enrollees in a market increases
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2025b),
which is consistent with the findings of other studies
that have also found relatively small changes in
favorable selection as MA penetration increases (Crow
and Fiedler 2025, Lieberman et al. 2023). Thus, the
increase in MA penetration has likely coincided with
similar growth of average risk-standardized spending
in both FFS and MA.
The Commission has developed and refined its
methodology for estimating the effect of favorable
selection over several years (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2025b, Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2024c, Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2023b, Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2012a). We use our current
methodology to estimate an aggregate selection
percentage for the entire population of MA enrollees
in each year beginning in 2015. That methodology
produces a comprehensive estimate that calculates a
base selection percentage in the year before enrolling
in MA and then estimates how the selection percentage
would have changed over time through the estimated

target year (incorporating the effects of both attrition
out of MA and mean reversion).64,65 We assessed the
selection of enrollees with ESRD using a modified
approach, and we apply a selection effect of 0 percent
to enrollees with ESRD based on that analysis. (See text
box on incorporating MA enrollees with ESRD into our
payment comparison, pp. 379–382.)
Our current methodology for estimating favorable
selection followed a similar approach to the one used
last year, with several refinements that had small effects
on the overall estimate. We improved our method for
generalizing estimates from our study population (of
switchers) to other MA enrollees (i.e., those outside our
study population, including beneficiaries who joined MA
when they were newly eligible for Medicare). We now
account for differences in dual-eligibility status when
applying estimates outside our study population, along
with continuing to account for differences in mortality
status and year of initial enrollment. That refinement,
along with the studies discussed below that find
substantial selection in the newly eligible population,
gives us greater confidence in our approach to modeling
selection for the nonswitcher population. We also
refined our methodology to more fully account for MA
beneficiaries’ dual-eligibility status when modeling the
selection of switchers within our study population and
implemented several other technical updates. More detail
on our methodology for estimating favorable selection
can be found in Technical Appendix 12-B, p. 425.
A substantial and growing body of research has
found evidence of favorable selection in MA and
provides support for the Commission’s estimates and
methodology (Brown et al. 2014, Crow and Fiedler
2025, Curto et al. 2021, Curto et al. 2019, Fuglesten
Biniek et al. 2024, Goldberg et al. 2017, Government
Accountability Office 2021, Jacobs and Kronick 2018,
Jacobson et al. 2019, Lieberman et al. 2023, Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2023b, Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2012a, Meyers et al.
2019, Newhouse et al. 2015, Pelech et al. 2025, Rahman
et al. 2015, Riley 2012, Ryan et al. 2023, Teigland et al.
2025a). The Commission’s method uses data from the
portion of the MA population who previously switched
(i.e., “switchers”) to MA from the FFS program to
estimate selection for the entire population of MA
enrollees, including those who enrolled upon their
initial eligibility for Medicare. The Commission’s

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

385

approach adjusts for many of the differences between
the study and nonstudy populations by assigning
selection percentages to the nonstudy population
using the estimated selection of enrollees in our study
population with the same year of entry, dual-eligibility
status, and mortality status in the following two years.
Cumulatively, the Commission’s work and that of
other research provide support for our approach
that generalizes estimates based on switchers in our
study population to the other two segments of the
MA population: beneficiaries who switched from FFS
but had insufficient data to be included in our study
population and beneficiaries who enrolled in MA upon
becoming newly eligible for Medicare.66
•

Beneficiaries who switch from FFS to MA—Analyses
of switchers from FFS have been used in other
studies and have found substantial selection
effects (Crow and Fiedler 2025, Jacobson et al.
2019, Lieberman et al. 2023, Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2012a, Newhouse et al. 2015).
In addition, the Commission found substantial
selection effects among beneficiaries with only one
prior year of FFS enrollment (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2024c).

•

Newly eligible beneficiaries directly enrolling in
MA—Two recent studies also estimated favorable
selection among MA beneficiaries who enrolled
upon their initial eligibility for Medicare and found
estimates that were even larger than our estimates
for switchers in similar years (Pelech et al. 2025,
Teigland et al. 2025a).67 These studies covered
a sample of beneficiaries who had commercial
insurance before enrolling in MA and beneficiaries
who had Medicaid coverage before enrolling in MA.

Some FFS beneficiaries who are outside our study
population may contribute to additional favorable
selection. Our analyses require our FFS comparison
group to have at least one full year of FFS, which
means we are not able to capture the effects of
switchers from MA to FFS during their first year in
FFS (because risk scores include the effects of MA
coding intensity). However, other studies have found
that MA plans experience favorable selection when
these beneficiaries switch out of MA and into FFS
(Fuglesten Biniek et al. 2024, Goldberg et al. 2017,
Government Accountability Office 2021, Rahman et al.
2015, Riley 2012).

386 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

Our analysis using our comprehensive method finds
persistent favorable selection over several years. We
estimate that favorable selection of the overall MA
population resulted in MA payments that were 10
percent above FFS spending in 2023, a slight decrease
from 11 percent above FFS spending in 2022 (Figure
12-8).68 Those estimates express favorable selection
as the amount by which MA payments are above
what FFS costs would have been for MA enrollees
due to the payment system’s overprediction of costs.
Favorable selection can also be expressed as a selection
percentage, which is the estimate of what FFS costs
would have been for MA enrollees relative to their
predicted costs. The selection percentage was below 100
percent in every year during the 2015 to 2023 period,
indicating that the spending that the FFS program
would incur for the MA population would be below what
would be predicted by their risk score. For example,
the estimated selection percentage of 91 percent in
2023 indicates that MA enrollees would have spent 9
percent less than predicted if they were enrolled in
FFS. Favorable selection persisted throughout the study
period even as a larger share of Medicare beneficiaries
enrolled in MA, including enrollees who had higher
risk scores. The estimates presented in Figure 12-8 use
the Commission’s comprehensive method for the nonESRD population and apply our estimate of a 0 percent
selection effect for the ESRD population, which we
estimated using the Commission’s simple method. We
discuss these methods in greater detail in Technical
Appendix 12-B, p. 425.

Coding differences increase payments to MA
plans in 2026 by a projected $22 billion and
continue to generate inequity across plans
The third component of the Commission’s payment
comparison adjusts the base comparison of MA
payments relative to FFS spending for coding intensity.
Payments to MA plans are risk adjusted to account
for differences in health spending risk. Higher risk
scores increase payments to plans for enrollees with
higher expected Medicare spending. MA enrollees’
risk scores are based on demographic information and
diagnoses that plans submit to CMS. Our estimates of
coding intensity are independent of our estimates of
the effects of favorable selection because we estimate
selection before MA enrollment and subsequent
changes in selection using FFS data (which are
unaffected by plans’ practices of coding diagnoses).

MA (Medicare Advantage), FFS (fee-for-service). Estimates include MA enrollees with and without end-stage renal disease (ESRD). Estimates for
beneficiaries without ESRD were constructed using the Commission’s comprehensive method for estimating favorable selection. Beneficiaries
with ESRD were estimated to have no favorable-selection effect using the Commission’s simple method for estimating favorable selection.
Selection percentages are computed as the ratio of the estimated spending that the FFS program would have incurred for MA enrollees relative
to the spending predicted by their county benchmark and Medicare’s risk-adjustment model. Selection-percentage values farther below
100 percent indicate greater favorable selection. Estimates are rounded to the nearest percent. Estimates of the impact of selection on MA
payments relative to FFS spending are computed using the inverse of the selection percentage (e.g., the impact of selection in 2023 was 100
percent divided by 91 percent minus 1). Thus, the impact of selection represents predicted spending for MA enrollees (for which MA payments
are based) as a percentage of what spending would have been for MA enrollees in FFS.

Source: MedPAC analysis of Medicare enrollment (2006–2023), Medicare claims spending (2007–2023), and risk-adjustment files (2007–2023).

Documenting additional diagnosis codes raises
plan enrollees’ risk scores, which increases the
monthly payments that MA plans receive from
Medicare and the rebates that plans receive to
provide supplemental benefits to enrollees. Plans
that document relatively more diagnosis codes for
enrollees with similar levels of health risk have a
competitive advantage over other plans.

chart reviews, that MA plans use to capture diagnoses—
tools that are not available or are not used as frequently
in FFS Medicare—coding intensity is higher in MA
than in FFS, and payments to MA plans are higher than
intended.

Documenting an additional HCC for an enrollee can
significantly increase Medicare’s payment to a plan
for that enrollee. Because of the increased financial
incentives for MA plans to code more diagnoses and the
additional tools, such as health risk assessments and

For 2026, we project that MA risk scores will be about
10 percent above risk scores for comparable FFS
beneficiaries. CMS’s adjustment reduces MA risk scores
by 5.9 percent. Thus, overall coding intensity is only
partially offset by CMS’s coding-intensity adjustment.
The net effect is a 4 percent increase in MA risk scores
due to coding intensity, leading to $22 billion in higher
projected payments to MA plans in 2026.

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

387

To align with our overall comparison of spending on MA
relative to FFS, we estimate MA coding intensity relative
to FFS risk scores (i.e., MA risk scores are 10 percent
higher than FFS risk scores due to coding intensity). The
denominator for CMS’s coding adjustment, however,
is the average MA risk score. Because the average MA
risk score is larger than the average FFS risk score, the
two percentages are not equivalent. Based on our MA
coding-intensity estimate of 10 percent above FFS, we
estimate that CMS’s coding adjustment would have
had to reduce MA risk scores by 9.3 percent to fully
account for the effects of coding intensity in 2026.69
However, coding intensity varies significantly across
MA organizations. As a result, CMS’s across-the-board
adjustment for coding intensity, which reduces all MA
risk scores by the same amount, generates inequity
across organizations by reducing net revenue for plans
with lower coding intensity and allowing other plans
to retain a significant amount of revenue from higher
coding intensity (see Figure 12-12, p. 397). Increasing
CMS’s across-the-board coding adjustment to 9.3
percent would increase the penalty for plans with
lower coding intensity while still not fully addressing
the effects of coding intensity for the organizations
with higher coding levels. A policy addressing coding
intensity more fairly would reduce risk scores more for
plans with the highest coding intensity.
The Commission’s approach to estimating coding
intensity compares risk scores for the MA and FFS
populations under the assumption that demographically
similar populations of beneficiaries have a similar
average prevalence of health conditions. We use the
combination of age, sex, Medicaid eligibility, and
institutional status to define demographic similarity.
Any population-level differences in risk scores that
are greater than what would be predicted using those
demographic factors are attributed to coding intensity.
More details on the methodology can be found in
Technical Appendix 12-C, p. 440, including a discussion
of sensitivity analyses we conducted to assess the effect
of including additional characteristics when defining
demographic similarity.
In our March 2016 report to the Congress, the
Commission recommended a multipronged approach
that would fully account for the impact of coding
differences between MA and FFS, improve the equity
of the adjustment across MA contracts, and improve
incentives to reduce costs and improve quality. The

388 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

Commission’s approach to reducing MA coding intensity
has been to address the mechanisms that generate
coding differences first (e.g., remove health risk
assessments and reduce year-to-year coding variations
by using two years of diagnostic data) and then address
remaining differences with either an across-theboard or tiered adjustment. The Commission’s 2016
recommendation did not address the use of chart
reviews because data were not available at that time,
but eliminating chart reviews as a source of diagnoses
for risk adjustment would be consistent with the
Commission’s approach.
Documenting more diagnosis codes increases
payments to plans

Among the 20 most common HCCs in MA—which
have payment amounts ranging from roughly $1,000
to $5,500—the average additional payment per HCC is
about $3,400 per year. Documenting each additional
HCC for an enrollee can thus significantly increase
Medicare’s payment to a plan. We can illustrate how
coding additional HCCs increases payment to a plan for
Medicare-covered services using average FFS Medicare
spending.70 For example, in 2022, the annual Medicare
payment to an MA organization for a non-Medicaideligible 80-year-old male (where the demographic
component of the risk score is valued at $6,726) with
diabetes without complication (HCC 19, valued at $1,284)
would have been $8,010. If the same 80-year-old male
with diabetes were also found to have vascular disease
(HCC 108, valued at $3,620), the Medicare annual
payment to the MA organization would increase to
$11,630.
Because the CMS–HCC model uses FFS Medicare claims
data to estimate the size of the model coefficients,
the model calculates an expected spending amount
based on FFS Medicare costs and diagnostic-coding
patterns. Most diagnoses are reported on physician and
outpatient claims, which in FFS Medicare tend to be paid
based on procedure codes, thus providing little financial
incentive to document diagnoses for FFS beneficiaries.
This distinction can lead to relative underreporting of
diagnoses in FFS. If certain diagnoses are not reported
on FFS claims, the cost of treating those conditions is
attributed to other components in the model, causing
the coefficients overall to be inflated above the value
they would have been if the diagnoses had been more
completely reported. Because Medicare’s risk model is

based on diagnostic-coding patterns in FFS, when MA
plans submit more diagnoses for a beneficiary than
would have been documented in FFS Medicare, the
program spends more for that beneficiary in MA than it
would have if the beneficiary were in FFS.
Because of the increased financial incentives for MA
plans to code more diagnoses and the additional
tools that MA plans use to capture diagnoses, coding
intensity is higher in MA than in FFS and payments to
MA plans are higher than intended. Although Medicare’s
accountable care organization (ACO) programs and
some other alternative payment models (APMs) offer
incentives to increase diagnostic-coding intensity in FFS
Medicare, we continue to see higher coding intensity
in MA. The tools that ACOs and APMs have available
result in less coding intensity than those available to MA
plans; notably, chart reviews, plan-initiated health risk
assessments, and subcapitation to medical groups are
used only in MA. Furthermore, CMS limits annual riskscore growth for ACO enrollees when calculating shared
savings or losses. Thus, we expect that FFS coding will
continue to identify fewer diagnosis codes than MA
coding does.
Higher MA payments due to coding differences have
been under scrutiny for more than a decade. Research
has consistently found that the impact of coding
differences on MA risk scores produces higher payments
for MA plans (Congressional Budget Office 2017, Geruso
and Layton 2020, Government Accountability Office
2013, Hayford and Burns 2018, Jacobs and Kronick 2018,
Kronick and Chua 2021, Kronick and Welch 2014). One
study found that when controlling for differences in
health status using Part D prescription drug data, from
2008 to 2015, MA risk scores grew by about 1 percent
more per year than FFS risk scores (Jacobs and Kronick
2018). A second study used a difference-in-difference
approach on risk-adjustment data for 2008 to 2013
to estimate that risk scores for enrollees remaining
in MA grew about 1.2 percent faster per year than for
beneficiaries in FFS Medicare (Hayford and Burns 2018).
A third study, using county-level data, found that in the
first year after MA enrollment, risk scores increased
about 6 percent faster than FFS and about 2 percent
faster in the second year (Geruso and Layton 2020).
The Government Accountability Office used a riskscore prediction model to estimate coding intensity
for 2010 through 2012, and those estimates align very

closely with the Commission’s estimates over that same
time period (Government Accountability Office 2013).
A study focusing on diagnoses submitted on hospital
claims has found higher levels of MA coding intensity for
beneficiaries who subsequently had a health assessment
in a skilled nursing facility (Kosar et al. 2024).
Several studies published in the past year have shown
evidence of coding intensity using new approaches
and data sources. The overall estimates of coding
intensity produced by these studies are all slightly
larger than MedPAC’s estimates of coding intensity.
One study controlled for mortality differences and the
differences in MA and FFS coding for specific HCCs
and found overall coding intensity of about 8.4 percent
in 2014 (Curto et al. 2025). Another study using risk
scores derived from encounter data and applying the
demographic estimate of coding intensity found coding
intensity of about 10.1 percent in 2016 rising to about 17.4
percent in 2021 (Kronick et al. 2025a). This study also
estimated that coding intensity under the V28 model
would have been about 7.3 percentage points lower than
actual coding intensity under the risk model used to
pay MA plans in 2021 and that removing the HCCs with
the largest MA and FFS coding differences could have
a significant effect on eliminating differences in overall
coding intensity. (There is more discussion of the V28
risk model and its effect on coding intensity later in this
section.) Another study analyzed switchers from FFS to
MA and found rapidly rising risk scores across cohorts of
MA entrants and an overall coding-intensity estimate of
18.6 percent for 2021 (Jacobs and Layton 2025).
Finally, one new study assessed the prevalence of certain
conditions using lab data and controlling for age and sex
differences and found that the prevalence of obesity,
hypertension, hyperlipidemia, and chronic kidney
disease was not higher among MA compared with FFS
beneficiaries; however, diabetes was more prevalent in
MA (Oseran et al. 2025). By reviewing medical records
and using lab data to validate the true prevalence of
these conditions, this study highlights the concern
over the diagnosis codes that MA plans submit for riskadjusting payments to plans. Plan-reported codes should
be supported by medical records (see text box “Update
on risk adjustment data validation audits,” pp. 402–403);
however, these conditions appear to be far more
prevalent in MA relative to FFS in risk-adjustment data
than they appear to be in the medical record findings

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

389

from the Oseran et al. study. One study looking at planreported conditions found obesity and Stage 3 chronic
kidney disease to be reported, respectively, 96 percent
and 56 percent more often in MA risk-adjustment
data than in FFS Medicare (Kronick et al. 2025a). We
previously found that angina pectoris (a symptom that is
often related to hypertension) was coded far more often
in MA (controlling for age, sex, and Medicaid eligibility)
and was coded 2.5 times more frequently than in FFS
among the MA contracts in the highest 20 percent of
contracts ranked by overall coding intensity (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2023a). CMS removed
angina pectoris from the CMS–HCC risk model due
to large differences in MA and FFS coding in the riskscore data (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services
2022a). Although the Oseran et al. study using medical
record review and lab data found a higher prevalence
of diabetes in MA, we previously found higher coding
intensity by level of diabetes severity reported in
MA risk-adjustment data. MA plans coded diabetes
without complications less frequently than FFS but
coded diabetes with chronic or acute complications
far more often, including MA contracts with the
highest overall coding intensity reporting diabetes with
acute complications two times more frequently than
demographically similar beneficiaries in FFS Medicare
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2023a).
Because of these differences in coding the severity of
diabetes between MA and FFS, CMS constrained the
risk-score coefficients for all three diabetes conditions
(without complications, with acute complication, or
with chronic complication) to be the same (Centers for
Medicare & Medicaid Services 2022a).
Starting in 2010, statute requires CMS to reduce MA
risk scores to address the impact of MA and FFS coding
differences on payments to MA plans.71 In response,
CMS reduced MA risk scores by 3.41 percent in each
year from 2010 through 2013. Starting in 2014, legislation
specified a minimum reduction of about 4.9 percent,
which rose gradually to about 5.9 percent in 2018,
where it will remain until the Secretary of Health and
Human Services implements risk adjustment using
MA diagnostic, cost, and use data (which is generally
thought to mean a risk model that has coefficients
estimated using MA encounter data rather than FFS
claims data). Although larger reductions are allowed
under the legislation, CMS reduced MA risk scores by
only the minimum amount required by law for 2014
through 2026.

390 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

CMS has made other changes to the risk-adjustment
model to better align FFS and MA risk scores. Between
2014 and 2016, CMS phased in a new risk-adjustment
model that removed some diagnoses found to be coded
more frequently in MA. We estimated that the new risk
model reduced overall coding intensity by about 2.5
percentage points compared with the previous model.
In 2017, CMS began accounting for Medicaid-benefit
eligibility more accurately (full-, partial-, or no-benefit
status by month), which reduced the gap in MA and FFS
risk scores by about 1 percentage point, which was the
amount by which MA risk scores were unduly higher
than FFS due to differing shares of beneficiaries by
Medicaid eligibility status. The largest reduction to MA
and FFS coding differences began in 2024 when CMS
initiated the phase-in of a new risk model (V28) that
reduces the gap in coding intensity relative to FFS by
removing or constraining the coefficients of diagnoses
that were found to be coded more aggressively in MA.
The V28 model will be fully phased in for payments
to MA plans in 2026; the effect on coding intensity is
discussed more below.
MedPAC’s estimates of coding intensity

The Commission’s method for estimating coding
intensity compares risk scores for MA and FFS
populations under the assumption that demographically
similar populations of beneficiaries have a similar
average prevalence of health conditions. We use the
combination of age, sex, Medicaid eligibility, and
institutional status to define demographic similarity. We
introduced this method in 2023. The previous method
used cohorts of MA and FFS enrollees matched by length
of enrollment, age, and sex to estimate coding intensity.
Over time, the cohort method became increasingly
complex to implement. After revising the cohort method
of estimating coding intensity to account for additional
beneficiary characteristics, the Commission found
that the new method produced substantially similar
estimates of plan coding intensity over the period 2007
through 2021. A detailed description of those methods
can be found in Chapter 13 of our March 2024 report
to the Congress, along with the research leading to
the revisions. For our 2025 report, we made additional
revisions to exclude beneficiaries residing in Puerto
Rico from our coding-intensity estimate because
Puerto Rico’s Medicaid program does not use the full-,
partial-, or no-benefit structure, which is necessary for
our coding-intensity estimation method. This revision

reduced our coding-intensity estimates by about 1
percentage point from what we previously reported. We
also modified how we weight new enrollees so that the
estimate more accurately reflects the share of payments
associated with new and continuing (those with risk
scores based on diagnosis codes) enrollees.
This year, we also estimate coding intensity for MA
enrollees with ESRD, and we incorporate that estimate
into our overall estimate of MA coding intensity. The
share of payments to MA plans for enrollees with
ESRD has grown rapidly since the enactment of the
21st Century Cures Act and is projected to account
for more than 6 percent of MA spending in 2026.
Given this trend, we expanded our estimate to include
beneficiaries with ESRD; however, the change had
little effect on our overall estimate of coding intensity.

Finally, based on comments from Commissioners and
others, we conducted a number of sensitivity tests
to assess adding variables to our demographic risk
model. Those sensitivity analyses had little effect on
our overall estimates of coding intensity. More details
on our method for estimating MA coding intensity, how
we estimate coding intensity for the ESRD population
including separate estimates for the ESRD and nonESRD populations, and sensitivity analyses can be found
in Technical Appendix 12-C, p. 440.
For 2024, the most recent year of risk-score data
available, we estimate that MA coding intensity fell
from 17 percent in 2023 to 14 percent due to the initial
phase-in of the V28 risk model (Figure 12-9). In addition
to removing HCCs or constraining the coefficients on
HCCs that had large differences in MA and FFS coding

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

391

Updates to coding-intensity estimates reflect newly available data and other
factors

W

e updated the coding-intensity estimates
published in last year’s March 2025 report
to incorporate newly available data from
2024. MedPAC updates its estimates each year to
reflect the most recent data available at the time of
publication. This year, the newly available data for
2024 were particularly consequential for updating
our earlier projections of coding intensity because
those data reflected the first year of experience with
the V28 risk-adjustment model. The newly available
data both allowed us to update the 2024 projection
using historical data and informed updates to the
methods used to project that 2024 estimate forward
to 2025 and 2026.
Compared with the 16 percent estimate of coding
intensity for 2025 that we published last year, we
now project coding intensity to be 12 percent in
2025 and 10 percent in 2026. The difference in the
estimates for 2025 is driven by three main factors,
explained in Figure 12-10.
First, we observed lower overall coding intensity
under V28 in 2024 relative to last year’s estimate.
Last year, we did not have access to V28 risk
scores, so we calculated V24 and V28 risk scores
using MA encounter and FFS claims data and
risk-model software and then estimated coding
intensity under the two models for 2021, 2022,
and 2023. The average difference across the three
years was 7.7 percentage points, which we assumed
to represent the baseline difference in coding
intensity for 2023 before the introduction of V28.
We then trended that difference forward to 2024,
assuming that coding intensity would grow faster

rates, the V28 model also revised the diagnosis codes
used to calibrate the model, which resulted in the
removal of several codes from the model.72 We estimate
that in 2024, coding intensity would have been 8.8

392 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

under V28 compared with V24 as MA organizations
and providers adapted to the new set of diagnoses
included in the V28 risk model. (We previously
observed a faster rate of coding-intensity growth
between 2014 and 2015 during the phase-in of
the V22 risk model, which, similar to the phase-in
of V28, also removed diagnoses with differing
rates of coding in MA and FFS.) As a result, we
projected a 6.9 percentage point difference in
coding intensity between the two models in 2024.
We now have V24 and V28 risk scores for 2024 and
calculate that coding intensity under V28 was 8.8
percentage points lower than under V24 in that
year. Therefore, the effect of incorporating actual
V28 risk scores accounts for 1.8 percentage points
(estimate is affected by rounding) of the difference
in coding-intensity estimates for 2025.
Second, we previously projected that coding
intensity would continue to grow based on the V24
trend from 2019 to 2023, or about 1.6 percentage
points per year. We now find that coding intensity
grew by 0.7 percentage points from 2023 to 2024
under the V24 model. Because we observed atypical
coding-intensity estimates during the coronavirus
pandemic, for 2022 and 2023, we now apply a trend
based on only postpandemic estimates, allowing for
the possibility that the coding-intensity trend has
fundamentally changed. In last year’s projection,
we applied the 1.6 percentage point annual trend
for two years, from 2023 to 2025. Therefore, the
difference in trend (0.9 percentage points annually)
accounts for 1.8 percentage points of the difference
between coding-intensity estimates for 2025 from
our March 2025 report and this report.

Finally, we modified our method of calibrating the
demographic risk model used in the demographic
estimate of coding-intensity method to better align
with how the CMS–HCC model is calibrated. The
demographic model now uses a single denominator
to calculate coefficients for differing groups of
beneficiaries based on their Medicaid eligibility and
institutional status. The change from using separate
denominators for these groups to using a single
denominator reduced our estimate of overall coding
intensity for 2024 by 0.3 percentage points. We
expect this effect will be similar in 2025.

For 2026, we project that coding intensity will be
about 10 percent. Projecting from a 12 percent
coding-intensity estimate in 2025 to 10 percent in
2026 reflects the combined effect of completing the
phase-in of the V28 risk model from two-thirds for
2025 to fully phased in for 2026 (–2.9 percentage
points) and an additional year of coding-intensity
growth (+0.7 percentage points) based on the
postpandemic trend for the V24 risk model. Next
year, we will have access to a second year of V28 risk
scores and can estimate a new trend based on that
model. ■

Because the data required to estimate coding intensity
are not yet available for 2025 and 2026, we project
coding intensity for those years by incorporating the
annual trend from 2023 to 2024 for the V24 risk model,
an increase of 0.7 percentage points per year, and
accounting for the effect of the continued phase-in
of the V28 risk model (one-third in 2024, two-thirds
in 2025, and fully phased in for 2026). (See text
box “Updates to coding-intensity estimates reflect
newly available data and other factors,” pp. 392–393.)
Based on the phase-in schedule, we project that V28
alone reduced coding intensity by an additional 2.9
percentage points in each of these years. Combining

394 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

the effect of the annual coding-intensity trend with
the V28 model phase-in, we project that coding
intensity will continue to decline to about 12 percent
in 2025 and to about 10 percent in 2026. Given that
we have V28 risk-score data only for 2024, there
is uncertainty about the impact of the continued
phase-in of the V28 model for MA coding intensity. In
particular, the V28 projection factor does not explicitly
account for plans responding to the new coding
incentives under the V28 model and actual trends
under the V28 model may be higher or lower than we
estimate. We will continue to monitor those effects
and will update our analysis as we receive additional
data.
For 2026, the projection of MA coding intensity of 10
percent is partially offset by CMS’s coding-intensity
adjustment, which reduced MA risk scores by

5.9 percent. The net effect is a 4 percent increase in
MA risk scores due to coding intensity, leading to $22
billion in higher projected payments to MA plans.
Between 2007 and 2024, MA coding intensity resulted
in $184 billion in increased payments to MA plans
(Figure 12-11). Using our projection, we estimate that
MA coding intensity in 2025 and 2026 will increase
program spending by another $28 billion and $22
billion, respectively (data not shown). In total, we
estimate that between 2007 and 2026, MA coding
intensity will have generated $233 billion in higher
aggregate payments to MA plans.
Documenting additional diagnosis codes
increases plan rebates and can distort
competition among plans

Documenting additional diagnostic codes increases
the size of MA plans’ rebates, which in turn allows
plans to offer their enrollees more supplemental
benefits than plans that document fewer additional
diagnoses. For a plan submitting a bid below its
benchmark (nearly all plans in 2026), the plan’s rebate
is based on the difference between the plan’s bid
for its expected enrollee population and the plan’s
risk-adjusted benchmark, which is the standard
benchmark (for a beneficiary of average risk, with
a 1.0 risk score) multiplied by the plan’s expected
average risk score. Raising a plan’s average risk score
raises the plan’s risk-adjusted benchmark and widens
the difference between the plan’s bid and the riskadjusted benchmark, thereby increasing the plan’s
rebate amount and ability to offer more supplemental
benefits. Plans may also adjust their bids in response
to higher benchmarks. In sum, plans can translate
greater coding intensity into the ability to offer more
supplemental benefits, giving them an advantage over
their competitors in attracting enrollees.
MA payment policies aim to give plans an incentive
to lower spending and improve quality by allowing
them to offer more supplemental benefits. By
reducing health care costs, plans can reduce their
bids, increasing their rebate and supplemental benefit
value. By improving quality scores, plans can be
rewarded with a 5 percent or 10 percent increase in
their benchmark or with an increase in the rebate
percentage (the percentage of the bid and benchmark
difference that determines the rebate amount).73 These
policies are intended to benefit enrollees through
improved quality, more supplemental benefits, and

reduced premiums, as well as lower taxpayer funding
for the Medicare program. Greater MA coding intensity,
however, distorts these incentives by allowing plans
to offer more supplemental benefits regardless of
whether they reduce costs or improve quality.
Table 12-7 (p. 396) illustrates the relationship
between coding intensity and rebate amounts using a
hypothetical example of three plans covering the same
set of enrollees for whom the expected cost of care
is the same, at $900 per member per month. Plan A
and Plan Z have an expected risk score of 0.97, while
Plan B has an expected risk score of 1.03 due to coding
more diagnoses. All three plans have bids below the
risk-adjusted benchmark and provide supplemental
benefits funded by rebates. However, because Plan B
has a higher risk score, its rebate is larger than Plan A’s
rebate ($52 per month vs. $15 per month), so it can offer
enrollees more supplemental benefits. Plan B’s coding
efforts have therefore given it an unfair competitive
advantage over Plan A.
In addition, increased coding intensity can influence
the size of the rebate more than MA quality bonuses
can. The higher risk score of Plan B, which has only 3.5
stars, gives it an advantage over bonus-level Plan Z,
which has 5 stars: Plan B’s rebate amount is higher than
Plan Z’s ($52 per month vs. $49 per month). Thus, by
inflating its risk score from 0.97 to 1.03, Plan B can offer
more supplemental benefits than are provided through
quality bonuses.
The plans illustrated in Table 12-7 (p. 396) have a riskscore difference of 6 percentage points, reflecting
different coding practices. We estimated coding
intensity for MA organizations and found much greater
variation in coding for 2024.74 Figure 12-12 (p. 397)
shows MA coding intensity relative to FFS coding,
broken out by MA parent organization, excluding
beneficiaries who reside in Puerto Rico, are enrolled in
chronic-condition special-needs plans, or have ESRD,
and organizations with fewer than 25,000 enrollees in
the analysis.
Consistent with prior years, we find that about
half of organizations (covering 16 percent of MA
enrollees) have coding intensity below CMS’s 2024
coding adjustment and are thereby penalized by the
adjustment, while the other half of organizations
(covering 84 percent of MA enrollees) have coding
intensity that increases their payment even after

across contracts by reducing net revenue for plans
