We also find significant variation in coding intensity
across the 10 largest MA organizations (covering 81
percent of MA enrollees), from about 8 percent below
to about 20 percent above FFS levels. Eight of the 10
largest MA organizations had greater coding intensity
than the 2024 coding adjustment and therefore
received a net increase in payment due to their coding
practices. These differences are large enough to
give MA organizations with higher coding intensity a
significant competitive advantage by increasing the
size of plan rebates and helping them to attract more
enrollees. Our finding that coding intensity varies
across MA organizations is consistent with other
research (Curto et al. 2025, Geruso and Layton 2020,
Kronick and Chua 2021, Kronick et al. 2025b, Kronick
and Welch 2014).

As noted earlier, we found that in 2024, coding
intensity under the V28 model was 8.8 percentage
points lower than under the V24 model, on
average. We also estimated the difference between
coding intensity under the two models across
MA organizations. Given the difference in coding
intensity across organizations, for the V28 model to
be an effective and fair approach to reducing coding
intensity, it should reduce coding intensity consistent
with an organization’s overall coding level. Table
12-8 (p. 398) shows that in 2024, the V28 risk model
reduced coding intensity more for organizations with
larger V24 coding intensity. The effects are smaller
for organizations with lower levels of coding intensity,
particularly those with coding levels below that of FFS,
suggesting that the effects of V28 on coding intensity
were well-targeted to the organizations with the
highest levels of coding intensity.

as major sources of plan revenue from coding intensity.
Coordination of coding incentives through payfor-coding programs, capitated arrangements, and
corporate ownership Some plans try to ensure that

providers submit all possible diagnoses for their
enrollees through pay-for-coding programs, in which
plans send physicians a patient-assessment form that
includes diagnosis codes that the plan has identified
for a beneficiary. Plans ask physicians to confirm the
existence of plan-identified diagnoses on the form
and document those diagnoses on subsequent claims.
Plans pay physicians based on completing the form
or as a dollar amount per diagnosis code submitted.
The corporate ownership of physician groups by
MA organizations seems to intensify such programs.
One organization is reported to have a dashboard
comparing chronic conditions identified for MA

398 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

enrollees across physician groups owned by that
company and to offer bonuses as an incentive.76
Corporate ownership of physician practices by MA
organizations or other companies focusing on riskbased payment arrangements with MA organizations
can be an effective way to align an MA plan’s coding
incentives with the providers who have access to
patient medical records and are responsible for coding
diagnoses. Under many capitated arrangements, a
portion of the increase in a plan’s revenue from more
diagnoses being documented is passed on through the
capitated payments to providers (often determined as a
percentage of the plan’s revenue).
Our prior analysis has found that a disproportionate
share of the organizations with the highest levels of
diagnostic coding are located in California and Florida
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2025b).
We also found a higher use of capitated payments to
providers among these organizations and suggested
that capitated arrangements may have contributed
to higher coding-intensity estimates. A new working
paper assessed the effect of corporate primary care
practices (CPCPs) by tracking risk scores before and

MA plans’ use of health risk assessments to increase
diagnostic coding Health risk assessments are

provided to all Medicare beneficiaries as part of an
annual wellness visit, and, for MA enrollees, health
risk assessments are often provided during a planinitiated home visit.77 Health risk assessments may
be part of a plan’s care-management approach and
should continue to be used for that purpose when
they are used to improve patient care. However, when
health risk assessments identify diagnosis codes
that are not documented on subsequent encounters
with providers, which would demonstrate that the

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

399

condition was not under active management, those
codes should not be used for risk adjustment when
calculating payments to plans. Health risk assessments
sometimes rely on patient self-reporting of medical
conditions, which may result in HCCs based on
inaccurate diagnoses, diagnoses that are no longer
active (and therefore not eligible for risk adjustment),
or diagnoses without sufficient evidence to conform
to International Classification of Diseases coding
guidelines (Department of Justice 2022). (More detailed
information can be found in our March 2023 report
chapter on Medicare Advantage.)
We analyzed 2022 encounter records to identify
HCCs that were supported only by a health risk
assessment, meaning that there was no physician or
hospital service provided to treat a beneficiary for a
specific health condition during the same calendar
year.78 In 2022, about 7.7 million MA enrollees had a
health risk assessment that identified at least one
HCC and a total of 17.1 million unique HCCs were
identified through health risk assessments. Of those,
3.5 million beneficiaries had a health risk assessment
that was the only source for at least one of the HCCs
identified, and a total of 5.7 million HCCs (one-third of
all HCCs identified on health risk assessments) were
identified only on a health risk assessment. Six HCCs
each generated more than $900 million in payments
from these assessments, accounting for about half of
all payments generated by codes found only on health
risk assessments.79 We found that diagnostic coding
that was associated with only health risk assessments
accounted for $15 billion in payments to MA plans in
2023, or a little more than 3 percent of all payments
to MA plans. About 80 percent of these payments
were from health risk assessments conducted as
part of an annual wellness visit or initial preventive
physical examination, while the rest of these payments
were from in-home health risk assessments. Other
researchers found similar estimates of the impact of
health risk assessments, contributing about $12 billion
in payments to MA plans in 2020 (James et al. 2024).
That research also found differences in the use of
health risk assessments across parent organizations.
MA plans’ use of chart reviews to increase diagnosis
coding Some MA plans devote significant effort to

conducting chart reviews to increase MA payments.
Because chart reviews are not used in FFS Medicare, all
diagnoses newly documented through chart reviews

400 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

contribute to differences in FFS and MA diagnostic
coding and contribute to increased payments to
MA plans. Chart reviews that are allowable for risk
adjustment document the diagnoses made during
hospital and physician encounters in which medical
services were provided. MA plans use chart reviews
to identify diagnoses not captured through the usual
means of reporting diagnoses (e.g., claims data and
encounter data): diagnoses that are not reported on
the provider’s claim sent to the MA plan, diagnoses
made during an encounter in which the MA plan does
not submit a record of the encounter to CMS, or
diagnoses made during an encounter in which the total
number of diagnoses from that encounter exceeds the
number of diagnosis fields on the encounter record.
Because Medicare requires each HCC to be supported
by diagnostic evidence in a patient’s medical record
(chart), chart reviews are one way for plans to identify
diagnoses not captured through provider claims or in
plan encounter data.
Like health risk assessments, some MA plans treat
chart-review programs as an independent revenue
stream that yields a positive return on investment
because the additional Medicare payments from
newly documented diagnoses far exceed the costs of
paying nurses and medical assistants to review medical
charts.80 Several lawsuits allege that MA plans use
chart reviews to identify new diagnosis codes but not
to verify the accuracy of already submitted codes.
Some lawsuits allege that an MA organization is aware
that diagnoses submitted to CMS are not supported by
the medical chart and therefore violate Medicare’s rules
governing the reporting of diagnoses (United States
of America ex rel. Benjamin Poehling v. UnitedHealth
Group Inc. et al. 2016, United States of America ex
rel. James M. Swoben v. Secure Horizons 2017, United
States of America v. Anthem Inc. 2020). Some plans
and vendors appear to selectively review charts with
a higher likelihood of increasing revenue and use
artificial intelligence to more accurately identify likely
revenue-producing charts (Blue Health Intelligence
2020, Optum 2020). When chart reviews identify
diagnoses that are not documented on subsequent
encounters with providers, they increase risk scores of
MA enrollees and the financial burden of the program
for taxpayers and beneficiaries.
We analyzed 2022 encounter records to identify HCCs
that were supported by a chart review but not through

We estimate that chart reviews and health risk

payments to MA plans in 2023.82 Combined with our
finding that all sources of coding intensity resulted
in MA risk scores that were about 17 percent higher
than risk scores for comparable FFS beneficiaries in
2023, we conclude that health risk assessments and
chart reviews together accounted for about half of all
MA coding intensity (Figure 12-14). Our estimates are
similar to the results of another published analysis in
terms of the overall magnitude of the impact and of the
trend in the growing impact of health risk assessments
and chart reviews on payments to MA plans from 2016
to 2021 (Jacobs 2024).

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

401

Update on risk-adjustment data-validation audits

M

edicare payments to Medicare Advantage
(MA) plans are based, in part, on diagnostic
data that plans submit to CMS. Program
rules state that, to be used for payment, diagnoses
submitted for risk adjustment must result from a
hospital inpatient stay, hospital outpatient visit, or
face-to-face visit with a physician or other health
care professional; diagnoses also must be supported
by evidence in the patient’s medical record. MA
organization leadership signs an attestation stating
that the submitted diagnostic data are accurate.
CMS conducts risk-adjustment data-validation
(RADV) audits after payments have been made
to the plan to check whether plan-submitted
diagnoses are supported by the medical record
as required by Medicare. If diagnoses do not
meet requirements, plans are required to return
payments to Medicare. These overpayments for
diagnoses that do not meet program requirements
are different in concept from payments for
higher MA coding intensity. Diagnoses that are
documented for MA enrollees that would have not
been documented FFS beneficiaries contribute
to differences in coding intensity, but those
diagnoses may or may not have been documented
appropriately in the medical record and therefore
may or may not be considered “overpayments” in
the RADV audit context. Similarly, diagnoses that
are not appropriately supported by the medical
record contribute to RADV overpayments, but such
diagnoses may have been documented for risk
adjustment equally in FFS and MA and therefore do
not contribute to differences in coding intensity.
However, there is an unknown amount of overlap
between overpayments for RADV audits and
payments to plans for higher MA coding intensity.
For RADV audits of payment years through 2017,
CMS has been using a protocol that audits roughly 5
percent of MA contracts per year; uses a sample of
201 enrollees in each contract across three strata of

beneficiaries with low, medium, and high risk scores;
calculates an error rate for the sample population;
and then calculates the overpayment amount for the
sample of audited beneficiaries. The majority of MA
enrollees would be eligible for audit since nearly 70
percent of MA enrollees had at least one hierarchical
condition category (HCC) in 2022, and only a small
fraction of these enrollees would be excluded from
the audit sample for other reasons.83 RADV audits of
MA contracts during this period have been limited,
and their results are largely unreported. Audits
of 2007 risk-adjustment data identified diagnoses
that did not meet risk-adjustment criteria and
determined that average overpayment rates were
well over 10 percent for most contracts under
audit (Schulte 2016). CMS recovered $13.7 million
in overpayments from audits of 37 contracts, based
on overpayments for only the 7,437 beneficiaries
included in the audit sample (Centers for Medicare &
Medicaid Services 2017a). No audits were conducted
for payment years 2008, 2009, or 2010. Kaiser Health
News obtained, through a Freedom of Information
Act request, summaries of the preliminary results for
90 audits completed during 2011, 2012, and 2013 and
found that 71 audits uncovered net overpayments,
with 23 audits finding overpayments of $1,000 or
more per beneficiary (Schulte and Hacker 2022).
For audits of 2018 and subsequent years, the scope
of RADV audits has changed in significant ways and
is under review in the legal system.
• First, in the final rule published February 1, 2023,
CMS outlined an audit method that would allow
CMS to recover overpayments by extrapolating
the error rate of the sampled enrollees to a
larger population of audit-eligible enrollees in
the contract (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid
Services 2023c). The final rule also eliminated the
use of extrapolation for RADV audits of 2017 and
earlier, thereby limiting overpayment recovery to
the audit sample only.

(continued next page)

402 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

Update on risk-adjustment data-validation audits
• In 2024 and 2025, CMS released audit methods
(and updates to those methods) for payment
years 2018 and 2019 that restrict the auditeligible population to a much narrower set of
enrollees in a given contract compared with
audit-eligible populations for audits of 2017 and
earlier. Audits of 2018 data will focus on only two
sets of beneficiaries, or two “sampling frames”:
beneficiaries in the top decile of a contract’s
enrollees based on the greatest expected
reduction in their risk score as a result of a RADV
audit (the expected reduction in risk score due
to an audit is based on a CMS improper-payment
prediction model) or contract enrollees who had
all of their HCCs supported only by chart reviews
(Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services
2024d). The first sampling frame includes roughly
one-tenth of the beneficiaries who would have
been audit eligible under the pre-2018 methods.
For the second sampling frame, we estimate
that between 2 percent and 3 percent of all MA
enrollees (or between 3 percent and 4 percent
of MA enrollees with at least one HCC) had all
of their HCCs supported by only chart reviews,
and those enrollees had an average of 1.5 HCCs.
Audits for 2019 will focus only on the first
sampling frame, beneficiaries in the top decile
of a contract’s enrollees based on the greatest
expected reduction in the risk score as a result of
a RADV audit (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid
Services 2025j).
• In May 2025, CMS issued a press release stating
that the agency will conduct RADV audits of all
contracts and will expand the size of the audit
sample for each contract (Centers for Medicare
& Medicaid Services 2025b). At the time of the
press release, the agency had already identified
58 contracts under audit for 2018 (Centers for
Medicare & Medicaid Services 2025h). For 2019,
the agency identified 355 contracts for RADV
audits, which appears to align with the May 2025
press release of auditing all contracts with a

sufficient audit sample (Centers for Medicare &
Medicaid Services 2025i). In the press release, the
agency also announced additional resources to
facilitate the expansion in audit scope, including
additional medical coding staff and the use of
enhanced technology.
• In the final rule establishing regulations for RADV
audits, CMS took the position that the agency
would not use a “FFS adjuster” when calculating
overpayment amounts in the audits (Centers for
Medicare & Medicaid Services 2023c, Centers
for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2018a). A FFS
adjuster was announced in a 2012 RADV audit
methods document as an offset to account for the
difference in documentation standards for FFS
claims used to develop the risk model (Centers for
Medicare & Medicaid Services 2012).
• After publication of the final rule, Humana sued,
claiming that CMS violated the Administrative
Procedure Act because of the changing
justification between the proposed and final rules
regarding the FFS adjuster. In September 2025, the
court vacated the final rule on the grounds that
CMS violated the Administrative Procedure Act
generally because the proposed rule justification
that FFS adjusters introduce inequities does not
appear in the final rule and could not have been
a contributing factor for the new justification
and that there was no meaningful dialogue in
the final rule regarding the costs and benefits of
the policy changes after MA organizations had
relied on a previous rule’s guidance from 2018
to 2023 (U.S. District Court for the Northern
District of Texas 2025). CMS has not yet issued a
new proposed rule to correct any Administrative
Procedure Act deficits for this policy and has filed
a notice of appeal of the judgment. Under the
current circumstances, the scope of overpayment
recovery from RADV audits under the final rule
remains uncertain. ■

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

403

The Commission’s 2016 recommendation on
coding intensity
In our March 2016 report to the Congress, the
Commission recommended a multipronged approach
that would fully account for the impact of coding
differences, improve the equity of the adjustment
across MA contracts, and increase incentives to reduce
costs and improve quality (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2016c). The Commission’s approach to
reduce MA coding intensity has been to address the
mechanisms that generate coding differences first (e.g.,
remove health risk assessments and reduce year-toyear coding variations by using two years of diagnostic
data) and then address remaining differences with
either an across-the-board or tiered adjustment. The
Commission’s 2016 recommendation did not address
the use of chart reviews because data were not available
at that time, but eliminating chart reviews as a source
of diagnoses for risk adjustment is consistent with the
Commission’s approach.84
The recommendation, which would replace the existing
mandatory minimum coding-intensity adjustment
(which has reduced MA risk scores by 5.9 percent since
2018), has three parts:
•

develop a risk-adjustment model that uses two years
of FFS and MA diagnostic data,

•

exclude diagnoses that are documented only on
health risk assessments from either FFS or MA, and
then

•

apply a coding adjustment that fully accounts for
the remaining differences in coding between FFS
Medicare and MA plans.

Implementing the first two policies—using two years of
diagnostic data and excluding diagnoses documented
through health risk assessments alone—and excluding
chart-review data from risk adjustment (consistent with
the Commission’s approach) would result in a more
equitable, targeted adjustment to MA contracts than the
current across-the-board adjustment. As noted earlier,
health risk assessments and chart reviews alone account
for roughly half of estimated MA coding intensity. The
Commission carefully considered options for addressing
coding intensity and supports this approach because
it balances implementation feasibility, administrative
burden, and effectiveness.

404 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

Part of the cause of coding intensity is that providers
do not report all possible diagnosis codes for their
FFS beneficiaries. We note that using two years of
diagnostic data would help address the underreporting
of FFS beneficiaries’ chronic conditions by helping to
capture conditions that are not reported consistently
year to year.
Adjusting for any remaining coding-intensity differences
could also improve equity across MA contracts. Under
one illustrative approach, contracts would be grouped
into tiers of high, medium, and low coding intensity, and
a coding-intensity adjustment would be applied based
on each tier’s average level of coding intensity (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2016a). CMS has used
a similar approach to select MA contracts for RADV
audits.85 This policy would improve the overall equity of
the coding-intensity adjustment relative to the single,
across-the-board adjustment used today.

Industry concentration and integration
Each year, we assess the structure of the MA market,
including trends in the concentration of enrollment
and the integration of MA organizations with other
entities in the health care sector.86 In 2025, the MA
program included 5,492 plan options offered by 164
organizations. As noted above, on average in 2026,
beneficiaries have 39 plan options to choose from,
offered by eight organizations. However, enrollment
is highly concentrated at the local level: The largest
organization in a county typically enrolls between 40
percent and 50 percent of the market. Enrollment is
also increasingly concentrated at the national level;
just three organizations enrolled more than half of
all MA enrollees nationally in 2025. Additionally, MA
organizations are increasingly integrating vertically, with
provider and insurer lines of business having common
ownership (or other financially aligned arrangements).
Vertical integration is greatest among provider-owned
MA plans—typically offered by large health systems—
though insurer ownership of providers has increased
significantly in recent years.

MA market was heavily concentrated
locally and nationally in 2025
Enrollment in MA is highly concentrated at the local
level and increasingly concentrated at the national

level. High enrollment concentration—particularly at
the local level—can be a cause for concern if it dampens
the competitive pressures that might otherwise drive
insurers to maintain or improve quality, make care
delivery more efficient, lower premiums, or provide
supplemental benefits. Researchers have shown
that markets with less concentration are associated
with increased MA benefit generosity and lower
MA premiums (Cabral et al. 2018, Pelech 2018, Pizer
and Frakt 2002, Song et al. 2013). In extreme cases,
dominance by a single firm (or small set of firms) may
make it difficult for competitors to enter or remain
active in a market (Frakt et al. 2012, Pelech 2017).87
Over the last decade, enrollment in MA has become
increasingly concentrated at the national level in plans
owned by a small set of large insurers that serve a
majority of markets in the country. Between 2008 and
2025, the share of total MA enrollment in the three
largest firms (UnitedHealth Group, Humana, and CVS
Health) rose from 32 percent to 58 percent. Much of
the growth of these firms—particularly UnitedHealth
Group and Humana—has been driven by enrollment
increases in counties in which the insurers have
offered plans for many years (Hnath et al. 2024).
However, all three have also significantly expanded
the number of counties in which they offer plans. For
example, UnitedHealth Group expanded from offering
coordinated-care plans (i.e., HMO or PPO plans) in 41
percent of counties (a service area covering 68 percent
of MA-eligible beneficiaries) in 2013, to 54 percent of
counties (covering 81 percent of eligible enrollees) in
2020, to 88 percent of counties (covering 96 percent of
eligible enrollees) in 2025 (Table 12-9, p. 406).88 Humana
and CVS Health have also expanded their service areas,
and all three organizations now offer plans in counties
that are home to more than 85 percent of MA-eligible
beneficiaries. A recent study found that some of the
service-area expansions for these three insurers were
due to the companies acquiring existing MA contracts
from another insurer (Hnath et al. 2024).89 For example,
acquisitions accounted for 30 percent of new county
participation for UnitedHealth Group between 2012
and 2023 (acquisitions accounted for 16 percent and 5
percent of new county participation for CVS Health and
Humana, respectively) (Hnath et al. 2024). However, the
study found that nationally, for all MA insurers, only 15
percent of total market-entry events were attributable
to acquisitions. These findings suggest that the servicearea expansions of large national insurers have (at the

time of market entry) generally served to introduce
new competitors into local markets and are not
simply an artifact of changes in ownership of existing
competitors.
Between 2024 and 2025, the share of enrollees covered
by these top three organizations fell by 1 percentage
point to 58 percent (but remained 2 percentage points
higher than in 2021) (Table 12-9, p. 406).90 Among
conventional plans (i.e., plans available to all Medicare
beneficiaries, excluding SNPs and employer group
plans), the top three organizations nationwide had 54
percent of enrollment in 2025—a decrease from 57
percent in 2024.91
Given the relevance of local competition for MA
enrollees, we place greater importance on examining
competition at the county level (Table 12-9, p. 406).
Measures of local-market concentration suggest
that concentration remained near 2024 levels in
2025. Excluding employer plans and SNPs, in 2025,
enrollment in the largest organization in each county
(regardless of the insurers’ national enrollment)
accounted for 43 percent, on average, of all MA
enrollment in the county. Enrollment in the top-three
organizations in each county accounted for 82 percent,
on average, of all MA enrollment (unchanged from
2021 but slightly higher than the 81 percent observed
in 2024). The share of MA enrollees living in counties
with highly concentrated markets (as measured using
the Herfindahl–Hirschman Index (HHI), a common
measure of market concentration) remained stable
at 95 percent (data not shown).92 The geographic
expansion of large national insurers has contributed to
the changing concentration in local markets.
Overall, local MA markets tend to be highly
concentrated, although the level of concentration has
trended downward in recent years. When measured
using the HHI, average county-level enrollment
concentration has fallen over the last decade, despite
the rising share of enrollees covered by the three
largest firms nationally. This trend coincides with
insurers entering new markets and steadily gaining
market share in areas that have historically been
very concentrated. Estimates for 2026 indicate
that the average beneficiary will have access to
many MA plans offered by a substantial number of
organizations, as illustrated earlier in this chapter.
However, large national insurers, and some regional

Vertical integration of MA plans and
providers
MA organizations are increasingly integrating
vertically, with provider and insurer lines of business
having common ownership (or other financially

aligned arrangements). Some have hypothesized
that MA organizations may view vertical integration
as a means of more directly promoting care that is
more efficient, higher quality, and more clinically
integrated. Although several features of MA payment
may reward firms that vertically integrate insurer and
provider businesses, incentives for integration are not
confined to Medicare and are influenced by trends in
other sectors of the health care system, as well as by
the actions of regulatory agencies—particularly the
Department of Justice and Federal Trade Commission.
In this section, we assess integration as it pertains to
MA and discuss elements of MA payment policy that
may incentivize integration.
The Commission has previously found that vertical
integration is highest in organizations in which a
provider-based organization owns and operates a
health plan, although the share of enrollees in such
vertically integrated plans has decreased over time
(Chartis Group 2024, Johnson et al. 2017, Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2024c). However,
insurer-led integration has accelerated in recent years,
and several of the largest MA organizations (MAOs)
have invested significantly in the acquisition of provider
businesses, suggesting that insurers see advantages
to owning a greater share of the health care delivery
system (CVS Health 2023, Humana 2022, Humana
2021, Humana 2020, Signify Health 2023, UnitedHealth
Group 2023, UnitedHealth Group 2022).
MA payment policy, though not the only factor
influencing firms’ decisions to integrate, may promote
such arrangements by incentivizing efficient care
delivery, rewarding plans that record diagnoses more
thoroughly, and providing bonuses to plans that perform
well on quality measures (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2024c). Although the evidence regarding
the relationship between plan–provider integration
and efficiency, coding intensity, and quality is limited,
MAOs may view integration as a way to influence
providers’ activities more directly to achieve the
outcomes incentivized under MA’s capitated payment
structure. For example, as described in the section on
coding intensity (p. 398), a recent working paper that
assessed the effect of corporate ownership of primary
care practices on MA risk scores found that—for some
insurer–provider combinations—the risk scores of MA
enrollees increased substantially in the years after their
PCP joined or was acquired by the vertically integrated

corporate entity (Meyers et al. 2025). Researchers
have also suggested that payments to plan-owned
providers may offer an opportunity for MAOs to retain
a higher share of profits within the parent organization
while meeting MLR requirements (Frank and Milhaupt
2023, Frank and Milhaupt 2022). Empirical evidence
on this effect is limited; one recent analysis of a small
nonrepresentative sample of practices found that
UnitedHealthcare’s payments to providers owned by
Optum (which is owned by the same parent company,
UnitedHealth Group) were higher than prices paid
to non-Optum providers (Arnold and Fulton 2025).
A different analysis, using data on prices paid in the
commercial sector, also found that corporate-owned
primary-care practices were paid higher rates than
independent practices (Wang et al. 2025). While these
findings provide some information, additional research
is needed to better understand if or how vertical
integration of insurers and providers affects the prices
insurers pay for services.
No public data provide a systematic accounting of
ownership relationships between MA plans and health
care providers, which poses a significant barrier to
studying the effects of vertical integration. Some
researchers have endeavored to create their own
datasets, focusing on particular types of providers.
For example, a recent paper used information from
corporate financial filings, merger and acquisition
databases, and insurer directories to identify medical
groups operated by large MA insurers; the study
estimated that the insurer-owned share of the primary
care market (measured by volume) grew from less
than 1 percent in 2016 to over 4 percent in 2023 (Adler
et al. 2025). The study found that Optum, owned by
UnitedHealth Group, was the largest of the entities—
accounting for more than 2 percent of the market
nationally but more than 35 percent in some counties.
CMS requires MAOs to submit limited information
about the extent of their financial relationships with
providers and other entities as part of the bidding
process. Specifically, plans submitting bids are required
to report the amount (including medical costs and
nonbenefit expenses) per member per month that
they expect their members to receive from a related
party.93 While the submitted data are projections and
not a report of actual utilization in a completed year,
they provide insight as to the MAO’s own assessment
of its integration with providers (or other entities)

that deliver services covered by the plan. Such entities
include providers of medical benefits (e.g., doctors and

supplemental benefits. The Commission has previously
found that MAOs often administer supplemental
benefits through vertically integrated arrangements
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2025a). For

own or operate businesses that administer OTC cards
and flex cards that are often used to provide MA

408 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

Figure 12-15 illustrates that the degree of vertical
integration in MA varies widely across parent
organizations and is highest in provider-owned
plans. Among these plans, the share is highest in
plans owned by health systems (data not shown). The
data show that large national insurers, on average,
remain significantly less vertically integrated than
their provider-owned competitors. However, at least
one large national insurer is now significantly more
vertically integrated than many provider-owned plans.
This finding is particularly noteworthy given that large
national organizations insure a significant share of MA

enrollees nationwide, so trends in the organization
of the businesses can affect millions of beneficiaries.
While the information presented here is reported at
the parent-organization level, health care markets
operate primarily at a local level, and national statistics
do not necessarily describe the markets in which most
beneficiaries live.
Altogether, we find that the MA industry has become
more vertically integrated over the last five years. The
Commission plans to continue monitoring trends in
integration in MA and evaluating its effects on enrollees
and the function of the program.

Quality in MA
The Commission has long held that MA presents
opportunities for innovation to achieve higher-quality
care at lower cost. But the current system for MA
quality reporting and measurement is flawed and does
not provide a reliable basis for evaluating quality across
MA plans (Medicare Payment Advisory Commission
2020a, Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2019).
It is important for the Medicare program to monitor
MA plan performance and promote higher quality (1) to
ensure that beneficiaries have access to high-quality
health care and (2) to provide good information for
beneficiaries about the quality of and access to care
provided by MA plans in their local market. While the
Commission has determined that current metrics
do not adequately reflect the quality of care that MA
enrollees receive, they are the basis for the MA qualitybonus program (QBP), which is expected to increase
MA payments by about $16 billion in 2026.

Quality-bonus program and star-rating
system provide limited insight into MA
quality
Quality measurement and rewards can promote
better plan quality and support beneficiary choice
of plans. The MA star-rating system, implemented in
2006, is composed of measures tied to clinical quality,
administrative capability, and patient experience.94
These ratings are made available through the Medicare
Plan Finder website to enable beneficiaries to compare
plans. As required by the ACA, since 2012 the MA starrating system has been the basis of the QBP, which
increases benchmarks for MA contracts that are rated

4 stars or higher.95 The star rating also contributes to
the level of rebate payments. Plans with higher star
ratings retain a higher share of the difference between
a plan bid and the benchmark when bids are below
the benchmark. Beneficiaries enrolled in an MA plan
with less than 5 stars can use a once-a-year special
enrollment period to switch to a 5-star plan outside of
the open enrollment period.
The share of MA contracts receiving quality bonuses
has been declining in recent years but remains
consistently high. Forty percent of rated MA contracts
are in bonus status for 2026, a decrease from 41 percent
in 2025 and 44 percent in 2024 (Centers for Medicare
& Medicaid Services 2024a). Sixty-four percent of MA
enrollees are projected to be enrolled in bonus-status
contracts in 2026, compared with 64 percent in 2025
and 76 percent in 2024. The average star rating was
3.98 in 2026, a slight increase from 3.95 in 2025.
The Commission has discussed several flaws of the
current approach to quality assessment in previous
reports (Medicare Payment Advisory Commission
2024b, Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2023b,
Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2020a,
Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2020b,
Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2016b). In
brief, the Commission has identified issues with:
•

The measure set used to assess quality. An excessive
number of measures are assessed, and many
administrative and process measures are not
reflective of the quality of care and/or experience
that plan enrollees can expect to receive. Medicare
currently collects close to 100 MA quality
measures, over 40 of which are used to determine a
star rating from 1 to 5 for each MA contract.96,97

•

Reliance on MA contract-level reporting. Measures
are currently reported at the contract, rather than
plan, level. Many contracts cover plans operating
in wide geographic areas, some of which are
not contiguous, resulting in scores that do not
necessarily reflect performance in a local area. The
ability of MAOs to consolidate plans into a single
contract also presents an opportunity to increase
the odds of achieving bonus status without
meaningfully improving quality.

•

Methods for comparing performance across plans/
contracts. The way that performance is compared
for purposes of the QBP can mask some true

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

409

differences in quality and amplify minor differences
in other areas. For instance, contracts can
aggregate plans that differ on features that are
associated with quality. They may include plans
across wide and noncontiguous geographic areas
or bundle SNPs with conventional HMOs. On the
other hand, the thresholds for scoring introduce
discontinuities or “cliff effects” that may result
in different rewards for contracts with relatively
similar performance and may also reduce the
incentive to improve for contracts that are far away
from a “cliff.”
•

Funding with additional program dollars. Unlike
quality incentive programs in FFS Medicare, which
are either budget neutral (balancing penalties and
rewards) or penalty only, the QBP is a reward-only
incentive program that is projected to increase
program spending by $16 billion in 2026. The
absence of penalties for poor performance may
also dilute incentives for MA organizations to focus
efforts on quality improvement, contradicting the
program’s aims.

The inability of the MA–QBP to meaningfully
characterize the quality of care that MA enrollees
receive makes it difficult for beneficiaries to make
informed choices and for policymakers to assess
the value that private plans bring to the Medicare
program. In our June 2020 report to the Congress, the
Commission recommended replacing the QBP with a
value-incentive program that addresses the flaws of the
QBP. First, focusing on a small set of population-based
outcome and patient/enrollee–experience measures
would facilitate comparisons across MA plans, enabling
beneficiaries to choose based on factors that are most
meaningful to their experience. A continuous scale
of performance, rather than one with cliff effects,
would provide MA plans with the incentive to improve
quality at every level. Performance evaluation at the
local-market level, rather than the contract level
as is currently done, would similarly improve the
information that beneficiaries can use for decisionmaking and the incentives for MA plans to improve
quality in every geographic area.
The Commission also recommended that the
value-incentive program address the variation in
the demographics of MA enrollees across plans. By
accounting for differences in enrollees’ social risk

410 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

factors by stratifying plan enrollment into groups of
beneficiaries with similar social risk profiles, plans
with higher shares of these enrollees would not be
disadvantaged in their ability to receive quality-based
payments, while actual differences in the quality of
care would not be masked. Finally, the Commission
contends that MA quality-bonus payments should
not be financed with additional program dollars,
especially given that, in aggregate, Medicare pays MA
plans more than would have been spent on FFS for
the same beneficiaries. Application of budget-neutral
financing would ensure that the MA quality system
is more consistent with Medicare’s FFS qualitypayment programs, which are either budget neutral or
produce program savings (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2020a).

MA patient experience
Good information on the quality and experience of
care that MA enrollees receive—and how they compare
with FFS Medicare—is necessary for beneficiaries and
policymakers to properly evaluate program and plan
options. However, several data and methodological
challenges affect the ability to compare performance
across MA plans and between MA plans and FFS
Medicare. The Commission has discussed its concerns
with these limitations in previous reports (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2024c, Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2023c, Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2020a, Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2020b, Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2016b). Despite
these challenges, the Commission has expressed a
strong interest in assessing the quality of care that
all Medicare beneficiaries receive. To that end, we
include a summary of MA Consumer Assessment
of Health Providers and Systems (CAHPS) survey
scores, a standardized and validated measure of
patient experience collected by the Agency for
Healthcare Research and Quality.98 The CAHPS survey
represents only one indicator of the quality of care
that MA enrollees receive, but it is subject to fewer
methodological limitations than other metrics that are
currently used in the star-rating system. On the other
hand, contract-level reporting means that responses
may not reflect the experience of enrollees in a
particular geographic area.
Patient-experience measures are an important
indicator of quality of care because they assess,

CAHPS (Consumer Assessment of Healthcare Providers and Systems), CAHPS responses are case-mix adjusted for the predictable effects of the
response tendencies of the people who complete them (e.g., age, sex, health status). The first four measures are composite measures of multiple
survey questions that have responses of “never,” “sometimes,” “usually,” and “always.” CMS converts these to a linear mean score on a 0 to 100
scale. The fifth and sixth measures are global rating measures in which survey questions have responses of 1 to 10, which CMS also converts to
a linear mean score on a 0 to 100 scale. The annual flu vaccine measure has a yes/no response. Years represent the experience of care during
the last six months from the survey. For example, 2024 scores are based on beneficiaries’ self-reported experience in the last six months from
surveys collected in the spring of 2025. The 2019 CAHPS surveys were halted due to the coronavirus pandemic. We report values for 2018 to
include a prepandemic comparison year.
* CMS revised which CAHPS survey items are scored in the “getting appointments and care quickly” composite measure in 2023. We caution
against comparing 2023 and 2024 scores with prior years.

Source: MA–CAHPS national mean scores published by CMS, 2024.

from the patient’s perspective, whether something
that should have happened (such as getting needed
care quickly) did and how often it occurred, across a
population. When patients have a better experience,
they are more likely to adhere to treatments, return
for follow-up appointments, and engage with the
health care system by seeking appropriate care (Doyle
et al. 2013, Navarro et al. 2021). MAOs are required to
contract with a third-party survey vendor to collect
CAHPS survey responses from a random sample of
each MA contract’s enrollees.99 To calculate national
scores, MA–CAHPS measures are case-mix adjusted
for beneficiary characteristics and weighted at the
contract level.100 Table 12-10 presents CAHPS measure
scores for the five most recent years available, 2020 to
2024, along with 2018 as a prepandemic comparison.101
MA enrollees generally rated their coverage and
experience favorably in 2024, and results have been

relatively stable over time.102 Scores for “getting
appointments and care quickly” have increased in 2023
and 2024 compared with previous years, but this finding
is in part attributable to changes in how CMS defined
the measure in 2023 (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid
Services 2025e). The share of respondents receiving an
annual flu vaccine has declined each year from a high
of 76 percent in 2020 to 69 percent in 2024; a similar
decline is observed in FFS Medicare (see Chapter 4
for a discussion of FFS–CAHPS results). On the other
hand, ratings of customer service, plan quality, and care
quality in MA remain consistently high.
These relatively high and consistent scores align
with our focus-group findings that beneficiaries are
generally satisfied with their plans and do not report
widespread challenges with access to care. However,
we recognize that this aggregate picture is not
inconsistent with individual beneficiaries experiencing
issues with access or quality of care in particular

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

411

circumstances. CAHPS surveys target a random
sample of all enrollees, not just those who have sought
care during the survey period. Also, certain subsets
of enrollees, such as those residing in institutional
settings, are excluded from participation. Further,
CAHPS survey items focus on breadth of access to
care, not the specifics of cases in which problems
have been experienced. While the Commission has
expressed concern that the small share of beneficiaries
with the greatest health needs may disproportionately
experience access problems, the CAHPS survey is not
designed to capture the depth of those problems. For
this reason, the Commission aims to contextualize the
results of these broad, population-level surveys with
other potential indicators of access and quality in MA.

Medicare payment and MA plan costs
for enrollees with ESRD
Although individuals with ESRD have been entitled
to Medicare Part A and Part B benefits since 1972,
they were mostly prohibited from joining MA plans
until 2021, when the 21st Century Cures Act of 2016
allowed all beneficiaries with ESRD to sign up for MA
plans.103 Within four years of the statutory change, MA
enrollment among beneficiaries with ESRD increased
from 27 percent in December 2020 to 55 percent by
December 2024. The increase in MA enrollment by
beneficiaries on dialysis is likely linked to the same
factors that have increased MA’s popularity among
beneficiaries without ESRD, including cost-sharing
reductions, non-Medicare benefits, and a cap on
OOP expenses at little or no additional premium.
The cap on OOP expenses, in particular, is likely
to be a valuable benefit for enrollees with ESRD
who, compared with other Medicare beneficiaries
without ESRD, have higher health care utilization and
spending (Powers et al. 2019). Average FFS Medicare
expenditures for beneficiaries with ESRD were $84,479
in 2022, compared with $14,044 per person per year
for beneficiaries without ESRD (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission and the Medicaid and CHIP
Payment and Access Commission 2025). Cost-sharing
burdens are consequently higher for beneficiaries
with ESRD, particularly those without supplemental
cost-sharing coverage through Medicaid, an employersponsored plan, or a Medigap plan.104

412 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

In our March 2021 report to the Congress, we assessed
MA plan costs and revenues for individuals with
ESRD, MA enrollment trends by coinsurance amounts,
and plan prices for dialysis. For this report, we have
updated these analyses using the most recent available
data. Our analysis finds that, since the 2021 statutory
change, overall finances appear to have improved for
both MA plans and dialysis facilities with increasing MA
enrollment. Between 2018 and 2023, Medicare payments
to MA plans for ESRD enrollees rose faster than costs. In
2023, Medicare payments to plans were about 7 percent
higher than average medical costs plus an estimate of
inflation-adjusted administrative expenses for enrollees
with ESRD (total cost-to-revenue ratio of 0.930).
(MA plans also incur additional costs for providing
supplemental benefits to their enrollees with ESRD.)
We also find that a growing share of plans apply the
maximum allowable amount of cost sharing to dialysis
services. In 2022, about 90 percent of enrollees with
ESRD in conventional plans and D–SNPs are responsible
for a 20 percent coinsurance for dialysis. Last, most MA
plans continue to pay more than FFS rates for dialysis
services, but those prices have decreased over time.
In 2022, the average MA plan price for dialysis was 22
percent higher than the FFS Medicare rate, down from
28 percent higher in 2020. The relative decline in prices
paid by MA plans may reflect a change in the balance
of negotiating leverage between MA plans and dialysis
providers over time, as a result of the increasing MA
enrollment by beneficiaries with ESRD and removal of
network-adequacy requirements for outpatient dialysis
facilities.

Medicare’s payments to MA plans for
enrollees with ESRD on dialysis grew faster
than plans’ reported costs
For enrollees with ESRD on dialysis, a base rate is
established for each state—called the “ESRD state
rate”—and is equal to the average FFS spending for
beneficiaries with ESRD in that state, standardized to
the national average spending for FFS beneficiaries
with ESRD. Medicare payments to MA plans for an
enrollee with ESRD on dialysis are based on the ESRD
state rate where the enrollee resides, adjusted by the
enrollee’s ESRD risk score.105 The ESRD risk model is
based on FFS beneficiaries with ESRD.
Because plans submit bids only for enrollees without
ESRD (ESRD state rates are calculated entirely by CMS),

a plan’s total rebate amount is based only on enrollees
without ESRD. However, all MA plan enrollees are
entitled to receive the same benefit package, including
receipt of supplemental benefits or requirement to
pay a plan premium. CMS offers plans the option to
reconcile financing for enrollees with ESRD with the
plan’s rebate using the “ESRD subsidy” (Centers for
Medicare & Medicaid Services 2025c). For plans with
ESRD payments that do not cover their projected
medical, administrative, and supplemental benefits
costs for enrollees with ESRD, the ESRD subsidy allows
plans to allocate a portion of their rebate funding to
cover the gap between ESRD payments and costs
(including plan costs for providing supplemental
benefits to enrollees with ESRD), thereby reducing the
level of rebates available to fund supplemental benefits
for all enrollees. Conversely, for plans with ESRD
payments that are greater than plan ESRD costs, the
ESRD subsidy allows plans to add net ESRD revenues
to their rebate funding and increase the level of
supplemental benefits for all enrollees.
Using the same methodology as in our March 2021
report to the Congress, we analyzed bid pricing tool
(BPT) data for plan years 2020 through 2025 to assess
whether Medicare payments to MA plans for enrollees
with ESRD covered plans’ reported medical costs for
those enrollees.106,107 BPT data include information on
total revenue, net medical expenses, and the share of
member months applicable, separately, to enrollees
with and without ESRD. Medical expenses include costs
for Medicare-covered services and Part A and Part
B mandatory supplemental benefits, but they do not
include expenses for optional supplemental benefits.
Plans do not report administrative and profit costs
separately for enrollees with ESRD in the BPT data, and
therefore we are unable to include those costs in our
estimate of average medical costs for enrollees with
ESRD, unless noted otherwise.
In 2018, MA plans’ revenues were, on average, about
equal to their medical costs for enrollees with ESRD.
Average medical costs for enrollees with ESRD were
$6,752 PMPM, slightly below the average plan revenue
of $6,769 PMPM—a medical cost-to-revenue ratio of
0.997. That suggests that plans, after accounting for
costs of administration, likely lost money on ESRD
enrollees. However, by 2023, Medicare payments were
much higher relative to plans’ costs for enrollees with
ESRD: Average medical costs rose 8.0 percent while

plan revenues climbed 22.4 percent ($7,291 PMPM and
$8,287 PMPM, respectively—a medical cost-to-revenue
ratio of 0.880) (Figure 12-16, p. 414).
Among contracts, there was variation in ESRD
enrollment-weighted medical cost-to-revenue ratios,
though the amount of variation decreased between
2018 and 2023. MA contracts with lower ESRD medical
costs than revenues have a cost-to-revenue ratio
below 1.0. The median MA contract medical cost-torevenue ratio in 2018, weighted by months of ESRD
enrollment, was 0.971 (0.861 at the 10th percentile vs.
1.197 at the 90th percentile) versus 0.885 (0.814 at the
10th percentile vs. 0.963 at the 90th percentile) in
2023 (Figure 12-17, p. 415). Contracts with a cost-torevenue ratio below 1.0 included about 56 percent of
MA enrollees with ESRD in 2018 and 93 percent of MA
enrollees with ESRD in 2023 (data not shown).
In addition to medical costs, plans incur administrative
expenses for their enrollees with ESRD. Based on data
from the 2019 base period, CMS estimated that plans
would have nonbenefit expenses of about $350 PMPM
for plan administration of benefits for enrollees with
ESRD in 2021 (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services
2020a). Adding inflation-adjusted administrative
expenses to average medical costs for enrollees with
ESRD, costs were about 7 percent lower than Medicare
payments to plans (total cost-to-revenue ratio of 0.930
in 2023) (not shown in Figure 12-17, p. 415).108 Depending
on whether an MA contract had administrative expenses
for beneficiaries above or below the average amount
estimated by CMS, the actual cost-to-revenue ratio may
also be higher or lower than 0.930. MA plans also incur
additional costs for providing supplemental benefits
to their enrollees with ESRD, but these costs are not
included in our analysis.
There are several potential reasons why Medicare’s
payments to MA plans for enrollees with ESRD are
rising faster than plan costs, though at this time we
do not have evidence that affirms why. MA plans may
have reduced their costs by becoming more efficient
in caring for enrollees with ESRD. The literature is
still sparse on how care is managed and delivered
to beneficiaries with ESRD in MA. An earlier study
by Powers et al. (2020) found that enrollees in an
ESRD C–SNP had fewer inpatient and skilled nursing
facility admissions and days than a control group of
beneficiaries with ESRD in FFS from 2010 to 2013;

Faster growth in average plan revenues relative to costs
could be driven by concentration of MA enrollment
growth in states with above-average ESRD payment
rates, as could more intensive coding of risk scores
by MA plans for enrollees with ESRD. Additionally, if
beneficiaries with ESRD who have higher spending
remain in FFS Medicare, that would increase the ESRD
state rate for payments to MA plans. The national
average monthly FFS spending for beneficiaries on
dialysis, as captured by ESRD U.S. per capita costs,
has, in fact, grown from $7,133 in 2018 to $9,333 in
2023 (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services
2022b, Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services
2017b). Beneficiaries who are eligible for Medicare
solely due to ESRD (rather than age or disability) were
completely barred from joining MA (except for an ESRD
C-SNP) prior to 2021. Further research is needed to
assess whether MA enrollment among those enrollees
contributed to the differing cost and revenue trends.

Cost sharing for dialysis services increased,
2018 to 2022
For dialysis services, the maximum allowable
cost sharing is 20 percent coinsurance. Although
beneficiaries with ESRD who enroll in MA may be
seeking lower OOP costs, those who enroll in plans
that charge 20 percent dialysis coinsurance may pay
a higher dollar value for dialysis cost sharing than is
allowable in FFS Medicare if their plan pays dialysis
prices above FFS Medicare rates.
Using the same methodology as in our March 2021
report to the Congress, we analyzed MA encounter
data, the plan benefits package (PBP) renal dialysis
benefits file, and SNP data for 2018 to 2022 to
measure the share of MA plans, and the share of
ESRD enrollment in MA plans, with the maximum

coinsurance amount (20 percent) for dialysis, by MA
plan type (conventional MA, D–SNP, or C–SNP).
We found that between 2018 and 2022, 80 percent to
90 percent of MA enrollees with ESRD were in plans
with coinsurance for dialysis, compared with about
5 percent of enrollees in plans with a copayment
for dialysis. Across all plan types—conventional MA,
D–SNPs, and C–SNPs—the share of plans with a 20
percent coinsurance for dialysis increased between
2018 and 2022. Over the period, the share rose from 82
percent to 91 percent among conventional MA plans;
from 72 percent to 83 percent among D–SNPs; and
from 74 percent to 82 percent among C–SNPs. There
was a corresponding decrease in the share of plans
with no cost sharing for dialysis from 2018 to 2022: a
7 percentage point decrease among conventional MA

Consistent with these changes in plan offerings,
the share of MA enrollees with ESRD in plans with
the maximum allowable 20 percent coinsurance for
dialysis services also grew among those enrolled in
conventional MA plans and D–SNPs (Figure 12-18).
Between 2018 and 2022, the share of enrollees with
. ESRD with a 20 percent coinsurance for dialysis
increased from 79 percent to 90 percent among those

416 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

in conventional MA plans and from 80 percent to 93
percent among those in D–SNPs. However, the share
among those in C–SNPs remained relatively stable (75
percent in 2018 vs. 76 percent in 2022). The share of
enrollees with ESRD with no cost sharing for dialysis
decreased during this time from 19 percent to 8
percent among enrollees in conventional MA plans
and 11 percent to 1 percent among enrollees in D–
SNPs. Among those in D–SNPs, the share of enrollees
with some cost sharing (either less than 20 percent
coinsurance or a copay) also decreased between 2018 (9
percent) and 2022 (6 percent).

Plans are prohibited by law from charging more than
FFS for dialysis. As a result, when CMS first put limits
on dialysis cost sharing in 2011, it specified that plans
could not charge more than 20 percent coinsurance
(the same as in FFS) or a $30 copayment. At the
time, the two amounts were actuarially equivalent.
However, CMS did not update the copayment limit to
reflect newer data until 2022, when it calculated that
the actuarially equivalent copayment had increased to
$64. The updated figure implies that the $30 limit had
become roughly equal to 10 percent coinsurance and
that any effort to use copayments meant, in effect,
charging much lower cost sharing than FFS. This
discrepancy between the coinsurance and copayment
limits may be one reason why almost all plans use
coinsurance for dialysis. The updated copayment
limit (rounded to $65) began to be phased in between
2023 and 2026; at that point, some plans might begin
using copayments (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2023b).
Setting cost sharing for dialysis services at the maximum
allowable amount allows plans to distribute rebate
funding to other supplemental benefits for all enrollees
in the plan, which can be beneficial for enrollees whose
dialysis cost sharing is covered through Medicaid.109
Although the MOOP constrains total OOP spending for
MA enrollees, charging the maximum allowable cost
sharing for dialysis services may discourage nondually
eligible beneficiaries from enrolling in a plan or from
staying enrolled in a plan throughout the year. However,
while cost-sharing amounts for dialysis services are
likely salient to beneficiaries with ESRD who typically
receive dialysis three times per week, other factors—
such as supplemental benefits, provider networks,
and cost-sharing structures for other services—also
influence beneficiaries’ decisions to enroll in MA (Brazier
et al. 2025).

Most MA plans paid more than FFS rates for
dialysis services, 2018 to 2022
For beneficiaries with ESRD in FFS Medicare, dialysis
facilities are paid a single case-mix-adjusted payment
under the ESRD prospective payment system (PPS) that
includes composite-rate services and ESRD-related
drugs, laboratory services, and medical equipment and
supplies.110,111 For 2026, the base FFS payment rate is
$281.71 for both freestanding facilities and hospitalbased facilities (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid

Services 2025g). Payments are adjusted by facility-level
factors (wage index, low-volume adjustment, and rural
adjustment) and patient-level factors (age, body size,
onset (first four months of dialysis treatment), and
comorbidities).112
For beneficiaries with ESRD in MA, dialysis facilities are
paid according to a negotiated rate that varies across
MA contracts.113 If MA plans are unable to negotiate
dialysis prices similar to (or lower than) FFS Medicare
payment rates for dialysis, plans have to offset higher
dialysis spending from higher prices for dialysis
services by reducing costs for other services for all plan
enrollees (e.g., care coordination to reduce inpatient
hospital and emergency room visits) or they risk losses
on enrollees with ESRD.
We analyzed FFS claims and MA encounter data
from 2018 to 2022 for the 50 states and the District
of Columbia to estimate the prices MA plans paid
for dialysis. The MA encounter data did not include
sufficient information to replicate the complete FFS
payment calculation, but we were able to adjust MA
plan payments to facilities by the two factors we
consider to have the greatest importance: enrollee age
and facility wage index. We do not expect differences in
the other factors to significantly affect the comparison
of dialysis prices. (See text box “Estimating MA prices
for dialysis services relative to FFS Medicare rates,” p.
419, for details on our methodology and how it changed
between the 2021 report to the Congress and this
chapter.)
From 2018 through 2020, the national average MA
plan prices for dialysis increased slightly from 26
percent to 28 percent higher than FFS Medicare rates,
respectively. In 2021 and 2022, the national average MA
plan prices for dialysis dropped to 23 percent and 22
percent higher than FFS Medicare rates, respectively.
The average price paid for dialysis varied widely across
contracts: Weighting by treatment volume, prices for
dialysis services in 2018 ranged between 1.1 percent
below FFS Medicare rates (10th percentile) to 55.6
percent above FFS Medicare rates (90th percentile).
The range of treatment volume–weighted MA contract
prices for dialysis service was narrower in 2022,
between 2.0 percent and 39.1 percent above FFS
Medicare rates (10th and 90th percentile, respectively)
(Figure 12-19, p. 418).
