One reason that MA plans pay more for dialysis than
FFS Medicare could be the high level of consolidation
in outpatient dialysis facilities. In 2022, two large
dialysis organizations (LDOs), DaVita and Fresenius,
operated three-quarters of dialysis facilities (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2025b). Further, among
1,889 counties in which there was at least one dialysis
facility in 2022, either one or both LDOs were the only

418 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

facilities available in approximately 54 percent of those
counties. Increasing MA enrollment by beneficiaries
with ESRD, combined with the removal of networkadequacy requirements for outpatient dialysis facilities,
may have increased the negotiating leverage for many
MA plans, which could have contributed to the drop in
MA prices for dialysis in 2021 and 2022. ■

Estimating MA prices for dialysis services relative to FFS Medicare rates

I

n the March 2021 report to the Congress, we
used fee-for-service (FFS) claims and Medicare
Advantage (MA) encounter data to estimate
average FFS versus MA prices for dialysis services by
(1) summing payment amounts for dialysis revenue
centers; (2) making adjustments for age and facility
wage index (geographic location); and (3) dividing
the adjusted payment sum by the total number of
dialysis treatments. We defined price as the sum
of Medicare program or MA plan payments and
beneficiary cost-sharing liability. Therefore, to
account for the 20 percent coinsurance for dialysis
in FFS, we divided the estimated FFS average from
Step 3 by 0.8 to impute the 100 percent FFS price.
To account for beneficiary cost sharing in MA, we
added the enrollees’ cost-sharing liability reported
in MA encounter data to the sum calculated in Step
1.114 Finally, we calculated the ratio of the 100 percent
MA price versus the FFS price. We reported that the
prices MA plans paid in 2018 for dialysis services,
after accounting for differences in age and facility
wage index, averaged about 14 percent higher than
FFS Medicare rates (Medicare Payment Advisory
Commission 2021b).
In this chapter, we updated our methodology for
imputing the 100 percent MA price for dialysis
services to address a limitation of the previous
analysis. Specifically, we changed our specification
to use information on beneficiary cost-sharing
liability amounts for dialysis services as reported
on the MA plan benefits package (PBP) renal
dialysis benefits file instead of using enrollees’
cost-sharing liability amounts reported in MA
encounter data. We made this change to address

high levels of missingness in enrollees’ cost-sharing
liability amounts on encounter records submitted
by MA plans: Approximately 87 percent of monthly
dialysis claims reported in the encounter data had
a $0 cost-sharing liability amount, even though 79
percent of MA enrollees with ESRD were in plans
with 20 percent coinsurance in 2018 (discussed in
the previous section titled “Cost sharing for dialysis
services increased, 2018 to 2022,” p. 415). Among
beneficiaries with ESRD enrolled in plans with a
20 percent coinsurance for dialysis, 83 percent of
encounter records had a $0 cost-sharing liability
amount reported. Of the remaining 17 percent of
encounter records for enrollees in plans with a 20
percent coinsurance for dialysis, the median enrollee
cost-sharing liability amount reported was $853 per
month (interquartile range (IQR): $599 to $1,848);
using the cost-sharing information from the PBP file,
we impute the median enrollee cost-sharing liability
amount for these monthly claims to be $728 (IQR:
$616 to $848).
Our updated methodology replaces what we
suspect is missing beneficiary cost-sharing
liability information from MA encounter data with
imputed cost-sharing amounts from the PBP file
and increases our estimate of MA plan prices for
dialysis in 2018 by 10.5 percent. After accounting for
differences in age and wage index, the prices MA
plans paid for dialysis services averaged about 26
percent higher than FFS Medicare rates. Our new
estimates are similar to Lin et al.’s (2022) finding that
in 2016 to 2017, MA plans paid 27 percent more than
FFS Medicare for dialysis services (Lin et al. 2022). ■

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

419

12-A

T E C H N I C A L

A P P E N D I X

Base comparison

The Commission’s method for
estimating what FFS spending would
have been for MA enrollees before
favorable selection and coding effects
The Commission’s method for estimating Medicare
Advantage (MA) payments relative to what feefor-service (FFS) spending would have been for
MA enrollees includes three components that are
estimated separately and then combined into a single
estimate. The first of these components (as presented
in Chapter 12, p. 379) constructs a base comparison of
MA payments and FFS spending that measures how MA
payments compare with the spending of FFS enrollees
with the same risk scores and county of residence.
The Commission’s approach appropriately reflects
the lower payments that MA plans receive due to the
inclusion of Part A–only beneficiaries in benchmarks.
The base comparison estimates the difference between
actual MA payments and what FFS spending would
have been for MA enrollees under the assumption
that risk scores and county of residence fully account
for differences between the MA and FFS populations.
Differences between the MA and FFS populations that
are not captured by the base comparison are found
in Technical Appendix 12-B (favorable selection) and
Technical Appendix 12-C (coding intensity).

Methodology for the base comparison of
MA payments and FFS spending
In constructing the base comparison, we compare
Medicare’s actual payments to MA plans with an
estimate of what the program’s spending would have
been if those enrollees were in the FFS program,
under the assumption that risk scores and county of
residence fully account for differences between the
MA and FFS populations. The analytic framework for
our base comparison is similar for MA enrollees with
end-stage renal disease (ESRD) and enrollees without
ESRD. This appendix describes the methodology for
constructing the base comparison for the population
without ESRD (about 99 percent of MA enrollees in
2024). The methodology for the population with ESRD
(about 1 percent of MA enrollees in 2024) is described in
the text box on incorporating this population into our
estimates (pp. 379–382).
We compute the base comparison as a ratio in which
MA payments are the numerator and FFS spending

is the denominator. For each MA enrollee, we first
measure the combined payment (bid and rebate) that
the Medicare program made to their plan during the
year. We compare that payment to the average riskadjusted spending of FFS beneficiaries living in the
same county. We then compute the base comparison
of MA payments to FFS spending as the sum of those
individual payments for each MA enrollee divided
by the sum of each of the FFS spending comparison
amounts. Our approach differs slightly for years in
which historical data are not available.
The methodology of our base comparison aligns
with the population CMS uses for calibrating the
hierarchical condition category (HCC) risk model
and the risk-adjustment CMS applies to an MA plan’s
county-level benchmark. We use historical data for
years in which data are available (2016 to 2023) to
remove the uncertainty from projection errors. Our
approach also standardizes for differences between
MA and FFS enrollees based on the type of Medicare
coverage, county of residence, and risk scores.
Study and comparison populations for
beneficiaries

In conducting our base comparison for the non-ESRD
population of MA payments and FFS spending for 2016
through 2023, we applied the same set of inclusion
criteria for the MA and FFS beneficiaries included in
our analysis. We restricted our analysis to beneficiaries
who—for the months in which they were alive during
a target year—had both Part A and Part B coverage,
had Medicare as their primary payer, did not have
ESRD, and had at least one nonhospice month. These
restrictions are consistent with those used for CMS’s
non-ESRD HCC risk-model calibration. In addition,
to ensure reliability, we required that beneficiaries
reside in the 50 states or the District of Columbia and
we removed hospice enrollment months (in which
MA plans do not have liability for Part A and Part B
services), which encompasses months in which an
enrollee did not have at least one nonhospice day.
Methodology for comparing MA payments to FFS
spending using historical data

For each MA plan, CMS publishes risk-standardized
base payment rates (reconciled for actual county-level
plan enrollment) and rebate amounts. To calculate
total MA spending, we multiplied risk-standardized
base payment rates by each beneficiary’s final risk

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

421

score and actual number of months of MA enrollment,
then we added rebate amounts multiplied by actual
MA enrollment months. For months in which CMS
applied sequestration adjustments to MA payments, we
reduced the sum of all base payments and rebates by
the applicable sequestration percentage (generally, a 2
percent reduction).115,116
For the FFS comparison group, we calculated the
monthly average of risk-standardized spending per
beneficiary at the county level. We include beneficiary
spending from adjudicated claims for Part A and Part B
services (removing hospice payments, medical education
payments, and kidney-acquisition costs117,118), provider
settlement amounts (e.g., reconciled payments after cost
reports are submitted119), provider incentive payments
(e.g., shared savings120), and CMS’s estimate of FFS
administrative claims costs.121 We then risk standardized
the average FFS spending within each county using final
beneficiary risk scores.122,123 We used our calculation of
the county-based risk-standardized spending average
for the FFS comparison group and multiplied this
average by the risk-adjusted (using MA enrollee risk
scores) MA enrollment months in each county:

We denote an MA enrollee as individual i, residing
in county g, with risk score r, and ȳg is the average
risk-standardized spending of FFS enrollees in the
same county g (standardized to a 1.0 risk score).
Thus, our total estimate compares the aggregate MA
payments for MA enrollees (denoted by p as an MA
enrollee’s individual Medicare payment) with their
corresponding aggregate FFS spending amounts (using
the same enrollment-weighted risk score and county of
residence):

For 2023, the base comparison—before accounting for
the effects of favorable selection and coding—is 100.6
percent without the ESRD population and 100.7 percent
including the ESRD population.
Methodology for comparing MA payments to FFS
spending using projected data

For years in which we do not have historical data (2024
to 2026), the Commission follows the same analytic

422 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

framework for our base comparison, but we rely on
MA bid and benchmark data from CMS. These data
include plan payment rates, plans’ projected enrollment
in each county, plans’ projected average risk score, and
CMS’s projections of county-level risk-standardized
FFS spending. CMS’s projections of county-level FFS
spending include a different population than our
historical estimates (e.g., unlike the population we use
for the historical estimate, they include beneficiaries
with Part A only, Part B only, or with Medicare as
a secondary payer). These population differences
include beneficiaries who are either not eligible for
MA enrollment or are not used in CMS’s calibration
of its HCC risk model. Despite those differences,
the Commission has found that computing the base
comparison using projected MA bid and benchmark
data produces very similar estimates to computing
the comparison with historical data that apply the
appropriate exclusions to the MA and FFS populations.
As shown in Figure 12-7 (p. 384), we compared the
estimates from these two approaches for years that
data are available and found that they produced
estimates within 1 percentage point of one another
(except for 2020 and 2021, when spending fluctuated
during the pandemic). For 2023, the most recent year
for which we can compare estimates under both
approaches, we estimate that the base comparison was
101 percent of FFS spending using both historical data
and projected data.
For the projections of MA spending, we calculate a
payment rate for each plan using information from
the bid data that MA plans submitted. Bid data include
expected payments for Medicare-covered Part A and
Part B services, plan rebates that fund extra benefits,
and payments resulting from the quality-bonus
program. These MA payment rates reflect the projected
MA enrollee risk scores in plan-bid data. We then
calculate total MA payments by multiplying each plan’s
estimated payment rate by the projected enrollment
that plans also include in their bids. Because employer
plans do not submit bids, we use CMS’s county-level
payment rates for these plans and multiply each of
these rates by our projection of employer-plan risk
scores and enrollment. These projections use countylevel employer-plan enrollment and historic differences
in risk scores between employer and nonemployer
plans.124

To calculate what Medicare FFS would have spent for
MA enrollees, we use CMS’s projections (as published
in the MA rate book), which risk standardize CMS’s
Part A and Part B spending estimates to reflect average
spending for a FFS beneficiary with average risk (i.e.,
with a 1.0 risk score) in each county. CMS is required
to exclude spending for some services that FFS
provides but MA plans do not: hospice services, kidneyacquisition costs, and graduate medical education
(both direct and indirect). We then calculate total
spending by multiplying CMS’s county-level estimates
of per capita FFS spending by the projected MA
enrollment in each county and the MA risk scores that
plans include in their bids.
We project our overall base comparison by dividing
total projected MA spending by total projected FFS
spending (assuming the same county of residence
and MA risk score). For 2026, the base comparison
is projected to be 99.2 percent without the ESRD
population and 99.4 percent when including the ESRD
population. This estimate does not include the effects
of favorable selection and coding, which are two of the
three components of our comparison of MA payments
to FFS spending. Favorable selection and coding are
vital to understand what FFS spending would have
been for MA enrollees, and the methodologies for those
components are subsequently explained in Appendix
12-B and Appendix 12-C.

Part A–only beneficiaries are appropriately
accounted for in the Commission’s
approach
The Commission’s methodology for comparing MA
payments to FFS spending appropriately accounts for
the Part A–only population (and the smaller Part B–only
population) in both parts of the calculation of the base
comparison: MA payments (the numerator) and FFS
spending (the denominator).
When measuring MA payments, the Commission
uses data that fully reflect the lower payments
that MA plans receive due to the inclusion of Part
A–only beneficiaries in benchmarks

When measuring MA payments, we use data on the
actual payment rates that Medicare uses to pay plans
for MA enrollees. Those payment rates are determined
by county benchmarks, plans’ bids, plans’ quality
ratings, and enrollee risk scores. The FFS population
that CMS uses to compute county benchmarks

includes beneficiaries enrolled only in Part A, who have
lower average spending than beneficiaries enrolled
in both Part A and Part B. The inclusion of Part A–
only beneficiaries in the population that CMS uses to
compute county benchmarks reduces benchmarks
relative to what they would have been if benchmarks
were based solely on the Part A and Part B population.
The effect of Part A–only beneficiaries on benchmarks
is fully reflected in the data that the Commission uses
to measure MA payments in both years when we are
able to use historical data (2016 to 2023) and years
when we rely on projected data (2024 to 2026). The
historical data use the actual payment rates (on a per
enrollee basis) that Medicare used to pay plans for MA
enrollees applied to each plan’s actual enrollment and
risk scores. The projected data use CMS’s projected
plan payment rates applied to each plan’s projected
enrollment and risk scores. Further detail on the
historical and projected payment data is included in the
previous section.
The Commission does not have a recent estimate of
how much the inclusion of Part A–only beneficiaries
reduces benchmarks. However, such an estimate is not
necessary for our modeling of the base comparison.
Our use of actual payment rates reflects the net effect
of actual benchmarks, bids, and quality bonuses.
Therefore, we do not require any assumptions or
modeling about the Part A–only population for this part
of the estimate. In 2017, the Commission recommended
excluding Part A–only beneficiaries from the county
benchmark population. If Part A–only beneficiaries
were excluded from benchmarks, we expect that
MA payments would be even higher relative to FFS
spending than the Commission currently estimates.
The numerator of the base comparison (MA payments)
would increase while the denominator (FFS spending)
would stay about the same.
When measuring FFS spending, the
Commission’s analysis of historical data excludes
Part A–only beneficiaries

As described earlier, we exclude beneficiaries with
Part A–only coverage from our analysis of FFS spending
for all years in which historical data are available (2016
to 2023). In 2023, we estimate that MA plans received
combined payments (including both the bid and
rebate) that were about 1 percent higher than what
the Medicare program spent on FFS beneficiaries
with Part A and Part B coverage who were living in the

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

423

same county as MA enrollees, on a risk-adjusted basis.
(After applying adjustments for favorable selection
and coding intensity that correct for inaccuracies
in risk adjustment, the Commission estimates that
MA payments are substantially higher than what FFS
spending would have been.)
For the three most recent years (2024 to 2026), we
rely on CMS’s projections to measure county-level
FFS spending. Despite the inclusion of Part A–only
beneficiaries in CMS’s projections, our analysis
indicates that our use of projected data has produced
reliable estimates of the base comparison. As Figure
12-7 (p. 384) shows, we compared estimates from the
approach using historical data to the approach using
projected data for years that data are available and
found that the two approaches produced estimates
within 1 percentage point of one another (except for
2020 and 2021, when spending fluctuated during the
pandemic). Based on that analysis, we have concluded
that no adjustment is needed to the projected FFS
spending used for the base comparison when historical

424 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

data are not available. We will continue to analyze
more recent data as they become available and will
incorporate an adjustment if a difference emerges.
Comparisons of MA payments to FFS spending using
projected data have produced similar estimates to
comparisons using historical data of FFS beneficiaries
enrolled in Part A and Part B, despite the differences
in populations included (Figure 12-7, p. 384). Our
analysis of historical data suggests that the county
FFS rates used for setting benchmarks have roughly
tracked the historical spending of the FFS Part A and
Part B population used in the Commission’s analysis.
(As described earlier, the Commission also applies
other appropriate exclusions to the Part A and Part
B population to align with the MA population.) That
analysis suggests that there are one or more factors
that offset the effects of including the Part A–only
population in the calculation of the county rates
that are used for benchmarks. The Commission will
continue analyzing these trends and incorporate new
data as appropriate. ■

12-B

T E C H N I C A L

A P P E N D I X

Favorable selection

The Commission’s methodology for
estimating favorable selection in MA
The risk-adjustment model that CMS uses to adjust
payments to Medicare Advantage (MA) plans predicts
Medicare spending using beneficiary characteristics,
including demographics and medical conditions. The
model is estimated using data from beneficiaries
enrolled in the fee-for-service (FFS) program. For
any set of characteristics, some FFS beneficiaries
with those characteristics will have actual spending
that is more than predicted and others will have
actual spending that is less than predicted by the
risk-adjustment model. The goal of risk adjustment
is to accurately predict spending for a population
of beneficiaries, on average. In other words, risk
adjustment is accurate for a population when
the overprediction errors are balanced with the
underprediction errors.
“Favorable selection” refers to the tendency for
Medicare’s risk-adjustment model to—on average—
overpredict the spending that the MA-enrolled
population would have had if they were enrolled in the
FFS program, even if medical conditions were recorded
with similar coding intensity. Favorable selection can
occur due to unmeasured differences in health status
but can also result from factors such as differences in
beneficiaries’ propensities to seek, or receive, care for
reasons that are unrelated to their health.
The analysis described in this chapter continues to use
the analytic framework from our March 2025 report
to the Congress but makes technical adjustments to
improve the accuracy of the estimated changes in
favorable selection during MA enrollment. We made
three sets of changes to address those aims:
•

First, we improve how we model changes in
selection during MA enrollment by including dualeligibility status in our matching of the MA study
population to the FFS proxy group.

•

Second, we improve how we extrapolate our
estimates of favorable selection to MA enrollees
outside of our study population by accounting for
the dual-eligibility status of those enrollees (along
with mortality status and initial year of enrollment).

•

Third, we improve the comparability between our
study population and FFS comparison groups by
applying a series of technical updates.

Chapter 12 presents estimates from two methods of
estimating the effect of favorable selection in the MA
population. The first is a comprehensive method that
we apply to estimate a selection effect for the entire
MA-enrolled population—except for those with endstage renal disease (ESRD)—in each year from 2015
to 2023. We also use estimates from that method to
project the effect of favorable selection for 2024 to
2026, years in which we do not yet have data available.
The second is a simple method that estimates favorable
selection for the portion of ESRD MA enrollees who
were on dialysis and who were enrolled in the FFS
program in the previous year, referred to as “recent
switchers.” This simple method compares the FFS
spending of recent switchers in the year before they
enrolled in MA with the spending of beneficiaries
who remained in FFS (“stayers”) and were alive for all
12 months, standardizing for differences in risk score
and state of residence. We use that simple method to
estimate selection for the years in which MA enrollees
on dialysis were allowed to switch from FFS to MA and
for which we have data (from 2021 to 2023).125 Because
the population of dialysis switchers into MA is relatively
small, we are not able to reliably construct the cohorts
of MA switchers and FFS proxy groups necessary for
our comprehensive method. Instead, we use our simple
method for estimating favorable selection because it
is not dependent on large subcohorts of switchers by
year of initial enrollment into MA and by mortality.
In addition, because beneficiaries with dialysis only
began switching into MA in 2021, there are fewer years
for the effects of mean reversion to meaningfully
change the initial estimated effects of selection. For the
ESRD population in transplant status or functioninggraft status, we did not have a sufficient population
to estimate selection using our simple method. We
applied estimates of selection from the dialysis ESRD
population to those other segments of the ESRD
population. The methodology for estimating favorable
selection for the ESRD population is described in more
detail in the text box on incorporating MA enrollees
with ESRD into our comparison of payments to MA
plans relative to FFS spending (pp. 379–382).
In this appendix, we also include estimates from a
simple method of estimating selection for recent

426 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

switchers into MA among the non-ESRD population.
Similar to the simple method applied to estimate
selection for the ESRD population, this approach
compares the FFS spending of recent switchers in the
year before they enrolled in MA with the spending of
stayers who were alive for all 12 months, standardizing
for differences in risk score and county of residence.
Estimates from that simple method are an easily
interpretable indicator of selection and can be more
readily compared with some papers in the literature
that follow the same approach. However, estimates
from the simple method do not account for several
important aspects of selection that are accounted for in
the estimates derived from our comprehensive method.
That is why we use estimates of favorable selection
using our comprehensive method when reporting our
overall comparisons of MA and FFS payments.

Simple methodology for estimating
favorable selection of the MA population
Below, we present our analysis using a simple version
of our favorable-selection method that compares the
FFS spending of beneficiaries who switched from FFS
to MA in the following year (“recent switchers”) with
the spending of stayers, standardizing for differences
in risk score and county of residence.126 For example,
we calculated the ratio of 2022 FFS spending for
beneficiaries who switched to MA in 2023 with the
2022 FFS spending for beneficiaries who remained
in FFS in 2023. We then converted the result to a
percentage that we call the “selection percentage.”
(When calculating benchmarks, CMS adjusts for
geographic distribution (i.e., county-level enrollment)
of beneficiaries and differences in risk scores.
Similarly, we adjusted the spending of beneficiaries
who remained in FFS to have the same geographic
distribution and risk scores as the beneficiaries who
switched to MA.) We calculated an initial selection
percentage for each cohort that entered MA in 2008
through 2023.
Using the simple method, we found evidence of
favorable selection among recent switchers into MA
during their last year in FFS throughout the period from
2007 to 2022 (Figure 12-B1, p. 428). We estimate that
the selection percentage ranged from 87 percent to 96
percent during the period. Favorable selection using the
simple method was highest in 2022. (MA enrollee localarea spending was 13 percent less than predicted by
their risk score, a selection percentage of 87.)

Comprehensive methodology for
estimating favorable selection of the MA
population
We use our comprehensive method to estimate
an aggregate selection percentage for the entire
population of non-ESRD MA enrollees in each year,
beginning in 2015. That aggregate selection percentage
represents the amount by which the predicted
spending used for MA payments (estimated using the
FFS population in Medicare’s risk-adjustment model)
overpredicted (or underpredicted) the actual spending
that the MA population would have had if those
beneficiaries had been enrolled in the FFS program in
that year. It is computed as a ratio of (1) the estimated
spending that the FFS program would have incurred for
the MA-enrolled population had they been enrolled in
the FFS program (the numerator) and (2) the estimated
FFS spending that the Medicare payment system
predicted for that population (the denominator).
We label that ratio the “selection percentage.” We
cannot directly observe the selection percentage of
beneficiaries during their enrollment in MA because
we cannot observe what their spending would have
been had they instead been enrolled in FFS in that
year. However, we can directly estimate the selection
percentage of MA enrollees who were previously
enrolled in the FFS program in the year before they
switched to MA. For that reason, we use data from
MA enrollees who were previously enrolled in the FFS
program as our study population and model how their
selection percentage changed between the year before
they enrolled in MA and their most recent year of MA
enrollment.
Our study population consists of beneficiaries who
had at least two years of prior enrollment in the
FFS program before switching to MA (“previous
switchers”) and who met our other inclusion criteria,
approximately 26 percent of non-ESRD MA enrollees in
2023 (8 million MA enrollees).127 For those enrollees, we
have complete data on their spending and risk scores
while they were in FFS that we can use as the basis
for estimating a selection effect. The remaining nonESRD MA population had at least two years of prior
enrollment in the FFS program but did not meet all
of our inclusion criteria (9 percent in 2023), switched
from FFS to MA but had less than two years of prior FFS
enrollment (30 percent in 2023), or enrolled in MA upon
their initial eligibility for Medicare Part A and Part B
and had no FFS months (35 percent in 2023).128

For enrollees outside our study population, we assign
a selection percentage using data from our study
population of previous switchers who joined MA in
the same year and had the same dual-eligibility status
during the target year and mortality status in the
following two years. Assigning selection percentages to
the nonstudy population using the estimated selection
of enrollees in our study population with the same year
of entry, dual-eligibility status, and mortality status
adjusts for many of the differences between the study
and nonstudy populations. MA enrollees with ESRD,
who accounted for 1 percent of the MA population in
2023, are assigned a selection effect equal to zero on
the basis of the analysis described later.129

428 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

We estimate selection for the study population of
MA enrollees who were previous switchers using two
main components: the selection percentage before
each beneficiary was enrolled in MA (“base-year
selection”) and the estimated change in selection
between the base year and the most recent year of
MA enrollment. We describe below how we measure
base-year selection and the change in selection for that
population.
Study and comparison populations

We include beneficiaries in our study population if they
(1) were enrolled in MA for at least one month of the
year for which we are estimating selection (referred to

as the “target year,” which is 2023 for the most recent
estimate); (2) had joined MA between 2010 and 2023;
and (3) had been enrolled in FFS and had both Part A
and Part B coverage for at least two full calendar years
before enrolling in MA. Because hospice enrollees
receive their Medicare Part A and Part B coverage
only from FFS and not from MA plans, we remove MA
enrollment months while the beneficiary is in hospice
(unless they were participants in CMS’s value-based
insurance design hospice demonstration). We address
the issue of decedents separately (discussed below).
Consistent with our comparisons of MA payments
and coding relative to FFS spending, we include
beneficiaries enrolled in conventional MA plans
(available to all beneficiaries), special-needs plans, and
employer plans because the Medicare program makes
payments to plans for all of these populations, and
they all can experience favorable selection. We require
beneficiaries to have at least two full calendar years of
FFS enrollment because the CMS hierarchical condition
category (HCC) risk-adjustment model calculates risk
scores using diagnoses from the prior year’s claims.
Thus, we require data on MA beneficiaries with two
years of prior FFS enrollment to calculate risk scores
for their last year of FFS enrollment. We divide the
study population for each target year into 14 annual
cohorts based on the year that they previously enrolled
in MA (2010 through 2023). The study population of
MA enrollees is used to construct the numerator of the
selection percentages we estimate.
For the comparison population used to construct the
denominator for the selection percentages, we include
FFS beneficiaries who generally would have met the
inclusion criteria for CMS’s calibration of its HCC
risk model and had the necessary data to construct
risk scores. We also apply an additional exclusion to
the FFS population used by requiring the comparison
population to have had both Part A and Part B FFS
coverage for at least one full calendar year before
the base year and at least one month of nonhospice
coverage in the base year (the study population’s last
year of FFS enrollment). That exclusion is applied to
enhance similarity to the MA-enrolled population and
to align with the population used to construct the base
spending component of the Commission’s comparison
of MA to FFS spending. Restricting to beneficiaries
enrolled in both Part A and Part B also aligns with the

population used to calibrate the risk-adjustment model.
In addition, we exclude from our FFS comparison
population beneficiaries with any Part A–only, Part B–
only, or MA months during the base year.
We exclude beneficiaries from both the study and
comparison populations if they had characteristics
that would have excluded them from CMS’s non-ESRD
HCC risk-model calibration: ESRD coverage, Part A–
only coverage, Part B–only coverage, hospice election,
or if they had another source of health coverage for
which Medicare acted as a secondary payer.130 CMS
excludes beneficiaries with ESRD from benchmark
calculations, pays MA plans state-based FFS rates
for ESRD beneficiaries, and adjusts benchmarks and
payments for those with Medicare as a secondary payer
to remove the secondary-payer effect. In addition,
we exclude beneficiaries who did not reside in the 50
states and the District of Columbia. Further, to ensure
geographic comparability, we exclude beneficiaries
who did not reside in the same county during any year
in which their spending is calculated. That restriction
allows beneficiaries in our study population to change
their county of residence provided that it does not
interfere with the calculation of spending in the base
year and the target year.
Base-year selection percentage

Each beneficiary has a selection percentage that
represents by how much their predicted spending
estimated using Medicare’s risk-adjustment model
(“predicted spending”) in year t underpredicts or
overpredicts the actual spending they would have
had if they were enrolled in the FFS program in that
year. That individual-year-level selection percentage
is denoted as Si,g,t, for individual i, residing in county
g, in time t. Time t indexes the time since individuals
in each cohort of MA switchers switched from FFS to
MA, with t = 0 representing the year before the switch
occurred and t = 1 representing the first year the cohort
was enrolled in MA. We use year t = T to denote the
“target year” in which we are measuring selection of
the MA population. For example, consider a group of
MA enrollees in 2023 who previously switched from the
FFS program to MA in 2019 (and stayed in MA through
at least one month in 2023). For those enrollees, t = 0 is
2018, t = 1 is 2019, and t = T is 2023.
We refer to a beneficiary’s selection percentage in the
year before switching to MA as the base-year selection

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

429

percentage and denote the base year as t = 0. Because
MA switchers were enrolled in FFS in year t = 0, we
can use their actual FFS spending to estimate their
selection percentage in that year.
We estimate their base-year selection percentage as
the ratio of each individual’s actual spending relative
to an estimate of their predicted spending (which is
specific to their county g):
(1)

Where yi,g,t=0 is the individual’s actual FFS spending
in year t = 0, ȳg,t=0 is the average risk-standardized
spending of FFS enrollees in the same county g
(standardized to a 1.0 risk score), and ri,g,t = 0 is the
individual’s risk score in year t = 0.131 That initial
selection percentage represents by how much
Medicare’s risk-adjustment model underpredicted
or overpredicted the actual spending that the MA
switchers had during their last year in FFS.
Selection in the base year is measured with a very high
degree of accuracy for the population of MA switchers
with the requisite two prior years of enrollment in
FFS. However, our primary objective is to measure the
degree of selection for the population of MA enrollees
during the target year in which they were enrolled in
MA in time t = T.
An individual’s selection percentage at time t = T could
differ from their selection in the base year for several
reasons, which can be seen from considering the terms
in the formula above. First, the spending an individual
would have had in FFS (the numerator) could get
closer to their predicted spending over time, holding
constant the risk model and the composition of the FFS
population used to calibrate risk scores and calculate
average spending. That phenomenon is known as
“mean reversion.”132 Second, an individual’s predicted
spending in the denominator could get closer to or
farther from the numerator, holding the numerator
constant. That could occur if changes to the risk
model between t = 0 and t = T improve the accuracy of
predicted spending or if changes in the composition

430 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

of the FFS population used to calibrate the risk model
and compute predicted spending change over time.
The composition of the FFS population can change
due to the net effect of inflows of people who newly
enroll in FFS and outflows of beneficiaries who die or
switch to MA. Our approach to estimating the change
in selection accounts for all of those reasons.
Change in selection

We are interested in measuring the selection
percentage in year t = T, when beneficiaries are
enrolled in MA and we cannot directly estimate their
selection percentage. To estimate their selection
percentage in that year, we note that selection in later
years can be expressed as the sum of an individual’s
base-year selection percentage (which we can directly
estimate) and the change in their selection percentage:
(2)

We can estimate the change in selection for each MA
enrollee as the average change in selection percentage
calculated for a proxy group of similar enrollees who
were enrolled in the FFS program between t = 0 and t
= T.133 We define P c,b,Td,m as the set of beneficiaries that
serve as the proxy group for any given cohort, c, of
MA enrollees in year T (defined by the year they first
enrolled in MA) who have the same base year (t = 0),
selection percentage b, year of death d relative to the
target year (t = T), and Medicaid eligibility status m.
The average change in selection for the proxy group is
calculated as
(3)

Each individual’s weight in the calculation is
represented by αi.134 The term Si,g,t = 0 is defined in
equation (1), and Si,g,t = T is defined similarly as the ratio
of each individual’s actual spending in the target year
relative to an estimate of their predicted spending in
that same year:

(4)

Proxy groups are constructed by segmenting the
population of beneficiaries who were enrolled in
FFS from t = –1 through at least one month of t = T
into 110 mutually exclusive groups.135,136 Those FFS
enrollees are assigned to groups according to three
key characteristics. First, they are assigned to one
of three mortality groups based on their mortality
in year t = T, t = T + 1, and t > T + 1. Second, they are
assigned to a group based on their base-year selection
percentage at t = 0. Third, for beneficiaries assigned to
mortality in year t > T + 1, they are assigned to a group
based on their dual-eligibility status for Medicaid
(fully dual, partially dual, nondual).137 Each MA enrollee
is matched to the proxy group that has the same
mortality status, level of base-year selection, and
dual-eligibility status (where applicable). The enrollee
is then attributed the average change in selection for
their matched proxy group.
For example, consider the set of MA enrollees in 2023
who were last enrolled in FFS in 2017 (including all
of 2016), died after 2024, were full duals, and had a
selection percentage of 92 percent in 2017. The proxy
group for those enrollees consists of beneficiaries
who were enrolled in FFS in 2017 (including all of
2016) through 2023, died after 2024, were full duals,
and had a selection percentage between 90 percent
and 100 percent in 2017.138 The average change in
selection percentage for that proxy group between
2017 and 2022 is used as the estimate for the change in
selection percentage for the MA enrollees who match
that proxy group.
Matching on base-year selection percentage is
important for capturing the potential for mean
reversion because people who begin the period with a
large degree of favorable (or unfavorable) selection may
be more likely to experience larger changes that bring
them closer to the mean. Matching on mortality is
important because people closer to death tend to have
higher spending on average, both in absolute terms
and relative to their predicted spending. Matching

on dual-eligibility status is important because these
beneficiaries comprise a larger share of MA enrollees
compared with FFS enrollees. The average change in
selection for the proxy group also accounts for changes
to the Medicare risk-adjustment model, changes in the
composition of the FFS population, and secular trends
in FFS spending over the period. For example, changes
to Medicare’s risk-adjustment model are reflected in
the calculation through changes in risk scores for the
proxy group, which we include when measuring change
in selection for MA enrollees.
Cohort-level estimates of MA switchers

We construct aggregate selection percentages at
the mortality–dual status–cohort-group level by
aggregating the estimates of individual-level selection
effects, weighting by predicted spending and number
of months enrolled in MA during year t = T.
(5)

Because the risk scores of MA enrollees at year t = T
are influenced by the coding practices of their plans,
in constructing the weights for this calculation we
estimate what each individual’s risk score would have
been in year t = T had they been enrolled in FFS.139
To do this estimate, we take the beneficiary’s FFS risk
score from the base year before they switched to MA
and trend it forward based on the change in risk score
for members of their proxy group.
Assigning cohort-level estimates to the
remaining MA population

About 26 percent of the MA-enrolled population in
2023 (8 million enrollees) are in our study population
and have their selection percentage estimated using
the method outlined above. The bulk of the remaining
MA population either switched from FFS to MA but
did not have sufficient data (39 percent) or enrolled
in MA upon their initial eligibility for Medicare and
had no prior FFS months (35 percent).140 For those
enrollees, we assign a selection percentage as the
estimated selection percentage of switchers in our
study population who joined MA in the same year,
had the same mortality status in the following two

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

431

years, and had the same dual-eligibility status in
the target year. That is, selection percentages are
assigned to MA enrollees outside the study population
at the mortality–dual status–cohort-group level. (We
also assign selection percentages to all beneficiaries
who enrolled in MA before 2010 using the estimated
selection percentages for the 2010 cohort of our study
population. This group consists of beneficiaries who
had any prior Medicare FFS enrollment and those who
had no prior Medicare FFS enrollment.)
The decision to generalize estimates of selection
from our study population to switchers outside our
study population and enrollees who joined upon
initial eligibility for Medicare is supported by our own
analysis and external studies. Our analysis suggests
that MA enrollees who switched from FFS but had less
than two years of prior FFS coverage had favorableselection effects that were at least as large as the MA
enrollees in our study population with at least two
years of prior FFS coverage. In our March 2024 report
to the Congress, we used risk scores constructed
from concurrent diagnoses to examine the effect of
favorable selection for beneficiaries who had only one
full year of prior FFS enrollment (before switching
to MA). We estimated favorable-selection effects
that were consistently larger for these beneficiaries
than for our study population of MA switchers (i.e.,
beneficiaries who had at least two years of FFS
enrollment before joining MA) (Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2024c).
For beneficiaries who enroll in MA upon initial
Medicare eligibility, the available evidence is also
consistent with favorable selection. One study used
mortality as a rough proxy for MA favorable selection
and found substantially lower mortality rates among
enrollees who elected MA during their first year of
Medicare eligibility relative to those who elected
FFS during their first year of Medicare eligibility
(Newhouse et al. 2019). Further, another group of
researchers examined pre-Medicare spending for a
sample of about 11,000 beneficiaries who enrolled
in MA at age 65 between 2015 and 2019. When
comparing the risk-adjusted pre-Medicare spending
of these MA enrollees with the pre-Medicare spending
of a sample of beneficiaries who elected FFS at
age 65, these researchers found about 13 percent
lower risk-adjusted spending in the MA sample
relative to the sample of FFS beneficiaries (Teigland

432 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

et al. 2025a). That estimate of favorable selection
exceeds our estimate of favorable selection for MA
switchers throughout the 2015 to 2019 period, which
we estimate to be 4 percent to 9 percent using
the Commission’s simple method, which is most
comparable with that study’s methods. Moreover,
a recent study examined Medicaid enrollees
immediately before becoming eligible for Medicare
from 2017 to 2022 (Pelech et al. 2025). Using the
spending and risk-score data in Appendix Table 11
of that study, MA enrollees had 23 percent lower
risk-standardized medical spending than the FFS
population before Medicare eligibility. Our analyses
combined with the results of other research suggest
that the effect of favorable selection for MA entrants
who have less than two years of prior FFS coverage, or
none at all, may be even larger than what we observed
in our study population.
Final aggregate selection percentage of MA
population

The final aggregate selection percentage of the
entire MA enrolled population in year t = T is a
dollar-weighted average of the individual selection
percentages. Each enrollee is weighted by the number
of months they were enrolled in MA in year t = T, their
FFS county-level risk-standardized spending in year t =
T, and their projected FFS risk score (using the change
in risk score of the proxy group during MA enrollment).
This weighting reflects each individual’s predicted
spending in year t = T as a share of the MA population’s
predicted spending in year t = T.
Our approach to estimating selection for the MA
population in the target year using data from people
who were enrolled in MA in that year implicitly
accounts for the role of selective attrition out of
the MA program. “Selective attrition” refers to the
tendency for the beneficiaries who either die or
disenroll from the MA program to FFS to have risk
scores that underpredict their spending. Analyses by
the Commission and other researchers have found
evidence of selective attrition, suggesting that the
size of favorable selection in the MA population
could be higher than estimates using data from all
people who initially enrolled in MA. By defining our
study population as beneficiaries who are enrolled in
MA during the target year, we exclude beneficiaries
who disenrolled from MA in earlier years. Because

we require more than one year of FFS data for
beneficiaries to be included in our FFS comparison
population, we may slightly underestimate the effect
of selection because we exclude beneficiaries who
disenrolled from MA in years t = 0 or t = T but did
not have enough FFS experience to be counted in our
comparison population for those respective years.
However, those beneficiaries account for a very
small share of enrollees and are not likely to have a
substantial effect on our estimate. This approach to
accounting for selective attrition implicitly through
the construction of the study population remains the
same in the current version of the methodology and
the methods used in our March 2024 and March 2025
reports to the Congress.

cohorts and their proxy cohorts by dual-eligibility
status (using the target year dual-eligibility status),
mortality (using the number of years to death
relative to the target year t = T) and the initial
selection percentage in year t = 0. This modification
allows our estimate to better account for changes
in selection that may be different for fully dualeligible beneficiaries and partially dually eligible
beneficiaries. Previously, we assumed that full duals
and partial duals had the same change in selection
percentage as MA enrollees who were nondually
eligible. In general, the selection estimates in
target year t = T were similar after including dual
eligibility as a matching characteristic. To maintain
a reliable number of beneficiaries in each of our
MA and proxy group subcohorts, we are unable
to apply our dual status match to the smaller
decedent (t = T) and near-decedent (t = T + 1)
populations. Matching on dual status is likely to
be less important for those populations because
we anticipate fewer differences in the changes in
risk-standardized spending between duals and
nonduals among these mortality groups. In other
words, we expect being close to death to be the
most consequential factor for modeling changes in
selection for both duals and nonduals.

Technical changes relative to the methods
used for the March 2025 report to the
Congress
The analysis described in this chapter continues to
use the analytic framework from our March 2025
report. We also continued to make refinements to
improve the accuracy of our estimates and address
some of the limitations of our prior method. The
methodology outlined above reflects three main
types of technical updates to our methods from last
year. First, we now include dual-eligibility status in
our matching of the MA study population to the FFS
proxy group when modeling changes in selection.
Second, we now account for dual-eligibility status
when applying estimates outside our study population,
along with continuing to account for differences in
mortality status and year of initial enrollment. Those
updates address the two main areas of uncertainty in
the Commission’s methodology (see below for further
discussion). Third, we apply a series of technical
updates to improve comparability between our MA and
FFS populations. Overall, these changes improved the
accuracy of the Commission’s estimates of favorable
selection but, on net, did not substantially change the
magnitude of our estimates.
We now describe each of the technical updates more
thoroughly:
•

Matching the MA study population to proxy
groups using dual status (fully dual, partially dual,
or nondual): When modeling changes in selection
during MA enrollment, for beneficiaries with
a mortality status t > T + 1, we now match MA

•

Assigning selection percentages to the nonstudy
MA population using dual status: We use estimates
of favorable selection for MA enrollees in our study
population to assign selection percentages to MA
enrollees outside of our study population. Under
the previous version of our method, we matched
MA enrollees who were outside our study group
to those in our study group based on their year
of entering MA and their mortality status. For
example, individuals who entered MA in 2018 but
did not have the required number of years in FFS
to be in our sample were assigned the average
selection percentage of members of our study
cohort who entered MA in 2018 and who had the
same mortality status (using the number of years
to death relative to the target year t = T). Under
our updated method, we now also match MA
enrollees outside our study population to those
in our study population by dual status (using dual
status in the target year t = T) when assigning
them a selection percentage. (To maintain a
reliable estimate, we apply this match only to MA
enrollees with a mortality status of t > T + 1.) In

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

433

addition, we trend forward the initial average risk
score of each individual in each study-population
cohort by the increase in risk score of their proxy
cohort–dual status–mortality group. We apply this
trended risk score to the broader MA cohort–dual
status–mortality group, which is used to weight
individuals in different cohorts. Thus, under our
updated method, each MA enrollee in the nonstudy
population receives a selection percentage and
expected risk score based on their MA entry year,
dual status, and mortality status in year t = T.
We previously implicitly assumed that enrollees
outside our study population had the same average
selection percentage as individuals with the same
mortality status and year of MA entry within the
study population. That did not allow our estimates
to reflect any differences in selection percentage
by dual status within those subcohorts when
assigning selection percentages outside our study
population. Because MA enrollees in our study
population have a somewhat larger share of dually
eligible beneficiaries relative to the general MA
population, reflecting those differences allows us
to more fully account for differences between our
study and nonstudy populations and increases the
accuracy of our estimates.141 Thus, this update to
our method more accurately accounts for dualstatus differences between MA enrollees included
and not included in our study populations.
•

Other updates to enhance comparability of the MA
and FFS populations include:
• Ensuring consistent county of residence in
the MA study population and FFS comparison
population: We rely on an annual spending file
for each beneficiary to estimate the average
risk-standardized spending in each county for
the FFS population. This county-level spending
is used to construct the denominators of the
selection percentages for MA enrollees in our
study population and FFS enrollees in our proxy
group. Under our previous method, we allowed
beneficiaries to reside in multiple counties during
the year in which spending was calculated. Thus,
we assumed that county-level differences in
spending would not be altered by beneficiaries
who may have resided in a different county
during the year. Under our updated method, we

434 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

exclude beneficiaries who did not reside in the
same county during the years in which spending
is calculated. This change applies to the FFS
comparison group in each year of our study, and
it applies to the MA study population and proxy
group during the base year (t = 0) and during
the target year (t = T). While this update did not
affect our overall estimates of favorable selection,
it ensures that we better account for geographic
differences in spending.
• Removing the weighting of hospice months
from spending calculations: Because MA plans
are not liable for Medicare spending during
hospice enrollment, we exclude beneficiaries
who were in hospice status for the entire
year from our analysis. For beneficiaries who
were in hospice status for part of the year, we
would ideally exclude all of their hospice and
nonhospice spending that was incurred during
hospice months. However, our ability to do so is
imperfect because we rely on annual spending
totals divided by the number of enrolled months
to construct average monthly spending. To
exclude spending on hospice services, we
subtract hospice spending from the annual totals
we use to calculate spending for our MA study
population, proxy group, and FFS comparator.
Under our prior method, we included all of a
beneficiary’s enrolled months (including months
in hospice status) when converting those annual
totals into average monthly spending amounts
for beneficiaries who were enrolled in hospice
for part of the year. Under our current method,
we remove hospice months from the calculation
of average monthly spending in order to better
measure average spending during nonhospice
months, which increases the average monthly
spending of beneficiaries who were in hospice
status for part of the year.142 While this update
did not affect our overall estimates of favorable
selection, it ensures better comparability
between the MA and FFS populations when
calculating risk-standardized spending
differences.
• Correcting for historical errors in the count
of decedent months from 2016 to 2020: In our
March 2024 report to the Congress, our estimates
relied on beneficiaries who were enrolled in

Medicare from the base year (t = 0) through the
target year (t = T), and all beneficiary months
were scaled to a full year (i.e., 12 months). In our
March 2025 report to the Congress, we updated
our method to include decedents. Under our
current method, we correct two discrepancies
that had moved our estimates in two different
directions. First, we correct a discrepancy in the
2017 to 2020 estimates published in the March
2025 report that undercounted decedents. This
discrepancy scaled decedent months to a full
year instead of reflecting the number of months
in the year that the decedents were alive. This
error did not affect our 2021 and 2022 estimates
of favorable selection, which were the basis for
our projections of favorable selection for 2023
to 2025 that we published in the March 2025
report. Second, we corrected an error in our 2016
estimate of favorable selection that overcounted
decedents. This discrepancy assigned decedents
in 2017 as decedents in 2016. This adjustment
increased our 2016 estimate of selection by 2
percentage points.
We compared the estimates that we published in
the March 2025 report to the Congress for the MA
population from 2016 to 2022 with our estimates for
the 2016 to 2022 MA population under the updated
methodology. The updated methodology did not
meaningfully change our overall estimates of favorable
selection for 2021 and 2022. However, our overall
estimate of the MA selection percentage in 2016
decreased by 2 percentage points (a 2 percentage point
increase in MA payments relative to FFS spending) and
increased our selection percentage in 2017 through
2020 by 2 percentage points to 3 percentage points (a
2 percentage point to 3 percentage point decrease in
payments relative to FFS spending) each year. Overall,
our updates make our estimate of favorable selection
much more consistent over the entire 2016 to 2022
period (Figure 12-B2, p. 436). We will continue working
to update our methods where appropriate.
Sensitivity analysis to assess the robustness of
modeling how selection changes over time

One of the most uncertain aspects of the Commission’s
methodology for estimating favorable selection is the
modeling of how selection changes over time (reverts
to the mean) while beneficiaries are enrolled in MA. We

rely on data from our MA-switcher study population
and the FFS population for that modeling. Our
modeling goes through two steps of matching:
•

First, we match our study population of MA
switchers to our FFS proxy group using base-year
(i.e., year in which our study population switched
into MA) mortality status, base-year selection
percentage, and dual-eligibility status. The average
change in selection percentage experienced by
each FFS proxy group is used to estimate the
change in selection during MA enrollment for the
study population of MA switchers with the same
characteristics.

•

Second, we match our study and nonstudy
populations by assigning selection percentages
to the nonstudy population using the estimated
selection of enrollees in our study population with
the same year of entry, dual-eligibility status, and
mortality status.

Neither step in our matching process originally
included dual-eligibility status, and we now add this
characteristic to our matching steps to better account
for the ways in which the MA population (both the
study and nonstudy populations) is demographically
distinct from the FFS population. While dual-eligibility
status is one important factor that makes the MA
population distinct, we wanted to understand how
using other characteristics as matching factors may
affect our estimate of the MA selection percentage.
We started with our original matching characteristics
that were restricted to base-year selection percentage
(for the FFS proxy group only), year of entry
into MA, and mortality status. We compared the
selection percentage of that original matching with
our estimated selection percentage when adding
one additional matching factor in 2022 and 2023.
For example, Figure 12-B3 (p. 437) reports that the
estimated selection percentages when matching only
on base-year selection percentage, year of entry into
MA, and mortality status was 90.3 percent for 2022
and 90.5 percent for 2023. When adding one further
matching factor to the FFS proxy group—base-year
risk score—those percentages slightly changed to 90.2
percent (2022) and 90.4 percent (2023). The remaining
columns in that figure report the estimated selection
percentage when adding one factor to the original
matching characteristics (rather than report effects

dual-eligibility status (a selection percentage of 90.0
percent in 2023) and race (a selection percentage of
89.6 percent in 2023) had a marginal impact on our
results. These social risk factors produced marginally
lower selection percentages (i.e., higher estimates
of favorable selection) in both 2022 and 2023. Thus,
dually eligible beneficiaries and Black and Hispanic
beneficiaries have somewhat different levels of
favorable selection that are not captured in the original
matching method. To better capture the increasing
share of dually eligible beneficiaries in MA (and any
differences in dual status between our study and

nonstudy populations), our estimates of favorable
selection now explicitly adjust for dual-eligible status
(i.e., fully dual, partially dual, and nondual).144 Adding
further matching characteristics, such as race, is
challenging because maintaining adequate sample sizes
in each subgroup is not always possible. An additional
challenge with using race as a matching characteristic
is that the data are incomplete for some beneficiaries.
We will continue to explore other opportunities to
refine our methods in estimating favorable selection.

Report to the Congress: Medicare Payment Policy | March 2026

437

Sources of uncertainty in the Commission’s
estimate of favorable selection

and may be conservative. This year, we improved
our methodology for assigning selection effects
to MA enrollees outside our study population. We
now account for differences in dual-eligibility
status when applying estimates outside our study
population, along with continuing to account for
differences in mortality status and year of initial
enrollment. That refinement, along with the studies
cited earlier that found substantial selection in
the newly eligible population, gives us greater
confidence in our approach to modeling selection
for this population. We will continue looking
for ways in which we can better account for
differences between our study population and the
general MA population in our modeling.

There are four main sources of uncertainty in the
Commission’s estimate of favorable selection. Below,
we discuss those sources. We have tried to make
reasonable modeling assumptions where there is
uncertainty. This year, we incorporated two modeling
updates to address two of the sources of uncertainty
and conducted additional sensitivity analyses; however,
our estimate of favorable selection could be higher or
lower if actual trends differ from those assumptions.
•

•

We have a high degree of confidence in our
estimate of favorable selection for beneficiaries in
our study population before they enter MA (“baseyear selection”). However, there is uncertainty
in our estimate of the change in selection while
our study population was enrolled in MA because
that change cannot be observed directly with
available data and must be estimated using a
different population of beneficiaries who are
enrolled in FFS. We make the best use we can of
available data to estimate the change in selection
by matching our study population to FFS “proxy
groups” using their base selection before entering
MA and their mortality status and dual-eligibility
status during the year of MA enrollment. Our
previous analysis suggests that this portion of the
estimate is conservative because using narrower
proxy groups of FFS beneficiaries who later
enrolled in MA produced larger selection effects
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2025b).
The sensitivity analyses we conducted this year
to assess the impact of matching on additional
characteristics when constructing FFS proxy
groups demonstrated that the estimated change in
selection was robust to alternative specifications.
However, the actual change in selection could be
higher or lower than we estimate.
There is also uncertainty regarding the selection
effect for MA enrollees outside our study
population. Due to a lack of data, we assign a
selection percentage to those beneficiaries using
data from MA beneficiaries in our study population.
Our analysis, described earlier in the appendix,
indicates that assigning selection percentages
that we estimate by using our study population
to the remaining MA population is appropriate

438 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

•

Another source of uncertainty stems from data
limitations that require us to exclude beneficiaries
who lack a full prior year of FFS enrollment
from the FFS comparison population used for
the denominators. That method excludes some
beneficiaries from the FFS comparison group
(denominator) whom studies have demonstrated to
have unfavorable (that is, higher) risk-standardized
spending if they had recently disenrolled from MA
to FFS (Fuglesten Biniek et al. 2024). This suggests
that our FFS comparison group should have slightly
larger risk-standardized spending than what we
currently estimate, and our estimated selection
effects would be slightly larger if FFS beneficiaries
who recently (within 12 months of the target year)
disenrolled from MA were included in our study.
In addition, we also exclude FFS beneficiaries who
recently became eligible for Medicare. Their riskstandardized spending may be higher or lower than
the population of FFS beneficiaries with complete
data who are in our comparison group, so omitting
those beneficiaries may cause our estimate of
favorable selection to be either higher or lower.

•

Finally, there is uncertainty in our estimate of
roughly zero favorable selection for MA enrollees
with ESRD. Because our estimate of favorable
selection is limited by observing plan switching,
it is particularly difficult to estimate favorable
selection for this small population. However,
our analysis using the simple method (detailed
on pp. 379–382 of Chapter 12) suggests that it is
appropriate to estimate a zero-selection effect

for MA enrollees with ESRD, given our limitations.
We include this zero selection effect in our overall
estimate of favorable selection. Because payments
to MA plans for ESRD enrollees account for a

projected 6 percent of total payments to MA plans
in 2026, the inclusion of ESRD enrollees did not
have a large effect on our overall estimate. ■

12-C

T E C H N I C A L

A P P E N D I X

Coding intensity

The Commission’s method for
estimating MA coding intensity
The Commission’s method for estimating coding
intensity is based on the demographic estimate of
coding intensity (DECI) method, originally developed
by Kronick and Chua (2021). In prior years, we applied
the DECI method to estimate coding intensity for
Medicare Advantage (MA) enrollees without endstage renal disease (ESRD) but had not yet analyzed
coding intensity for ESRD enrollees. This year, we
have incorporated enrollees with ESRD in our analysis
of coding intensity. Analyzing coding intensity for
ESRD enrollees must be conducted separately from
analysis of coding intensity for enrollees without ESRD
because payments for ESRD enrollees are adjusted
using separate risk models. The estimates presented in
this chapter reflect our combined estimate of coding
intensity for the overall MA population, including both
enrollees with and without ESRD. We first explain
the methodology used to estimate coding intensity
for beneficiaries without ESRD. Subsequent sections
explain additional analysis for beneficiaries with ESRD
and how we combine estimates for beneficiaries with
and without ESRD to arrive at a final estimate of overall
MA coding intensity.

Estimating coding intensity for MA
enrollees without ESRD
The Commission’s method for estimating coding
intensity for the non-ESRD population uses the
same DECI methodology used last year. The DECI
method implicitly assumes that MA enrollees have
a similar average prevalence of health conditions as
fee-for-service (FFS) Medicare beneficiaries with
similar demographic characteristics. The method first
estimates the ratio of the national average MA CMS
hierarchical condition category (CMS–HCC) risk score
to the national average FFS CMS–HCC risk score. This
ratio reflects differences in risk scores due to coding
intensity and true differences in health condition
prevalence between the two populations. Second, the
method estimates the ratio of the national average MA
demographic-only risk score to the national average
FFS demographic-only risk score. The DECI estimate
is the result of dividing the MA-to-FFS CMS–HCC
risk-score ratio by the MA-to-FFS demographic-only
risk-score ratio. Therefore, the DECI method attributes

higher demographic-adjusted MA risk scores relative
to FFS to higher MA coding intensity rather than worse
health acuity or complexity among MA enrollees than
FFS beneficiaries. Figure 12-C1 (p. 442) shows how the
DECI method estimates coding intensity.
National average CMS hierarchical condition
category risk scores

We use data from the Medicare common enrollment
files and risk-score files to construct national average
CMS–HCC risk scores for the MA and FFS populations.
To define the two populations, we identify monthly
MA or FFS enrollment using the plan identifier in the
Medicare common enrollment file, and we require
all MA and FFS beneficiaries to have both Part A and
Part B using the “Medicare enrollment code” data
field. We exclude beneficiaries residing in Puerto
Rico based on their monthly county of residence.
(Starting with our March 2025 report, we excluded
beneficiaries residing in Puerto Rico from our codingintensity estimate because Puerto Rico’s Medicaid
program does not use the full-, partial-, or no-benefit
structure, which is necessary for our coding-intensity
estimation method.) Then we use monthly indicators
in risk-score data to exclude beneficiary months in
which an ESRD risk score would be applied and to
assign “new-enrollee” and institutional risk scores as
appropriate. For all remaining months, we assign the
appropriate community-model risk score using the
monthly Medicare–Medicaid dual status code from the
enrollment file to adjust for full, partial, or no Medicaid
benefits, and we use the beneficiary’s age from riskscore data to determine aged or disabled status. In
each year, we use the version of the risk model or
blend of versions that was used for payment to MA
plans. Finally, we aggregate the monthly risk scores
to calculate national average MA and FFS CMS–HCC
scores for the four groups identified below.
When developing this method, we benchmarked our
estimate of the national average risk score for all FFS
beneficiaries in 2019 (including those with Part A only)
of 1.0682 against the national average published by CMS
of 1.0685 to confirm that we were assigning risk scores
appropriately. (When estimating the effects of coding
intensity, we exclude beneficiaries with Part A–only
coverage.) Our estimate of the average risk score for all
FFS beneficiaries was similarly close to CMS’s published
results for 2017 and 2018.

National average demographic risk scores

We calibrate an annual risk model based only on
demographic characteristics for FFS beneficiaries with
both Part A and Part B (excluding beneficiaries with
ESRD) by including age category (from the CMS–HCC
model), sex, Medicaid eligibility (full benefits, partial
benefits, or no benefits), and institutional status (see
text box, p. 444). We use the same enrollment and riskscore indicator variables as in the CMS–HCC risk-score
analysis described above.
We calculate monthly Medicare spending by summing
the annual spending amounts in the Medicare
beneficiary summary file (excluding beneficiaries with
any hospice use) and dividing by the months of Part A
and Part B enrollment in the year. We calculate a single
risk model (with dollar-value age and sex coefficients)
with fully interacted indicators for beneficiary age
category × sex × Medicaid or institutional status (if
not in institutional status, we identify eligibility for
full Medicaid benefits, partial Medicaid benefits, and
no Medicaid benefits). The dollar coefficients are then
converted to risk scores by dividing each coefficient
by the average spending for all beneficiaries in the
analytic sample. Note that this step reflects a technical

442 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

revision to the approach used in prior years. The
change resulted in our 2023 coding-intensity estimate
being 0.3 percentage points lower than under the prior
method, and the effect was smaller on earlier years
of analysis. Previously, we divided the coefficients for
each group by the average spending for beneficiaries
in that group. Given this change, the average risk score
for the whole model is 1.0. Finally, we apply those
demographic risk scores to MA and FFS beneficiaries
with both Part A and Part B and aggregate them to
national averages for each of the four groups identified
below.
Calculate coding-intensity accounting for
Medicaid eligibility and institutional status

For MA and FFS, we calculate average CMS–HCC and
demographic risk scores separately for continuing (not
new-enrollee) beneficiary months with institutional
status, full Medicaid benefits, partial Medicaid benefits,
and no Medicaid benefits. We then calculate average
CMS–HCC and demographic risk scores for continuing
enrollees by using the share of MA enrollees in each
of the four groups as weights for both the MA and
FFS averages. Then we calculate a DECI estimate for
continuing enrollees using the formula in Figure 12-C1.

Finally, we calculate a DECI estimate for all enrollees
by adding together the continuing-enrollee DECI
estimate multiplied by the risk-score-weighted share
of continuing MA enrollees and the new-enrollee
DECI estimate multiplied by the risk-score-weighted
share of new enrollees in the analysis. This final step
constrains risk scores for new enrollees to have no
coding intensity because their risk scores are not based
on diagnostic coding. We use a risk-score-weighted
new and continuing enrollment share to combine new

and continuing enrollee DECI estimates because those
weights reflect the share of MA payment associated
with each group. Table 12-C1 shows an example DECI
calculation for 2024 that accounts for Medicaideligibility and institutional status in MA and FFS.
Calculating the effect of CMS’s coding
adjustment

CMS’s adjustment reduces MA risk scores by 5.9
percent. To account for this adjustment, we multiply
