443

Sensitivity estimates of alternative demographic risk-model specifications

A

pplying the demographic estimate of coding
intensity (DECI) method involves calibrating
a risk model based only on demographic
characteristics. That model is used to construct an
alternative measure of Medicare Advantage (MA)
and fee-for-service (FFS) population-level risk that,
unlike the CMS hierarchical condition category
(HCC) model, is not affected by coding intensity.
The ratio of MA and FFS demographic risk scores
is used to measure the relative risk of the two
populations, and any population-level differences
in risk scores that are greater than what would
be predicted using the demographic model are
attributed to coding intensity.
Because the demographic risk model is how the
DECI method controls for differences in the
MA and FFS populations, including additional
demographic characteristics that are not affected
by plan coding may improve the model’s ability
to measure differences in population risk.
Commissioners and other researchers have
provided suggestions about other demographic
characteristics that could be included in the
demographic model. This year, we modeled
alternative demographic risk models to assess the
robustness of our coding-intensity estimates.
We tested three alternative model specifications
that add race, original reason for entitlement code
(OREC), or both to the demographic risk model.
Race was identified for each beneficiary based
on the RTI race code in the common Medicare
enrollment file and defined as Black, Hispanic,
non-Hispanic White, or other (including Asian/
Pacific Islander, American Indian/Alaskan native,
other, and unknown categories). For original
reason for entitlement, we combined entitlement
based on ESRD and disability with entitlement
based on ESRD to create three categories: aged,
disabled, or ESRD. For each alternative model
specification, we started with our primary
demographic model, which has 96 fully interacted
variables: 12 age categories × 2 sexes × 4 statuses

444 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

(eligible for full, partial, or no Medicaid benefits
or in institutional status). The first alternative
model added race by fully interacting the four
race categories with each of the 96 variables in
our original model for a total of 384 variables. The
second alternative model added original reason
for entitlement by including indicator variables for
OREC being disabled and OREC being ESRD (OREC
being aged is the reference category) for a total of
98 variables. We did not fully interact OREC with
the other demographic variables because of the
small number of beneficiaries with OREC being
ESRD. The third alternative model added both race
and original reason for entitlement by starting
with the first alternative model that has race fully
interacted with our original model variables and
adding two indicator variables for OREC being
disabled and OREC being ESRD, for a total of 386
variables.
We found that adding race notably increased the
accuracy of the demographic–only risk model as
measured by the R2 statistic, but adding original
reason for entitlement did not improve model
accuracy. The R2 statistic is an indicator of the
proportion of variance in the dependent variable
(annual Medicare spending for all models in this
analysis) that can be explained by the independent
variables in the model.
Coding-intensity estimates for all alternative
demographic models were within 1 percentage
point of coding intensity when using our original
demographic risk model for both V24 and V28 risk
models in 2024. The largest difference in coding
intensity occurred for the alternative demographic
model that adds race and had coding-intensity
estimates that were 0.7 percentage points or 0.6
percentage points larger than estimates for our
original demographic model for V24 and V28 risk
models, respectively. Overall, we found the two
alternative specifications had little effect on our
estimates of coding intensity. ■

our estimate of coding intensity by 1 minus 5.9 percent.
For example, for 2024 we multiply 1.147 percent (average
MA risk score due to coding intensity if average FFS
risk score is 1.0) by 94.1 percent and subtract 1 to get
a coding-intensity estimate after adjustment of 7.9
percent (last row of Table 12-C1, p. 443).
To align with our overall comparison of spending on
MA relative to FFS, we estimate MA coding intensity
relative to FFS risk scores (i.e., MA risk scores are
14.7 percent higher than FFS risk scores due to
coding intensity). The denominator for CMS’s coding
adjustment, however, is the average MA risk score.
Because the average MA risk score is larger than
the average FFS risk score, the two percentages are
not equivalent. Based on our MA coding-intensity
estimate of 14.7 percent above FFS, we estimate that
CMS’s coding adjustment would have had to reduce
MA risk scores by 12.8 percent to fully account for
the effects of coding intensity in 2024. We calculate
this amount as (1 – (1.0 / 1.147)) = 0.128 or 12.8 percent.
However, coding intensity varies significantly across
MA organizations. As a result, CMS’s across-the-board
adjustment for coding intensity, which reduces all MA
risk scores by the same amount, generates inequity
across organizations by reducing net revenue for
plans with lower coding intensity and allowing other
plans to retain a significant amount of revenue from
higher coding intensity. Increasing CMS’s acrossthe-board coding adjustment to 12.8 percent would
increase the penalty for plans with lower coding
intensity while still not addressing the effects of
coding intensity for the organizations with higher
coding levels. A policy addressing coding intensity
more fairly would reduce risk scores more for plans
with the highest coding intensity.

First, beneficiaries in dialysis status have a model
calibrated on FFS claims for beneficiaries in dialysis
status that aligns with the state-based dialysis payment
rates that MA plans receive. Second, Medicare uses a
risk model for beneficiaries in transplant status for the
three months following transplantation. That model
includes one variable that varies depending on the
month after transplantation (first, second, or third).
Transplant risk scores do not vary by demographic
characteristics or by diagnosis codes, so there can
be no differences in coding intensity between the
MA and FFS populations in transplant status. Last,
Medicare uses a separate risk model for beneficiaries
with ESRD who are more than three months
posttransplant. The postgraft risk model is adapted
from the non-ESRD risk model and is modified to
remove conditions that are common to all beneficiaries
in postgraft status and adds several interacted
indicators to capture differences related to being 4 to
9 months posttransplant versus 10 months or more
posttransplant, aged versus disabled status, eligibility
for full versus partial versus no Medicaid benefits,
and original reason for entitlement being disability.
The postgraft risk model has separate segments for
beneficiaries residing in the community or in an
institutional setting for longer than 90 days.
Below, we describe steps taken to estimate coding
intensity for beneficiaries in dialysis status and those
in postgraft status. Beneficiaries in transplant status
cannot have coding intensity because diagnoses are
not used in that model, so we assign them a codingintensity estimate of zero.
Estimating coding intensity for beneficiaries in
dialysis status

To estimate coding intensity for beneficiaries in dialysis
status, we applied our method for calculating coding
intensity by:

Estimating coding intensity for MA
enrollees with ESRD
Beginning this year, we conducted a separate analysis
of coding intensity for enrollees with ESRD using the
Commission’s DECI method. Payments to MA plans for
beneficiaries with ESRD are made using a different risk
model than for beneficiaries without ESRD, and the
ESRD risk model is different for beneficiaries in dialysis,
transplant, or postgraft status. Because different
segments of the ESRD population have their payments
adjusted using different risk models, we assessed
coding intensity separately for the three segments.

•

limiting our analysis to beneficiary months in
dialysis status for both MA and FFS populations
when calculating national average HCC scores and

•

including only beneficiaries in dialysis status for all
12 months of the calibration year when calibrating
the demographic risk model.

The method follows the same calculation as for
beneficiaries without ESRD shown in Table 12-C1
(p. 443). We estimate that coding intensity for

Estimating coding intensity for beneficiaries in
postgraft status

The method follows the same calculation as for
beneficiaries without ESRD shown in Table 12-C1 (p.
443). We estimate that coding intensity for beneficiaries
in postgraft status ranged from 4 percent to 14 percent
over the period 2015 to 2024 before accounting for the
effect of the coding adjustment (Table 12-C2).

To estimate coding intensity for beneficiaries in
postgraft status, we applied our method for calculating
coding intensity by:
•

limiting our analysis to beneficiary months in
postgraft status for both MA and FFS populations
when calculating national average HCC scores,

•

including only beneficiaries in postgraft status
for all 12 months of the calibration year when
calibrating the demographic risk model, and

446 Th e M e d i c a re Ad va nta g e prog ra m : Sta tus repor t

combining age categories with small numbers,
which resulted in two categories that are broader
than the categories otherwise used in the risk
model: 0 through 45 and 85+.

Estimating coding intensity for the full ESRD
population

To combine estimates for the dialysis, transplant
(assigned to be zero), and postgraft populations, we
calculated a weight for each estimate based on total
payments to MA plans for that population. For example,

we estimated total spending for the dialysis population
as the number of months that MA enrollees were in
dialysis status × the average risk score for the dialysis
population × the monthly denominator for the dialysis
risk model ($7,271 for 2024). The monthly denominators
for transplant and postgraft risk models are $7,271 and
$874, respectively. We estimate that payments to MA
plans for enrollees with dialysis reflect 89.1 percent
of all payments for enrollees with ESRD. The share of
payments was 9.2 percent for enrollees with transplant
status and 1.7 percent for enrollees with postgraft
status.
Table 12-C2 shows how we calculated the overall ESRD
coding-intensity estimate for 2015 through 2026. To
project dialysis coding intensity for 2025 and 2026, we
used the average dialysis coding intensity for 2022,
2023, and 2024, representing coding-intensity patterns
after the end of the coronavirus pandemic and after
the implementation of policies under the 21st Century
Cures Act of 2016. To project postgraft coding intensity
for 2025 and 2026, we used the average annual trend
from 2022 through 2024, or 0.7 percentage points per
year. Projecting changes in coding intensity over time
is challenging. The actual coding intensity in 2025 and
2026 may be higher or lower than what we project. We
used estimates since the implementation of the 21st
Century Cures Act to try to limit projection errors.
Finally, we note that none of the risk models used for
the different groups of beneficiaries with ESRD are
affected by the implementation of the V28 risk model
that is used for payments to MA plans for beneficiaries
without ESRD.

Estimating overall MA coding intensity
To combine our estimates of coding intensity for MA
enrollees with and without ESRD, we weight each
estimate based on the share of payments to MA plans
for enrollees with and without ESRD.
To estimate the impact of coding intensity on
payments to MA plans, we first estimate residual MA
coding intensity after applying the coding-intensity
adjustment, which reduces most MA risk scores by 5.9
percent. The coding adjustment applies to risk scores
for beneficiaries without ESRD and to beneficiaries
with ESRD who are in postgraft status. The coding
adjustment does not apply to dialysis or transplant
risk scores, which use a different denominator during
risk-model calibration. We then repeat the same
process of aggregating these postadjustment codingintensity estimates for ESRD beneficiaries and MA
enrollees overall. As noted earlier, the denominator
for our coding-intensity estimate is the average FFS
risk score, whereas the denominator for CMS’s coding
adjustment is the average MA risk score. Because the
average MA risk score is larger than the average FFS
risk score, accounting for the effect of the coding
adjustment reduces our coding-intensity estimates (for
the populations to whom the adjustment is applied) by
more than 5.9 percentage points. ■

1

2

3

CMS includes FFS-claim administrative costs in MA
benchmarks, which account for about 0.20 percent of FFS
spending (Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2023a,
Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services 2021). Expenses for
FFS-claim administration are included in our comparison of
FFS spending with MA payments and differ from the expenses
found in Medicare’s Trustees’ reports, which include the
administration and oversight of the MA program and the
enrollment of all Medicare providers (which is required for
contracting with MA plans). The Medicare Trustees reported
that administrative expenses (including those for MA
enrollees) accounted for 1.04 percent of CMS’s total Medicare
benefit costs in 2020 (Boards of Trustees 2021).
MA enrollees could have lower cost-sharing liability in
FFS if they do not use many services or if they obtain a
Medigap policy when they switch to FFS. While there is
no maximum out of pocket limit in FFS like there is in
MA, Medigap policies limit the out-of-pocket expense by
covering an enrollee’s cost-sharing. Beneficiaries have
a federal guaranteed-issue right to a Medigap policy
with no medical underwriting, even if they have a
preexisting condition, if they purchase it during the
six-month Medigap open enrollment period that
begins on the first day a beneficiary is both 65 years
old and enrolled in Medicare Part B. Only four states
(Connecticut, Maine, Massachusetts, and New York) require
guaranteed-issue rights to Medigap for aged (65 and over)
beneficiaries in FFS Medicare, regardless of medical history,
meaning that insurers cannot deny a Medigap policy to
applicants based on preexisting conditions (Boccuti et al.
2018). MA enrollees joining an MA plan when they are first
eligible for Part B or who leave FFS and a Medigap policy to
join an MA plan have what are known as federal “trial
rights” during the first year of MA enrollment, when
they may return to FFS during the first year of MA
enrollment and purchase a Medigap plan with guaranteedissue rights. There are also limited circumstances (e.g.,
a beneficiary moves outside of their MA plan’s service
area) when beneficiaries have additional federal or
state guaranteed-issue rights.
Because private FFS (PFFS) and medical savings account
(MSA) plans have a very small number of enrollees, we do
not describe them further. PFFS plans that operate without
a network are limited to counties where fewer than two
network-based plans are offered (32 counties, or about 1
percent of counties for 2026); by the end of 2025, PFFS plans
covered about 40,000 beneficiaries. MSA plans combine a
high deductible and a medical savings account, and by the
end of 2025 they covered about 1,400 beneficiaries.

One study found that additional benefits and limits on outof-pocket spending were the two leading reasons that MA
enrollees chose an MA plan (Leonard et al. 2022).

Part B spending represents about 60 percent of all FFS
Medicare spending (which is assumed to be the same share
of spending on Part B services by MA plans). Twenty-five
percent of Part B spending is financed through premiums
paid by all Medicare Part B enrollees. The estimate does not
account for the reduction in Part B premiums that is offered
by some MA plans as a supplemental benefit.

enrollees with ESRD is described later, in the section titled
“Medicare payments and MA plan costs for enrollees with

If a plan bids above the benchmark, the plan’s base rate is
set at the benchmark and enrollees must pay a premium (in
addition to the usual Part B premium) equal to the difference
between the bid and the benchmark. For 2026, almost 100
percent of plans bid below their benchmark.

Examples of non-Medicare-covered supplemental benefits
include dental, vision, and hearing coverage. Plans can offer
such benefits on a mandatory or optional basis. Mandatory
supplemental benefits are automatically included in the
benefit package for all enrollees in a plan. Plans may use
rebates to finance mandatory supplemental benefits and may
charge enrollees a premium to cover costs not covered by the
rebate. Additionally, plans may offer optional supplemental
benefits. Plans are not permitted to apply rebate dollars
toward optional supplemental benefits; enrollees pay an
additional premium to access these benefits. Optional
supplemental benefits cannot include reduced cost sharing
for Medicare Part A and Part B services.

9

Benchmarks are calculated using FFS spending for all
Medicare beneficiaries, including those with both Part A
and Part B coverage and those with only Part A or Part B.
For Puerto Rico, CMS uses data for FFS beneficiaries who
have both Part A and Part B enrollment. (This is because
Medicare beneficiaries in Puerto Rico must opt in to Part B
coverage, in contrast to other beneficiaries who are generally
automatically enrolled in Part B unless they opt out.) In our
March 2017 report to the Congress, we recommended that
CMS change the calculation to include FFS spending for only

those beneficiaries with both Part A and Part B coverage
(that is, expenditures for only those beneficiaries eligible to
enroll in MA plans) (Medicare Payment Advisory Commission
2017). This change would make the assumptions about FFS
spending in the calculation of MA benchmarks and payments
more reflective of the MA-eligible population.
10 CMS determines FFS spending quartiles using their projected
FFS spending from the prior year. For example, benchmark
quartiles in 2026 were determined using FFS spending from
the 2025 MA ratebook.
11 An MA plan’s benchmark may be as high as 125 percent of
CMS’s local-area projected FFS spending. The ACA caps
any county’s benchmark at the higher of (1) its pre-ACA
level, projected into the future with a legislatively modified
national growth factor, or (2) 100 percent of its estimated FFS
spending in the current year. Our March 2016 report to the
Congress provides more detail on double-bonus counties and
benchmark-growth caps. In that report, we recommended
eliminating the double bonuses as well as the benchmarkgrowth caps, which limited the benchmarks in many counties
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2016c).
12 Before 2022, MA plans also submitted diagnostic information
through the Risk Adjustment Processing System (RAPS). The
use of RAPS data was phased out from 2016 through 2021,
except for contracts in the Program of All-Inclusive Care for
the Elderly, which continue to use a combination of RAPS
and encounter data as the source of diagnostic data for risk
scores.
13 Other possible sources of diagnostic information—such
as encounters for home health services, skilled nursing,
ambulatory surgery, durable medical equipment, lab and
imaging tests, and hospice services—are not used to
determine payment through the risk-adjustment model for
several reasons. First, CMS has found that adding diagnoses
from these sources does not improve the model’s ability to
predict medical expenditures. Second, concerns exist about
the reliability of diagnoses from providers with less clinical
training (e.g., home health and durable medical equipment
providers). Third, a high proportion of reported diagnoses
from certain settings (e.g., lab and imaging tests) are used to
rule out having a diagnosis (Pope et al. 2004).
14 CMS tied its decision to end the MMP demonstration to
such factors as the Congress’s decision to make dualeligible SNPs (D–SNPs) permanent, greater flexibility for
MA plans in offering supplemental benefits, and regulatory
changes that incorporate some MMP features into D–
SNPs. The Commission’s review of evaluations of the MMP
demonstrations, as well as CMS’s, found that they tended
to result in statistically significant increases in Medicare

spending and had mixed effects on service use (Center for
Medicare & Medicaid Innovation 2024, Medicare Payment
Advisory Commission 2024c).
15 In 2025, 16 percent of MA enrollees and 18 percent of MAeligible enrollees resided in rural areas.
16 Our measurement of beneficiary access to plans uses 2026
plan bids and July 2025 county-level enrollment for the
Medicare population with both Part A and Part B coverage.
Plans are included in a county only if they project enrolling at
least one beneficiary in that county.
17 All beneficiaries enrolling in Medicare Part B, regardless of
their decision to receive benefits through FFS or MA, are
required to pay the Medicare Part B premium. Some MA plans
use rebate dollars to pay a portion of their members’ Part B
premium as a supplemental benefit. Beneficiaries enrolling
in Part D may pay a separate Part D premium, although MA
Prescription Drug plans may use rebate dollars to reduce
the amount a beneficiary pays for drug coverage under the
plan. Plans bidding above the local benchmark or offering
more supplemental benefits than can be financed by the
plan rebate charge enrollees an additional plan premium. We
refer to plans that do not charge a separate plan premium
(including any Part D premium) as “zero-premium” plans. In
2026, 93 percent of MA-eligible beneficiaries had an available
zero-premium local PPO plan, and 97 percent of beneficiaries
had an available zero-premium HMO plan.
18 From 2011 to 2018, CMS required that insurers with multiple
MA plans in the same county had to demonstrate that their
plans had “meaningful differences” from each other. CMS
only approved bids if the plan benefit package or plan cost
structures (e.g., premiums, cost-sharing, formulary structure,
benefits offered) were substantially different from those of
other plans offered by the same organization.
19 In most cases, beneficiaries without an available MA plan
must enroll in FFS. In some cases, beneficiaries who meet

available conventional plan. For example, in about 1 percent of

plan but not an available conventional plan. In addition, some
beneficiaries may have an available employer plan. Further,
some beneficiaries may have an available non-MA private

20 In 2026, about 1 percent of eligible beneficiaries have
an available MA plan offered by only one organization
(unchanged from 2025).

benchmark (after both have been adjusted to reflect a person

of average risk), the plan’s base payment rate is set at the
benchmark and enrollees must pay a premium (in addition

22 In previous years, MedPAC has reported rebates only for
plans that included Part D coverage (i.e., MA Prescription
Drug plans (MA–PDs), excluding employer plans). This
year, we include plans that do not offer Part D coverage
(sometimes referred to as “MA-only” plans). Rebates for the
combined group of MA–PDs and MA-only plans tend to be
very similar to the rebates for MA–PDs since the vast majority
of MA plans are MA–PDs. For 2026, 92 percent of MA plans
are MA–PDs, and 97 percent of MA enrollees are projected
to be in such plans. Additionally, in our previous reports to
the Congress, we have typically used enrollment projections
from plan bids as the basis for the enrollment weighting
when calculating average MA rebates. In this year’s report,
we use plans’ actual enrollment to calculate average rebates
for 2015 through 2025. The weights are based on non-ESRD
member months since plans do not receive rebates for
members with ESRD. Non-ESRD enrollment is calculated
by subtracting ESRD enrollment from total enrollment. For
2025, data on ESRD enrollment were not available at the

bids to account for ESRD enrollment. Figures for 2026 are
weighted using enrollment projections from plans’ 2026 bids.
For almost all of the years we analyzed, averages calculated
using actual enrollment were slightly higher (generally within
1 percent or 2 percent) of those calculated using projected
enrollment.
23 We exclude employer group plans because they do not
submit bids and so do not receive a rebate in the same
manner as bidding plans. Instead, starting in 2019, CMS began
paying employer group plans based on the bidding behavior
of non–employer group plans in the prior year: The employer
group plans receive a base payment rate that is based on
the average bid-to-benchmark ratio of non–employer group
plans (by quartile of FFS spending and plan type), plus a
share of the difference between the base payment rate and a
county-specific benchmark.
24 Part D benefit enhancements include, for instance, lowering

26 In 2026, of the rebate dollars plans project using to reduce

27 The plan liability for the MOOP cap is not analogous to the
amount spent by FFS beneficiaries who have out-of-pocket
costs that exceed the MA MOOP limit. Most beneficiaries in
FFS Medicare have supplemental insurance and are unlikely
to have cost-sharing expenses that exceed the OOP cap for
MA enrollees. In addition, MA enrollees are prohibited from
purchasing Medigap coverage because MA plans are expected
to provide cost-sharing reductions as supplemental benefits
in lieu of Medigap coverage. The MOOP liability reported
in plan bids is less than half of the 2.8 percent MOOP value
estimated by Wakely in a report commissioned by AHIP,
which used FFS claims data to estimate the MOOP value.
The Wakely estimate did not account for the lower MOOP
liability accrued by MA plans because of out-of-network
care, claims denials, plan switching during the year, and costsharing reductions that are financed through plan rebates. In
addition, the Wakely estimate did not consider cost-sharing
reductions for FFS beneficiaries with supplemental coverage
that would have reduced the MOOP liability in FFS. Further,
Wakely did not adjust for Medicare Part A and Part B services
that were financed by FFS on behalf of MA enrollees (e.g.,
Medicare services during hospice election). Wakely counted
these services toward the MOOP estimate even though these
types of services were not financed by MA plans.
28 In contrast to prior years, we now include out-of-network
MOOP liability in our estimates. While MA plans have
enrolled a growing number of beneficiaries with ESRD who,
on average, have higher expenditures relative to beneficiaries
without ESRD, MA plans’ projected MOOP liability remained
at 1 percent of projected payments annually from 2023 to
2026.

30 The Commission previously estimated that Medicare would

We estimate that Medicare will pay approximately $71 billion
and $13 billion in rebates to non–employer group plans and
employer plans, respectively, in 2025. Rebates paid to non–
employer group plans were estimated using rebate amounts
from MA bids and monthly enrollment data for non–employer
group plans. Employer plans do not submit bids. Instead,
starting in 2019, CMS began paying employer plans based
on the bidding behavior of non–employer group plans in the
prior year. Because employer plans are mostly PPOs, their
payment in 2025 largely reflects the average bidding behavior
of non–employer group PPOs in 2024. We use employerplan enrollment data for 2025 and apply 2025 employer-plan
payment rates (adjusted to reflect recent employer-plan riskscore trends) to estimate Medicare’s payments to employer

the same method CMS uses to determine employer-plan

plans (based on the average bid-to-benchmark ratio for non–

quartile) is multiplied by the plan-specific rebate percentage
(based on the plan’s star rating) and the risk score.

34 MA plans are required to maintain an MLR of 85 percent (42
CFR Sec. 422.2410(b)). The MLR is the minimum percentage
of revenue that an insurer is required to spend on benefits
for their members. An MLR requirement of 85 percent means
that the insurer is required to spend at least 85 percent of
their revenue on care for their enrollees and can use no more
than 15 percent of their revenue for administrative costs and
profit. MLR requirements for MA organizations are monitored
at the contract level. The numerator of the MLR includes
incurred expenses for members’ medical claims, including
both Medicare and non-Medicare services (i.e., basic and
supplemental benefits).
35 MA plans can include more generous supplemental benefits
than can be fully financed by the rebate they receive from
Medicare. In such cases, plans can charge enrollees an
additional premium. Further, plans can offer “optional”
supplemental benefits, which enrollees can access by paying
an additional premium.
36 The MLR reporting instructions label this category “NonPrimarily Health Related Benefits – Other” but do not

interpretation of the reporting instructions is that non–
primarily health-related benefits offered under the
demonstration would be reported in this category and that
those benefits should likely account for the majority of the
reported spending. However, it is possible that some plans

32 We estimate that total rebate spending for 2025 will be $84

40 We note that our 2026 estimate of spending on MA relative to
the amount Medicare would have spent for comparable FFS
beneficiaries (114 percent) reflects some changes from the
method used in the Commission’s 2025 comparison (reported
to be 120 percent for the year 2025 in our March 2025 report)
(Medicare Payment Advisory Commission 2025b). First, in
the 2025 report, we did not include the ESRD population
in our projections because we had not yet constructed the
analytic framework for including that population. For 2026,
we included the ESRD population in our estimates. We
also made technical updates to refine our methodology for
estimating favorable selection; those updates resulted in
similar projections in our estimates of favorable selection—
continuing to account for 11 percentage points of the
difference in spending. Second, we revised our estimated
effect of phasing in the V28 model using newly available V28
risk scores from 2024. We also updated our estimates to
reflect the slower trend in coding intensity between 2023
and 2024 that we observed using the newly available data
from 2024, and we revised our method of estimating coding
intensity to use a single denominator for the demographic
risk model. The net effect of newer data (including access to
V28 risk scores) was –3.9 percentage points on our projected
coding-intensity estimate for 2025. To project coding
intensity from 2025 to 2026, we reduced the 2025 projection
for coding intensity by 2.3 percentage points to reflect the
final phase-in of the V28 risk model and an additional year
of coding intensity trend (see text box “Updates to codingintensity estimates reflect newly available data and other
factors,” p. 392–393, for more details).

42 Multiple studies have found that plan bids tend to decrease
as plan benchmarks decrease or plan bids tend to increase
as plan benchmarks increase (Cabral et al. 2018, Medicare

43 Another study assessing the effect of MA enrollment on
medical spending relied on a very small sample of 541 MA
enrollees from one insurer and 541 enrollees in Medicare FFS
who had Medigap coverage from the same insurer (Schwartz
et al. 2021). The small sample led to imprecise estimates with
the 95 percent confidence interval spanning a spending effect
between –$282 and $0 per beneficiary per month. Schwartz
and colleagues acknowledged that the true effect could be
substantially smaller or larger than estimated and may not be
generalizable. The authors used a difference-in-differences
design with propensity score matching to control for
preexisting differences between the MA treatment and FFS
control group and found that MA enrollment reduced medical
spending by 36 percent. The result was driven primarily by
reducing inpatient spending on the 74 stays used by the MA
population relative to the 56 stays for the FFS population.
However, the pre-Medicare inpatient utilization patterns
appeared to differ between the treatment and control groups,
raising questions about whether the FFS control group
provided an accurate counterfactual estimate for the MA
treatment group. The study population also differed from the
overall MA population in several ways, potentially limiting
the study’s generalizability. For example, individuals in the
matched MA treatment and FFS control groups had a much
higher prevalence of chronic conditions and were much more
likely to reside in the Northeast compared with the insurer’s
overall MA membership of similar ages. Moreover, this study
included members of the treatment and control group from
disparate geographical locations but did not standardize
Medicare rates.
44 The two recent white papers that examined the differences
in spending for about 10,000 newly eligible Medicare
beneficiaries (Inovalon 2025, Teigland et al. 2025a) may
overstate the amount of MA spending reductions in three
key ways. First, both studies required two consecutive years
of enrollment in FFS or the same MA plan, which potentially
creates some favorable selection into MA during the study
period by removing beneficiaries who switched into a
different plan or program. Beneficiaries who switched from
MA to FFS or switched MA plans may have had relatively
high risk-adjusted spending, and beneficiaries who switched
from FFS to MA may have had relatively low risk-adjusted
spending. Second, both papers remove decedents, and
the effects of MA on reducing decedent spending may be

particularly dampened (especially given that MA is not
responsible for spending during hospice election). Third, it
is unclear whether the data repository used in both white
papers is able to capture the completeness of MA utilization
data—particularly for subcapitated arrangements with
providers— better than the CMS’s MA encounter data. To the
extent that FFS data are more complete, the differences in
spending between MA and FFS may be overstated.
45 The 2025 Inovalon white paper reported higher pre-Medicare
disease burden and mortality risk in the MA population
compared with FFS (Inovalon 2025). However, these findings
conflict with prior research, including those by Inovalon
researchers. One recent white paper found that, immediately
before Medicare eligibility, beneficiaries going into MA had
less of a disease burden than their FFS enrollee counterparts;
the researchers found that MA enrollees had 10 percent lower
pre-Medicare risk scores and 12 percent lower Charlson
Comorbidity Index scores. In addition, the researchers
found that MA enrollees were less likely to have cancer,
joint issues, and heart issues before enrolling in Medicare
(Teigland et al. 2025b). One peer-reviewed study found that
newly eligible beneficiaries who initially enrolled in MA had
adjusted mortality rates that were 30 percent lower than FFS
beneficiaries in their first year of Medicare (Newhouse et al.
2019).
46 Jung and colleagues’ recent study found a 13 percent
reduction in spending for MA enrollees under specifications
that reduced individual risk scores by excluding diagnoses
found only in chart reviews or health risk assessments
and adjusted for differences in beneficiary characteristics
using a regression estimate (Jung et al. 2025). However,
this estimate may overstate the amount of MA spending
reductions in three key ways. First, the study adjusted for
MA coding intensity by removing chart reviews and health
risk assessments from the calculation of MA risk scores.
However, our analyses and analysis from Paul Jacobs show
that chart reviews and health risk assessments only account
for about half of the estimated difference in coding between
MA and FFS (Jacobs 2024). Second, Jung and colleagues
controlled for potential beneficiary selection by matching
on observable beneficiary characteristics (e.g., geography,
risk score, race, dual-eligibility status) and risk of mortality.
Our estimate of favorable selection suggests that selection
persists even after adjusting for observable beneficiary
characteristics and mortality differences. Third, Jung and
colleagues’ results include greater MA spending reductions
in earlier years (relative to the later years in their study),
which may result from less complete encounter data during
those years. These three effects may be somewhat offset by
the authors’ exclusion of skilled nursing facility and home
health spending, which may have relatively high reductions in
spending when beneficiaries are enrolled in MA.

our functioning-graft study population’s inpatient hospital
spending. Second, we remove direct graduate medical
education and kidney-acquisition costs using the passthrough amounts in inpatient hospital claims for our study
population. Third, we remove all hospice spending for our
study population, and we remove the months during hospice
when calculating our monthly spending average.
59 For the functioning-graft population, we use a similar
framework as CMS for applying credibility adjustments
to counties with less than 12,000 FFS enrollment months.
For these counties, we blend spending in the county with
spending at the state level. County spending is weighted by
county enrollment, and spending in the state is weighted by
12,000 minus county enrollment months.
60 We also exclude beneficiaries for whom Medicare is a
secondary payer in our comparisons using historical data.
61 For Puerto Rico, CMS uses data for FFS beneficiaries who
have both Part A and Part B enrollment. This is because
Medicare beneficiaries in Puerto Rico must opt in to Part B
coverage, in contrast to other beneficiaries who are generally
automatically enrolled in Part B unless they opt out.
62 Our estimates of the base-spending comparison for the years
before 2016 continue to rely on CMS’s projections of FFS
spending (including adjustments that eliminate the effect of
the sustainable growth rate) because those were the data we

used under an older version of our methodology when those
estimates were first published in earlier reports. We have not
updated those estimates because our analysis indicates that
results would be similar under our updated methodology
that uses historical data. A more detailed description of the
change in methodology for the base payment comparison
can be found in the Commission’s March 2023 report to
the Congress, where it is described as a change from the
prospective method to the retrospective method (Medicare
Payment Advisory Commission 2023c).
63 The Commission’s March 2023 report details our
methodology for comparing our estimates using CMS’s
projections with historical spending for MA-eligible
beneficiaries (Medicare Payment Advisory Commission
2023c). That report describes several adjustments that should
be made when making this comparison. A 2024 analysis
by Wakely that was commissioned by AHIP estimated the
raw average spending difference between all Medicare
beneficiaries and the subset of Medicare beneficiaries with
both Part A and Part B coverage (Wakely 2024). This method
is inadequate for comparisons of MA payment differences
because CMS’s payment methodology accounts for (among
other things) Medicare as a secondary payer, the geographic
differences in risk-standardized FFS spending, the geographic
distribution of MA enrollees, and nonclaims FFS spending.
The Commission’s comparisons adjust for these differences,
including the use of county-level FFS-spending data and
risk scores, MA enrollment, MA risk scores, and MA payment
rates.
64 We also report estimates from a simpler, more suggestive
method that focuses on the selection percentage of MA
entrants who recently switched from FFS compared with
beneficiaries who remained in FFS. The advantages of that
simpler method are that it can more easily be compared with
estimates from the literature that use similar methods, and
we can compute them for more years of data (going back to
2007).
65 Our estimates account for the incentives that beneficiaries
have to switch from FFS to MA because of the MOOP limit.
However, MOOP limits do not appear to be an important
factor in MA plan selection. For example, one study found
that more generous OOP maximums did not result in
enrollment gains in 2022 (Cates et al. 2022). Instead, the
study found that lower premiums and a higher prevalence
of supplemental benefits were associated with plans that
experienced enrollment growth. This finding is consistent
with prior research that found premiums were a driving
factor in beneficiary plan selection (Jacobson et al. 2016,
Jacobson et al. 2014, Medicare Payment Advisory Commission
2015, Meyers et al. 2019, Skopec et al. 2019).

66 One study found an additional effect of favorable selection
that results from CMS’s projections. This study found that MA
enrollment increased faster in counties with overpredicted
spending between CMS’s historical baseline years and
projected years (Ryan et al. 2023).
67 The studies by Teigland and colleagues (2025a) and Pelech
and colleagues (2025) do not themselves estimate selection
effects that are constructed to reflect the effect of favorable
selection on MA payments, as MedPAC’s estimate is designed
to do. However, they include estimates of the pre-Medicare
spending and risk scores of individuals who go on to enroll
in MA and FFS, which can be used to construct selection
effects. To do so, we standardized the pre-period spending
by the risk scores and converted the risk-standardized
spending differences to payments above FFS spending. For
the nondual Medicare population aging into the program
at age 65, Teigland and colleagues’ 2015 to 2019 sample
indicated that MA enrollees had 13 percent lower riskstandardized spending than the FFS population (in the same
state with the same type of commercial insurance) before
Medicare enrollment (Teigland et al. 2025a). For Medicaid
enrollees immediately before becoming eligible for Medicare
from 2017 to 2022, the estimates reported by Pelech and
colleagues indicated that MA enrollees had 23 percent lower
risk-standardized medical spending than the FFS population
before Medicare enrollment (Pelech et al. 2025).
68 Our projection of favorable selection for 2026 is 11 percent.
Similar to the projections in our March 2025 report to the
Congress, we use the average selection effect from our two
most recent years of historical data (2022 and 2023 in our
most recent estimates).
69 We calculate the coding adjustment that is necessary to fully
account for 10 percent higher MA coding intensity as 10.3

77 Recent reporting shows that agents and brokers are often

adjustment because agents and brokers are not clinicians and

78 The general steps we followed were to identify physician and
hospital encounter records allowable for risk adjustment;
identify each record as a health risk assessment (using
procedure codes for annual wellness visits or initial
preventive physical exams, or an evaluation and management
visit provided in the home), chart review (using a chartreview indicator), or other service; map diagnoses from
those records to HCCs; apply HCC hierarchies; compare the
HCCs we identified from encounter records with the HCCs
in CMS’s risk-score file and exclude HCCs not identified in
both sources; apply HCC coefficients for the appropriate risk
model; and apply Part A and Part B payment rates specific
to each plan. We then identified the number of HCCs and
associated dollar amounts that were supported through a
health risk assessment, chart review, or both.

79 The six HCCs that each generated more than $900 million in
payments from health risk assessments and the percentage
of the time that a health risk assessment was the only
source of the HCC were vascular disease, 45 percent; major
depressive, bipolar, and paranoid disorders, 44 percent;
disorders of immunity, 64 percent; diabetes with chronic
complications, 15 percent; chronic obstructive pulmonary
disorder, 26 percent; morbid obesity, 37 percent. We note
that diabetes with chronic complications has a constrained
coefficient in the V28 risk model, meaning that differences
in the level of severity (e.g., diabetes without complications,
with chronic complications, or with acute complications) for
these conditions are not reflected in the V28 risk-adjustment
model coefficients.

92 The HHI is calculated by squaring the market share of each
entity competing in the market and summing the results. The
index approaches zero when a market is occupied by a large
number of firms of relatively equal size; the index reaches its
maximum of 10,000 points when a market is controlled by a
single firm. The index rises both as the number of firms in the
market drops and as the disparity in size among those firms
increases. Under Department of Justice and Federal Trade
Commission guidelines, markets with an index above 1,800
are considered highly concentrated (Department of Justice
and Federal Trade Commission 2023).
93 A “related party” is defined as any entity that “has a different
tax identification number than that of the MAO but is
associated with the MAO by any form of common, privately
held ownership, control, or investment, including any
arrangement in which the MAO does business with a related
party through one or more unrelated parties” (Centers for
Medicare & Medicaid Services 2023b). Because the MA bids
measure the share of MA expenses that are paid to related
parties, we assume that Part D benefits paid to related parties
(such as an insurer-owned pharmacy benefit manager) are
not included in our measure of MA vertical integration.
94 Star rating is a framework that CMS uses across MA and
FFS Medicare. On its Care Compare website, CMS publishes
star ratings for different types of Medicare providers
(like physicians, hospitals, nursing homes, and others) so
that beneficiaries can see how providers perform for FFS
beneficiaries in their local area. However, there is no single
quality evaluation for FFS Medicare in its entirety; star
ratings of providers in FFS Medicare reflect their individual
performance. The performance of a set of providers in a local
area is not directly comparable with an MA star rating, which
reflects the joint performance of an MAO and its network of
contracted providers, at the contract level.
95 Currently, quality results for MA are reported on a contractwide basis, and those results are used to determine the star
rating for all plans under the contract’s offerings.

132 Mean reversion can occur in two directions. When an
individual’s spending is initially below their predicted
spending, mean reversion can occur if their growth in
spending exceeds their growth in risk score. Conversely,
when an individual’s spending is initially above their
predicted spending, mean reversion can occur if their growth
in spending is lower than their growth in risk score.
133 We bound the selection percentages of the proxy group
to match the upper and lower bound of the selection
percentage within the MA population. We apply a linear
trend of the proxy group to the MA population. In very
rare instances, when we apply a linear trend, the selection
percentage estimate in base year t = 0 will have a more than 5
percentage point difference between the proxy group and the
MA subcohorts. When this occurs, we apply a multiplicative
trend to the MA population where the base-year selection
percentage in year t = 0 is multiplied by the growth (as a
factor) in selection percentage of the proxy group.
134 The weights represent each individual’s share of the total
predicted spending in the proxy group. Thus, the individual

135 Each cohort of MA enrollees and their proxy cohort of FFS
beneficiaries were placed into 110 mutually exclusive groups
and matched on those groups. Beneficiaries were first placed
into one of three mortality groups based on the year of death:
year t = T, t = T + 1, and t > T + 1. Decedents (death in t = T) and
near-decedents (death in t = T + 1) were assigned to 1 of 10
categories within their respective mortality group based on
initial selection percentage in year t = 0. Nondecedents (death
in t > T + 1) were placed into 1 of 3 dual status categories (fully
dual eligible, partially dual eligible, and nondually eligible),
and each nondecedent in a dual status category was placed
into 1 of 30 categories based on initial selection percentage
in year t = 0. Categories of initial selection percentage
were developed based on the distribution of spending and
enrollment, with the goal of having meaningful differences
between the categories while sustaining a reliable sample of
enrollees within each category.
136 Although there is an insufficient number of observations to
match MA enrollees to proxy groups by county of residence,
we required beneficiaries in the proxy group to reside in
counties with at least one MA enrollee in MA cohort c.
Conversely, we also required that MA enrollees reside in
counties with at least one proxy-group beneficiary.
137 Dual-eligibility status is based on a hierarchy of Medicaid
eligibility in the target year. Beneficiaries with at least
one month of fully dual status are assigned as “full duals.”
Beneficiaries without any months of fully dual status and

at least one month of partially dual status are assigned
as “partial duals.” All other beneficiaries are assigned as
“nonduals.”
138 Beneficiaries in the proxy group are also required to be
enrolled in FFS in the year before the base year (2016 in this
example) in order to have a populated HCC risk score for
2017.
139 The weights are constructed to reflect each individual’s
predicted spending in year t = T as a share of the mortalitycohort group’s predicted spending in year t = T:

where mi,T indicates months of enrollment.
140 Due to data limitations, beneficiaries who switched from FFS
to MA before 2006 are considered to have no FFS enrollment.
141 We compared our 2023 MA study population with the general
non-ESRD population by dual status. Full duals comprised 19
percent of our study population and 17 percent of the general
MA population. Partial duals comprised 9 percent of our
study population and 8 percent of the general MA population.
Nonduals comprised 72 percent of our study population and
75 percent of the general MA population.
142 Because we rely on a beneficiary-level annual spending file,
we cannot remove nonhospice spending during hospice
months from our calculations. We do not anticipate that this
materially affects our results. After removing the weighting
of hospice months from each beneficiary’s annual spending
calculation, we did not observe any difference in our overall
estimate of favorable selection.

143 Including risk-score level or spending level resulted in
each cohort of MA enrollees and their proxy cohort of
FFS beneficiaries being placed into 380 mutually exclusive
groups and matched on those groups. Including MA marketpenetration level resulted into beneficiaries being placed into
210 mutually exclusive groups.
144 The Commission recognizes that social risk factors are
associated with differences in health status and health care
use and that switching between MA and FFS may lead to
changes in a beneficiary’s use of health services and in their
Medicaid eligibility. Limited evidence suggests that MA plans
do not provide greater access to services overall relative to
FFS (Agarwal et al. 2022, Commonwealth Fund 2021, Fuglesten
Biniek et al. 2021). In addition, MA plans have a financial
incentive to ensure that beneficiaries who are eligible but
not enrolled in Medicaid receive those benefits while in
MA. These additional Medicaid benefits would potentially
reduce the cost-sharing liability for both beneficiaries
and MA plans. Further, enrolling beneficiaries in Medicaid
who were Medicaid eligible but previously unenrolled also
increases beneficiary risk scores because their predicted
costs in MA would be under a Medicaid segment in CMS’s risk
model (rather than a non-Medicaid segment). To the extent
that such beneficiaries would have remained unenrolled in
Medicaid if still in FFS Medicare, this phenomenon would
increase favorable selection in MA. Given our reliance on
a beneficiary’s FFS experience when estimating favorable
selection, we cannot account for these additional changes in
a beneficiary’s social risk while enrolled in MA.
